Foreword

That sounds like a Layer 8 issue.

If you've spent any meaningful time in cybersecurity, you've heard some version of this OSI model joke. I've been hearing it for over two decades. It's the knowing laugh shared between engineers when an incident, mistake, or malfunction traces back not to a misconfigured firewall or an unpatched vulnerability, but to a person, a click, a shortcut, or a moment of fatigue. The punchline has always been the same: the human is the problem.

In the beginning, I laughed along. But somewhere in those two decades, the joke stopped being funny, not because it wasn't true, but because it perpetuated a culture that was not conducive to security.

In 2019, I started a podcast with a close friend called Hacker Valley Studio. What most people don't know is that we almost called it Layer 8. We chose that working title because we recognized something that this industry has been slow to fully embrace: while uninformed human action is one of the greatest vulnerabilities in any security program, human beings are also the greatest asset. The people behind the keyboards, the analysts triaging alerts at 3 a.m., the security leaders making impossible decisions with imperfect information, they aren't weaknesses or a joke. They are the answer to our plight.

That conviction is exactly why this book matters so deeply to me.

Nikki Robinson is one of those rare leaders in cybersecurity who communicates not through hype or headline, but through her work and her sustained contribution to this community. She doesn't seek the spotlight; she builds the stage that others stand on. Alongside her co-author Calvin Nobles, she has produced something that goes far beyond the usual cybersecurity awareness literature, the well-meaning but ultimately squishy books that tell us to foster a security culture without ever showing us how.

What Nikki and Calvin have done here is different. This is a true methodology. A rigorous, field-tested framework for understanding how human beings actually think, decide, and fail under pressure, and for designing systems that work with that reality instead of against it. They draw from Human Factors Engineering, a discipline born in the crucible of aviation safety during World War II, and they apply it to cybersecurity with the kind of precision and operational empathy that only comes from people who have lived this work. That surely speaks to the Marine Veteran in me. The 3 U's framework—User, Usage, Usability—is deceptively simple and profoundly actionable. The adaptation of the HFACS model to cyber incidents reframes how we investigate failures, pushing us past the lazy conclusion of user error and into the systemic conditions that made the error inevitable. The honest examination of SOC analyst burnout, cognitive overload, and alert fatigue is a mirror held up to every security operations center in the world.

I am an optimistic person by nature. Anyone who knows me will tell you that. But there is one soapbox I stand on that carries a warning tone, and I will not step down from it: we are in the calm before the storm.

We are standing at the dawn of autonomous attacks. Artificial intelligence has become a tool that scales human innovation and motivation in extraordinary ways, but it scales attacks in kind. The adversary no longer needs a team of skilled operators to find and exploit your weaknesses. They need an agent. And soon, they will have countless agents probing for targets of opportunity at a speed and scale that no purely technical defense can match.

This is the reality we are walking into. And it demands that we not only get our own houses in order and strengthen the foundations of our cybersecurity programs, but that we learn to leverage AI in that fight. Most importantly, and this is the part I need you to hear, we must learn to leverage human nature itself. Our intuition. Our creativity. Our ability to adapt, to collaborate, to recognize when something simply doesn't feel right. They are capabilities to be cultivated and championed.

That is what this book teaches you to do.

The best part is that it could not arrive at a more critical moment. As the threat landscape accelerates beyond what any single tool or platform can contain, the organizations that will endure are the ones that understand a fundamental truth: resilience is not about surviving a threat. It is about designing systems, cultures, and teams where people can think clearly, act decisively, and recover stronger than before. Nikki and Calvin don't just argue this point and send you off to figure out a way forward, they hand you the blueprint to use today.

If you are a CISO trying to make the case for investing in your people as seriously as you invest in your tools, this book is your evidence. If you are a SOC leader watching your analysts burn out under a flood of false positives, this book is your intervention plan. If you are an architect designing the next generation of security systems, this book will change how you think about every interface, every alert, and every workflow you build.

This is the book we need right now. I'm grateful Nikki and Calvin wrote it, and I'm honored to welcome you into its pages.

Read it. Apply it. Share it. The storm is coming, and our people are how we weather it.

Chris Cochran, Field CISO and VP of AI Security, SANS Institute.

Contributors
About the authors

 

Nikki Robinson is a Senior Technical Staff Member, an Adjunct Professor, inventor and frequent speaker in the Cybersecurity space. She holds a DSc in Cybersecurity and a PhD in Human Factors. She is an award-winning, best-selling author of two previous titles, Mind the Tech Gap and Effective Vulnerability Management. She has over 18 years' experience across IT and cybersecurity operations, holding multiple industry certifications.

I could not be more grateful for the opportunity to write this book; a culmination of my experience and research career all living in one book. First and always, to my daughters, who have cheered me on every step of the way, thank you. I am so proud of you both and honored to be your mother. Remember, anything is possible with hard work. Now go brush your teeth!

To my co-author, Calvin, thank you for your time, collaborative spirit, and brilliant insights. This book would not have been possible without you. When are we getting lunch?

To the brilliant team at Packt, thank you for your support, professionalism, and guidance along this journey. Major thank you to Rahul for reaching out to me, here's to many more fruitful collaborations. We did it!

Dr. Calvin Nobles, a native of Mount Vernon, Georgia, is the Portfolio Vice President and Dean of the School of Cybersecurity and Information Technology at the University of Maryland Global Campus (UMGC). Dr. Nobles holds four doctoral degrees; a Ph.D. in Management and Engineering Technology, a Ph.D. in Human Factors, a Ph.D. in Offensive Cyber Engineering, and Doctor of Business Administration. With more than three decades of distinguished leadership spanning cybersecurity, the financial sector, academia, and military service, Dr. Nobles is widely regarded as a visionary leader dedicated to advancing STEM education through experiential learning. A retired U.S. Navy Commander, he has served as a Cybersecurity Policy Fellow at Harvard University and a Cybersecurity Fellow at the New America Think Tank in Washington, D.C. and is recognized as one of the nation's leading researchers in human factors engineering for cybersecurity.

This book represents the culmination of sustained reflection, research, and commitment, and I am deeply grateful for the opportunity to write on human factors in cybersecurity, a topic I have championed for more than a decade. Writing a book is never a solitary endeavor, and this work has been shaped by the encouragement, insight, generosity, and unwavering support of many people along the way. I am profoundly thankful for my village, whose love, uplift, and belief in me sustained this journey in meaningful ways. I am especially indebted to my family for their enduring love, patience, encouragement, and steadfast confidence in me throughout the writing process. Their support provided both the foundation and the motivation to continue this work across many demanding seasons. Above all, I give thanks to God for the strength, wisdom, grace, and perseverance to bring this project to completion.

I also extend my sincere appreciation to my colleagues, mentors, friends, and professional peers whose insights, feedback, and scholarly engagement helped sharpen the ideas presented in this volume. Their willingness to challenge my thinking and offer thoughtful perspectives significantly strengthened the quality of this work. I am equally grateful to the students, practitioners, and leaders whose experiences continue to inform my understanding of the field and reinforce the importance of this scholarship. Finally, I offer special thanks to Dr. Nikki Robinson, as well as the publisher and editorial team, for their guidance, professionalism, encouragement, and commitment to bringing this project to fruition.

About the reviewer

 

Josiah Dykstra, Ph.D., is a cybersecurity researcher, consultant, and author with more than 20 years of experience in cybersecurity, human factors, and applied research. He is the owner of Designer Security and serves as a Principal Cyber/AI Research Portfolio Lead at a leading advanced research and development organization. Previously, he spent 19 years at the National Security Agency and held a strategy role at Trail of Bits. Dr. Dykstra holds a Ph.D. in computer science from UMBC, has authored more than 50 peer-reviewed papers and three books, and has been recognized with the Presidential Early Career Award for Scientists and Engineers (PECASE).

Preface

Cybersecurity is not only a technical problem; it is a human one.

Every alert that appears in a security operations center must be interpreted by a person. Even with AI coming to the forefront to analyze and summarize alerts, a human must remain in the loop for cybersecurity alerts and incidents. Missing something could spell disaster for any organization. Every vulnerability must be prioritized by someone making a judgment call. Every incident response requires teams of humans communicating under pressure. Security tools may generate the signals, but people make the decisions. When those systems are poorly designed for human use, the results are predictable: overload, missed signals, cognitive bias, fatigue, and ultimately failure.

For decades, industries such as aviation, healthcare, nuclear power, and industrial safety have studied how people interact with complex systems. They have learned that performance is not simply about individual skill or effort. Instead, it is shaped by environment, workflow, interface design, cognitive limits, organizational culture, and decision-making under uncertainty. When systems are designed with human capabilities and limitations in mind, safety and performance improve dramatically.

Cybersecurity is only just beginning to fully adopt these lessons.

In many organizations, analysts are expected to process thousands of alerts per day while navigating multiple tools with inconsistent interfaces. Detection engineers must translate threat intelligence into meaningful detections while balancing false positives and operational cost. Vulnerability management teams face overwhelming volumes of findings without clear prioritization frameworks. Incident responders must coordinate across teams in high-stress situations where time and clarity are critical.

These are not purely technical challenges. They are human factors challenges.

This book explores how principles from human factors engineering can be applied to cybersecurity operations. Rather than focusing solely on technology, it examines the interaction between people, tools, processes, and the technical solutions they rely on to perform their job functions. It looks at how cognitive load affects analysts, how interface design influences decision-making, how bias shapes investigation outcomes, and how motivation and organizational culture influence security performance.

Who this book is for

This book is written for cybersecurity professionals who work at the intersection of technology and human decision-making. It is intended for security operations analysts, detection engineers, incident responders, vulnerability management teams, security leaders, and anyone responsible for designing or operating cybersecurity programs. It will also be valuable to researchers, human factors practitioners, and technology leaders interested in understanding how cognitive science, behavioral insights, and system design influence cybersecurity outcomes.

What this book covers

Chapter 1, The Human Element and Cybersecurity, explores the historical, conceptual, and practical aspects of human factors engineering (HFE) in cybersecurity.

Chapter 2, Current Challenges in Cybersecurity, focuses on the current challenges for cyber professionals, including modern technology and complexities, security fatigue, mental workloads, and the technological impact on burnout.

Chapter 3, Rethinking Incident Response through the lens of Human Factors, covers the basics of incident response from a human factors perspective, not focusing solely on the tools and techniques that an IR analyst may use.

Chapter 4, Human Factors in Security Operations, covers modern challenges that any Security Operations or SOC team may face, including overloaded dashboards, complex tooling, and alert fatigue.

Chapter 5, Human Factors in Vulnerability Management, explains how adding human factors in vulnerability management will lead to better remediation practices, faster time to remediation, ultimately focusing on the vulnerabilities that matter, and reducing the extra noise.

Chapter 6, Human Factors Engineering-as-a-Service, explains how Human Factors Engineering as a Service can help organizations reduce risk more effectively by intentionally designing security systems to align with real human capabilities and limitations.

Chapter 7, Addressing Stress, Fatigue, and Burnout in Cybersecurity, covers the pathway to burnout, cybersecurity environments and unique stressors, theoretical frameworks, and burnout mitigation strategies.

Chapter 8, Cyberpsychology in Cybersecurity, studies the relationship between human behavior, cognition, and digital settings, which have become known as cyberpsychology.

Chapter 9, Benefits of Human Factors Analysis and Classification System in Cybersecurity, provides a comprehensive framework review for applying HFACS to cybersecurity operations, including its definition, scope, origin, and theoretical foundations.

Chapter 10, Identification and Technology Strategies of Human Factors in Cybersecurity, explains to cybersecurity leaders and practitioners how to identify human factors in cybersecurity, integrate technology strategies for HFE, and provide a strategic roadmap for HFE.

Chapter 11, Implementation Path for Human Factors in Cybersecurity, bridges theory and execution by transforming the psychological, cultural, and technical principles discussed throughout the book into a structured roadmap for cybersecurity leaders, managers, and practitioners.

To get the most out of this book

The concepts discussed in this book are most valuable when they are applied to real operational environments. As you read, consider how each idea relates to the systems, workflows, and teams within your own organization.

Throughout cybersecurity operations, individuals are constantly making decisions under pressure. Alerts must be triaged quickly, vulnerabilities must be prioritized, and incidents must be investigated with incomplete information. The goal of this book is to help you better understand how human cognition, system design, and organizational structures influence those decisions.

As you move through the chapters, reflect on questions such as the following:

Where do analysts in your environment experience overload?
Which tools create friction instead of clarity?
Where might cognitive bias influence investigation outcomes?
Which processes depend on heroic effort rather than well-designed systems?

Readers in different roles may find certain chapters particularly relevant to their work.

Security operations analysts may focus on topics such as alert fatigue, attention management, and decision-making under uncertainty.
Detection engineers may benefit from discussions around alert design, signal clarity, and automation.
Vulnerability management teams may find value in chapters related to prioritization, motivation, and reducing decision bias.
Security leaders and program architects may use the material to rethink how systems, workflows, and incentives are designed across their organizations.

Many of the ideas in this book are best explored collaboratively. Consider discussing these concepts with your team, using them to evaluate existing processes, or identifying opportunities to experiment with small improvements. Human factors improvements do not always require large technological investments; often, they begin with a better understanding of how people interact with the systems we build.

===== FILE 4/19: Preface.xhtml =====

Preface

Cybersecurity is not only a technical problem; it is a human one.

Every alert that appears in a security operations center must be interpreted by a person. Even with AI coming to the forefront to analyze and summarize alerts, a human must remain in the loop for cybersecurity alerts and incidents. Missing something could spell disaster for any organization. Every vulnerability must be prioritized by someone making a judgment call. Every incident response requires teams of humans communicating under pressure. Security tools may generate the signals, but people make the decisions. When those systems are poorly designed for human use, the results are predictable: overload, missed signals, cognitive bias, fatigue, and ultimately failure.

For decades, industries such as aviation, healthcare, nuclear power, and industrial safety have studied how people interact with complex systems. They have learned that performance is not simply about individual skill or effort. Instead, it is shaped by environment, workflow, interface design, cognitive limits, organizational culture, and decision-making under uncertainty. When systems are designed with human capabilities and limitations in mind, safety and performance improve dramatically.

Cybersecurity is only just beginning to fully adopt these lessons.

In many organizations, analysts are expected to process thousands of alerts per day while navigating multiple tools with inconsistent interfaces. Detection engineers must translate threat intelligence into meaningful detections while balancing false positives and operational cost. Vulnerability management teams face overwhelming volumes of findings without clear prioritization frameworks. Incident responders must coordinate across teams in high-stress situations where time and clarity are critical.

These are not purely technical challenges. They are human factors challenges.

This book explores how principles from human factors engineering can be applied to cybersecurity operations. Rather than focusing solely on technology, it examines the interaction between people, tools, processes, and the technical solutions they rely on to perform their job functions. It looks at how cognitive load affects analysts, how interface design influences decision-making, how bias shapes investigation outcomes, and how motivation and organizational culture influence security performance.

Who this book is for

This book is written for cybersecurity professionals who work at the intersection of technology and human decision-making. It is intended for security operations analysts, detection engineers, incident responders, vulnerability management teams, security leaders, and anyone responsible for designing or operating cybersecurity programs. It will also be valuable to researchers, human factors practitioners, and technology leaders interested in understanding how cognitive science, behavioral insights, and system design influence cybersecurity outcomes.

What this book covers

Chapter 1, The Human Element and Cybersecurity, explores the historical, conceptual, and practical aspects of human factors engineering (HFE) in cybersecurity.

Chapter 2, Current Challenges in Cybersecurity, focuses on the current challenges for cyber professionals, including modern technology and complexities, security fatigue, mental workloads, and the technological impact on burnout.

Chapter 3, Rethinking Incident Response through the lens of Human Factors, covers the basics of incident response from a human factors perspective, not focusing solely on the tools and techniques that an IR analyst may use.

Chapter 4, Human Factors in Security Operations, covers modern challenges that any Security Operations or SOC team may face, including overloaded dashboards, complex tooling, and alert fatigue.

Chapter 5, Human Factors in Vulnerability Management, explains how adding human factors in vulnerability management will lead to better remediation practices, faster time to remediation, ultimately focusing on the vulnerabilities that matter, and reducing the extra noise.

Chapter 6, Human Factors Engineering-as-a-Service, explains how Human Factors Engineering as a Service can help organizations reduce risk more effectively by intentionally designing security systems to align with real human capabilities and limitations.

Chapter 7, Addressing Stress, Fatigue, and Burnout in Cybersecurity, covers the pathway to burnout, cybersecurity environments and unique stressors, theoretical frameworks, and burnout mitigation strategies.

Chapter 8, Cyberpsychology in Cybersecurity, studies the relationship between human behavior, cognition, and digital settings, which have become known as cyberpsychology.

Chapter 9, Benefits of Human Factors Analysis and Classification System in Cybersecurity, provides a comprehensive framework review for applying HFACS to cybersecurity operations, including its definition, scope, origin, and theoretical foundations.

Chapter 10, Identification and Technology Strategies of Human Factors in Cybersecurity, explains to cybersecurity leaders and practitioners how to identify human factors in cybersecurity, integrate technology strategies for HFE, and provide a strategic roadmap for HFE.

Chapter 11, Implementation Path for Human Factors in Cybersecurity, bridges theory and execution by transforming the psychological, cultural, and technical principles discussed throughout the book into a structured roadmap for cybersecurity leaders, managers, and practitioners.

To get the most out of this book

The concepts discussed in this book are most valuable when they are applied to real operational environments. As you read, consider how each idea relates to the systems, workflows, and teams within your own organization.

Throughout cybersecurity operations, individuals are constantly making decisions under pressure. Alerts must be triaged quickly, vulnerabilities must be prioritized, and incidents must be investigated with incomplete information. The goal of this book is to help you better understand how human cognition, system design, and organizational structures influence those decisions.

As you move through the chapters, reflect on questions such as the following:

Where do analysts in your environment experience overload?
Which tools create friction instead of clarity?
Where might cognitive bias influence investigation outcomes?
Which processes depend on heroic effort rather than well-designed systems?

Readers in different roles may find certain chapters particularly relevant to their work.

Security operations analysts may focus on topics such as alert fatigue, attention management, and decision-making under uncertainty.
Detection engineers may benefit from discussions around alert design, signal clarity, and automation.
Vulnerability management teams may find value in chapters related to prioritization, motivation, and reducing decision bias.
Security leaders and program architects may use the material to rethink how systems, workflows, and incentives are designed across their organizations.

Many of the ideas in this book are best explored collaboratively. Consider discussing these concepts with your team, using them to evaluate existing processes, or identifying opportunities to experiment with small improvements. Human factors improvements do not always require large technological investments; often, they begin with a better understanding of how people interact with the systems we build.

===== FILE 5/19: Chapter_1.xhtml =====

1
The Human Element and Cybersecurity

Cybersecurity fails not because technology is weak but because people make mistakes. One click on a phishing email, one reused password, or one ignored warning can open the door to catastrophic breaches. Malicious threat actors know this, and they exploit it relentlessly. The truth is clear: human behavior is the greatest vulnerability in any security system. Ignoring human factors is like locking every door while leaving the windows wide open. The solution is not more complexity; it is smarter design. By understanding how people think and act, we can create security that feels natural, training that sticks, and policies that people actually follow. Organizations that invest in human factors do more than reduce risk. They build a culture where every employee becomes a defender, not a liability. In today's threat landscape, technology alone cannot win. The strongest cybersecurity strategy is one that turns human weakness into human strength. The real question is not whether you can afford to do this but whether you can afford the cost of not doing it.

Despite decades of technical advancement, the most persistent vulnerability in cybersecurity is not technological; it is human. The 2025 Verizon Data Breach Investigations Report found that nearly 60% of security breaches stem from human error, a figure that has remained consistent over the years (Verizon, 2025). Further reinforcing this trend, Proofpoint's 2024 Voice of the CISO reported that 74% of Chief Information Security Officers (CISOs) identified human error as the most pressing cybersecurity risk, up from 60% in 2023 and 56% in 2022. A report by Proofpoint (2024) highlights that 80% of CISOs highlighted broader human challenges and friction, including negligence, distraction, and unintentional actions, as among the most significant threats to organizational security over the next two years.

One of the most consequential cybersecurity risks to organizations is human error. Human factors engineering (HFE) offers a scientific and disciplined means to mitigate a substantial portion of this risk by designing socio-technical systems that explicitly account for human limitations and predictable vulnerabilities in perception, attention, memory, and decision-making. Rather than presuming perfect vigilance, flawless recall, or consistent policy compliance, HFE aligns security technologies, interfaces, and workflows with how people actually process information, allocate attention, make judgments under uncertainty, and execute tasks under time pressure, interruptions, and cognitive load.

When security controls are engineered to reduce unnecessary complexity, clarify cues and feedback, and fit user roles and contexts, the probability of common failures decreases, including misconfigurations, unsafe workarounds, and inadvertent disclosure. In this framing, human error is not treated primarily as an individual shortcoming to be corrected through admonition or training. Human errors are indirectly targeted and reduced through intentional design choices that anticipate normal performance variability and build resilience into the system. For example, confirmation prompts and "undo" functionality for destructive actions (such as deleting a document or revoking access) introduce a deliberate pause and recovery pathway, thereby preventing a routine slip from becoming an irreversible error.

Securing the human element in cybersecurity is a complex challenge, and progress is frequently impeded by nonscientific approaches and superficial solutions that mask the true science of HFE. This includes slogans and blame narratives, one-size-fits-all awareness campaigns, compliance checklists disconnected from daily work, and security controls that add friction without improving usability. Science and evidence-based methodologies must remain at the forefront because they translate into practical, testable mitigations grounded in how people actually work, decide, and perform under real constraints. Advancing human-centered cybersecurity through HFE, therefore, depends on reducing reliance on these surface-level interventions and collaborating on solutions that fit real roles, real workflows, and real decision-making contexts. In this effort, human factors engineers and human systems engineers, working in concert with psychologists, cognitive scientists, neuroscientists, and behavioral scientists, are essential to shaping and designing cybersecurity systems that anticipate human limitations, reduce unnecessary burden, and enable secure behavior by design.

This chapter explores the historical, conceptual, and practical aspects of HFE in cybersecurity. In this chapter, human factors refers to the scientific study of how people perceive, decide, and perform within socio-technical systems, while HFE is the applied engineering discipline that translates that science into requirements, designs, and evaluations that shape safer and more effective human-system interaction. Human-centered design (HCD) is a complementary approach that applies these insights through iterative, user-informed design and testing so that technologies and workflows align with actual user needs, contexts, and constraints. Drawing on psychology, cognitive science, and systems engineering, HFE provides a rigorous, evidence-based methodology for understanding and strengthening human-system interaction. HCD supplies a practical process for implementing those findings in usable, resilient solutions. By reframing the human element from a liability into a design opportunity, cybersecurity can evolve toward more usable, resilient, and adaptive systems. This chapter introduces the human element in cybersecurity, defines key concepts, and presents practical principles; it then applies lessons from aviation, articulates the strategic value of HFE, advances the rationale for human-centered cybersecurity, addresses the role of organizational culture, and concludes with exploring how human factors engineering can be integrated into organizational culture through example scenarios. This is aimed at enhancing cybersecurity resilience through HFE-informed HCD.

In this chapter, you will learn about the following topics:

Understanding the human element in cybersecurity
Exploring the historical origins of human factors engineering
Defining human factors in cybersecurity
Applying human factors lessons from aviation to cybersecurity
Using human factors to achieve human-centered cybersecurity
Integrating organizational culture with human factors engineering
Synthesizing HFE as a strategy for cybersecurity resilience

Understanding the human element in cybersecurity

Cybersecurity breaches are not just technical failures; they are human ones. Sophisticated malware and zero-day exploits may make headlines, but many successful attacks still hinge on exploiting predictable user behavior. Social engineering, phishing, and capitalizing on poor security hygiene remain among the most frequently used methods of exploiting weaknesses. As long as organizations continue to pursue technological solutions with little regard for the human element, these practices will yield the same ineffective results. For example, an employee who is rushing between meetings may approve a multifactor authentication prompt without noticing that it was triggered by an attacker, or a staff member may bypass a secure file-sharing process because it is slower than emailing an attachment. Social engineering remains especially effective when an email appears to come from a senior leader requesting an urgent wire transfer, or when a convincing "IT support" message pressures a user to reset a password through a counterfeit portal. Even routine security hygiene failures often emerge from design and workflow friction, such as complex password rules that encourage reuse, or unclear access request processes that lead teams to share accounts "temporarily" and never revisit the risk. As long as organizations continue to deploy technological controls without accounting for the human element, these same patterns will persist, and security investments will continue to yield inconsistent, avoidable outcomes.

Numerous studies support this reality. The Chartered Institute of Ergonomics and Human Factors (2022) concluded that nearly 90% of breaches are attributable to human error. These findings underscore the need for a shift in cybersecurity strategy, from fortifying technological perimeters to designing systems that drive the human behavior and performance that organizations need. A key element is designing for the desired human behavior and performance, which reduces organizational risk. Other socio-technical fields, such as aviation, healthcare, nuclear power, and space operations, demonstrate that designing and accounting for human weaknesses and limitations leads to optimal performance and lower risk.

Adversaries increasingly exploit predictable psychological triggers, including urgency, fear, curiosity, authority, and social obligation, because these cues reliably compress deliberation and increase the likelihood of rapid, unverified action. In practice, attacks are often engineered to create a narrow decision window: a "payment overdue" notice designed to provoke anxiety, a "security alert" intended to trigger immediate compliance, or an "executive request" crafted to leverage hierarchy and perceived accountability. Under these conditions, the user is not simply making a choice; they are making a choice while distracted, time-constrained, and cognitively loaded. This is why the user interface becomes a frontline control surface. The interface is where critical decisions are made, including whether to trust an email, approve a multifactor authentication prompt, download an attachment, grant application permissions, or share access to a file. If those interactions are ambiguous, noisy, or poorly timed, the system effectively transfers risk to the user at the precise moment when adversaries are attempting to manipulate attention and decision-making.

Adversaries increasingly target psychological triggers, such as urgency, fear, or curiosity, to gain access to secure systems. In this context, the user interface becomes the new frontline. Without integrated HFE, even the most advanced cybersecurity tools can become operationally fragile because poor usability, excessive friction, unclear cues, and unmanageable alert volume lead to predictable behaviors such as habituation, workarounds, and erroneous confirmations (Ncubukezi, 2022). For example, frequent noncritical warnings condition users to click through prompts without reading them, and complex access procedures can incentivize shortcuts such as password sharing or the use of unsanctioned tools to complete work. HFE addresses these failure modes by aligning security controls with real workflows, reducing cognitive burden at decision points, designing warnings and confirmations that are specific and actionable, and ensuring that secure choices are the easiest choices to execute correctly.

Exploring the historical origins of human factors engineering

HFE emerged as a formal discipline during World War II in response to unacceptable rates of aviation accidents. Seminal work by Fitts and Jones (1947) demonstrated that many "pilot error" events were better explained as design-induced failures: cockpit layouts, controls, and displays that conflicted with human perceptual limits, attention constraints, and decision-making demands. That evidence catalyzed a durable shift in safety-critical domains away from a deficit model of "fixing the human" and toward a systems model of "fixing the system." As the field matured across cognitive psychology, ergonomics, physiology, and systems engineering, HFE established a practical premise: predictable human limitations are not aberrations to be trained away but design constraints to be engineered around (Chapanis, 1999 Rogers & McGlynn, 2018).

This history matters for cybersecurity because modern security failures mirror the same attribution error that aviation learned to correct. Cyber incidents are frequently described as "user mistakes" (clicking a link, reusing a password, or approving a prompt), and many organizational responses still prioritize admonition, awareness campaigns, and compliance policing. The aviation lesson is that persistent "human error" signals a design problem in the broader system, not merely a training gap in the operator. In cybersecurity, the equivalents of poorly designed cockpits include ambiguous authentication prompts, overly complex access workflows, noisy alerts in security operations centers, confusing administrative consoles, and policies that conflict with how work is actually executed.

Early HFE research provides direct guidance for human-centered cybersecurity design and decision-making today: conduct task and workflow analysis before adding controls; minimize cognitive load at decision points; design warnings and confirmations to be specific, timely, and actionable; build forcing functions and recovery pathways (for example, phishing-resistant authentication, contextual MFA, and reversible destructive actions); evaluate controls in realistic simulations that replicate time pressure, interruptions, and competing priorities. Framed in this way, the historical overview is not a standalone narrative. It establishes the chapter's central argument that cybersecurity resilience increases when security is treated as a socio-technical systems problem and engineered to be robust to normal human performance variability rather than dependent on idealized human behavior.

Unfortunately, in cybersecurity, HFE has not yet been recognized as a distinct engineering discipline. The constant, repeated episodes of human behavior as a central causal factor in cybersecurity incidents are telling. Many organizations are building enormous cybersecurity ecosystems with little regard for human vulnerabilities and limitations.

Defining human factors in cybersecurity

Human factors, also known as ergonomics, is the scientific discipline that examines the interactions between people and other elements within a system. Its purpose is to use evidence from research and real-world observation to shape design decisions so that systems fit human capabilities and limitations, rather than forcing people to adapt to poorly designed processes. In practice, human factors reduce avoidable complexity, anticipate predictable mistakes, and support dependable performance when people are under time pressure, interrupted, or managing high workloads. By systematically analyzing and evaluating how work is actually done, human factors strengthen safety, protection, and comfort while also enhancing system outcomes such as efficiency, effectiveness, and operational resilience.

Cybersecurity professionals are often expected to design systems that work for people, but many have never been formally introduced to the science that helps achieve this goal. There is a widespread misunderstanding of what "human factors" really means. In both industry and academic circles, the term is frequently lumped in with general user behavior, missing its true identity as a well-established scientific discipline focused on optimizing how humans interact with systems.

A recent review of over 100 academic articles that use the term "human factors" in their titles found that fewer than 10% defined it in line with widely accepted standards (Nobles & Burrell, 2024). This inconsistency creates more than a scholarly oversight. It produces practical confusion with real consequences. When the field does not clearly define human factors as a scientific discipline and professional practice grounded in evidence-based methods, organizations are more likely to reduce it to generic "user behavior" or to awareness training, making it far harder to incorporate human factors into cybersecurity strategy in a rigorous and effective manner.

Understanding human factors in the right light means seeing it as a design-driven approach. It is about shaping systems that support how people think, behave, and make decisions, particularly in high-pressure environments such as cybersecurity. Instead of focusing on fixing user errors after the fact, HFE is about designing technology that reduces the likelihood of those errors in the first place. It is a shift from a reactive to a proactive approach, and from blaming the user to building more innovative and resilient systems that help account for human limitations and weaknesses.

Think of a cybersecurity plan that not only addresses problems when they occur but also anticipates them and designs systems that work in harmony with people, rather than against them. Integrating human factors into cybersecurity represents a paradigm shift in fundamental beliefs, realigning the human element as the central aspect in cybersecurity. It demonstrates a shift in attitude, moving away from blaming users and intentionally designing for the behaviors and performance desired.

Cybersecurity has long focused on what individuals do wrong, such as clicking on suspicious links, disobeying rules, and making poor choices under stress. That point of view is not entirely erroneous, but it is incomplete and often misleading. Organizations significantly influence individual behavior; thus, inadequate consideration of both unintentional and deliberate actions imposes responsibility on the organization itself. It presents a limited view of human behavior as a problem rather than a benefit. The real opportunity lies in understanding how people use systems and then optimizing those systems to work better for them by accounting for people's strengths and weaknesses.

This is where the field of HFE comes into play. This field is not just about making fewer mistakes; it is about getting the best results. It asks, "How can we make technology and settings that help people do well, even when things are stressful and dangerous?" When used in cybersecurity, the results could be powerful and innovative, transforming traditional cybersecurity practices and defense mechanisms.

Why not create security solutions that are easy to use, responsive, and help people make informed decisions, rather than requiring repeated education to navigate complicated, inflexible systems? Why not address the core causes of those behaviors—overload, misunderstanding, and burnout—rather than punishing people who fail to follow the rules? Unfortunately, this proactive strategy has not gained widespread adoption in the cybersecurity community. One significant reason is that people often do not fully understand what "human factors" entail. In many companies, the term still refers to mistakes made by users or breaking the rules. That narrow description misses the field's bigger design-driven possibilities. It is like attempting to improve flying by simply examining what pilots do wrong, rather than considering how the cockpit is designed, how communication works, or how air traffic control operates.

There is also a lack of knowledge. Most cybersecurity teams lack knowledge of psychology, cognitive science, and ergonomics, all of which are essential components of human factors. Because of this, technical security solutions come first, and people come second. This imbalance creates instruments that are safe in theory but annoying in practice, which ultimately weakens the defenses they were intended to reinforce.

Organizations need a more precise and comprehensive understanding of human factors to address this issue. This new definition should focus on system design, human performance, and ensuring that people's behavior aligns with security goals. With that foundation, cybersecurity teams can utilize HCD to create systems that are not only safer but also easier to use, more intuitive, and more resilient.

This change extends beyond the classroom. The effects in the real world are tremendous. Systems that fail to account for how people think and work can lead to problems, including cognitive overload, security fatigue, poor automation utilization, and decision paralysis. Organizations can start to solve these problems with insight and purpose by viewing human factors as a solution space, rather than merely a list of problems.

Cybersecurity is not just a concern with technology. It is not easy to manage in a digitally and technologically advanced society. To fix it, you need more than firewalls and protocols. You need to understand how people work under pressure and have great respect for them. When businesses adopt this point of view, they do not merely lower risk. They create systems and cultures that are inherently safe.

Identifying and utilizing human factors principles

This section outlines several human factors principles that provide a framework for designing systems that account for the human element and human performance. It is important to note that, in HFE, a system comprises a group of people, processes, procedures, tools, resources, and environments. In cybersecurity, the system could be the end-to-end security ecosystem that surrounds a business function, including the users who perform the work, the security policies and procedures they are expected to follow, the identity and access management tools they interact with, the devices and applications they use daily, the security operations workflows that monitor and respond to threats, the training and support resources available to them, and the organizational and physical environments in which all of these activities occur.

Table 1.1 outlines essential principles applicable to cybersecurity. It does not encompass all possible human factors principles; however, it lists some critical factors. The human factors principles listed here are indicative of proven practices developed by other socio-technical domains:

Principle

	

Description

	

Reference




User-centered design

	

Involve users throughout the design process to align systems with their needs and limitations

	

Norman (2013)




Minimizing cognitive load

	

Simplify tasks and organize information to reduce mental strain

	

Wickens et al. (2004)




Consistency and predictability

	

Ensure system behavior is intuitive and consistent

	

Wickens et al. (2004)




Accessibility and inclusivity

	

Design for diverse populations, including those with disabilities

	

Sanders & McCormick (1993)




Error prevention and recovery

	

Use intuitive controls and provide clear error recovery pathways

	

Shneiderman & Plaisant (2010)




Simplicity and clarity

	

Avoid unnecessary complexity in the interface and workflow

	

Norman (2013)




Feedback

	

Provide immediate, understandable responses to user actions

	

Sanders & McCormick (1993)




Psychosocial considerations

	

Account for job demands, autonomy, and well-being

	

Neumann et al. (2021)

Table 1.1: Human factors principles (Nobles, 2025)

Again, these principles are not exhaustive but offer a foundation for integrating HFE into cybersecurity systems and policies. Let us look at them in some detail:

User-centered design: User-centered design requires involving intended users throughout requirements definition, prototyping, testing, and iteration so that security controls fit real tasks, constraints, and decision points rather than idealized behavior (Norman, 2013). In cybersecurity, user-centered design means observing how employees authenticate, share files, handle exceptions, and respond to alerts, then shaping workflows so secure actions are natural, efficient, and sustainable. User-centered design also treats "workarounds" as diagnostic signals that the system is misaligned with the realities of work, and it uses that insight to redesign the control rather than blaming the user.
Minimizing cognitive load: Minimizing cognitive load simplifies tasks and structures information so users can make accurate decisions under time pressure, interruptions, and competing demands (Wickens et al., 2004). In cybersecurity, minimizing cognitive load means reducing the number of steps to complete secure actions, presenting only the information needed at the moment of choice, and eliminating unnecessary mental translation, such as forcing users to interpret ambiguous warnings or memorize complex rules. Minimizing cognitive load also supports faster and more reliable incident response by prioritizing alerts with actionable context, which reduces fatigue-driven errors and missed signals.
Consistency and predictability: Consistency and predictability ensure that system behavior, terminology, and interaction patterns remain stable across tools and situations so that users can form accurate mental models and execute actions with confidence (Wickens et al., 2004). In cybersecurity, consistency and predictability reduce errors that arise when different systems use different labels for the same concept, present the same risk with different visual cues, or require different steps for equivalent tasks. Consistency and predictability also enhance operational coordination in security teams by standardizing how alerts, severity ratings, and response actions are presented, thereby decreasing handoff friction and decision delay.
Accessibility and inclusivity: Accessibility and inclusivity ensure that security technologies and workflows support diverse users, including those with disabilities and varying levels of experience, so that secure behavior is achievable for the entire workforce (Sanders & McCormick, 1993). In cybersecurity, accessibility and inclusivity require interfaces that work with assistive technologies, authentication experiences that do not rely on a single sensory modality, and communications that are readable and unambiguous. Accessibility and inclusivity reduce insecure workarounds that emerge when legitimate users cannot effectively use the approved security pathway, which strengthens both compliance and resilience.
Error prevention and recovery: Error prevention and recovery anticipate predictable slips and mistakes and build safeguards and recovery pathways so that routine human error does not become irreversible harm (Shneiderman & Plaisant, 2010). In cybersecurity, error prevention and recovery include confirmations for destructive actions, context-rich prompts that reduce accidental approvals, and "undo" mechanisms such as reversible permission changes and rapid account recovery procedures. Error prevention and recovery also include designing workflows that make it difficult to commit high-impact errors silently, while making it straightforward to detect, correct, and learn from mistakes when they occur.
Simplicity and clarity: Simplicity and clarity remove unnecessary complexity from interfaces and workflows so that users can understand what is happening, what action is required, and why it matters (Norman, 2013). In cybersecurity, simplicity and clarity mean plainly described security actions, streamlined access requests, and a visual hierarchy that highlights the most important cues at decision points, such as distinguishing legitimate login prompts from suspicious ones. Simplicity and clarity increase the likelihood that users will follow secure processes because the secure path is understandable, quick to execute, and less error-prone than improvisation.
Feedback: Feedback provides immediate, understandable responses to user actions so that users can confirm outcomes, detect issues early, and adjust behavior appropriately (Sanders & McCormick, 1993). In cybersecurity, feedback includes clear confirmation messages after password changes, access approvals, or file-sharing actions, as well as alerts that explain what happened, what risk is present, and what the user should do next. Feedback strengthens learning and trust because users can see the consequence of an action and verify that the system recorded it correctly, which is especially important during incident response when uncertainty can drive hesitation or incorrect action.
Psychosocial considerations: Psychosocial considerations account for job demands, autonomy, stress, and well-being because human performance and decision quality are shaped by workload, organizational climate, and sustained pressure (Neumann et al., 2021). In cybersecurity, psychosocial considerations include designing security operations center (SOC) operations to reduce chronic alert overload; clarifying roles and escalation pathways; and ensuring adequate recovery time, staffing, and psychological safety for reporting near misses. Psychosocial considerations reduce burnout-driven errors and turnover-related fragility by making secure performance sustainable, which strengthens the reliability and continuity of cybersecurity operations over time.

Together, these principles provide a practical foundation for integrating HFE into cybersecurity systems and policies by treating human limitations as design inputs, aligning controls with real work, and engineering environments in which secure behavior is the default and error is containable rather than catastrophic.

Applying human factors lessons from aviation to cybersecurity

Aviation offers a mature, evidence-driven model for managing risk in complex socio-technical systems, and cybersecurity can directly adopt many of its human factors practices rather than inventing new approaches in isolation. Both domains rely on tightly coupled technologies, standardized procedures, and human operators who must make rapid decisions under uncertainty, time pressure, and information overload. Aviation's safety gains were not achieved by demanding perfect performance from pilots and maintainers; they were achieved by redesigning systems, training teams for predictable breakdowns, and building learning mechanisms that treat human error as a signal of system vulnerability. Cybersecurity can use the same orientation by shifting from a primary focus on "fixing users" through awareness campaigns toward "fixing the system" through usable security design, workload-aware operations, and engineered recovery pathways. The practical implication is that security controls, interfaces, and workflows should be treated as safety-critical design artifacts that must be tested, standardized, and continuously refined around real human capabilities and limitations.

The first transferable practice is aviation's emphasis on team-based performance and coordination through crew resource management (CRM) and single-pilot resource management (SRM). CRM and SRM formalize behaviors that reduce errors, including structured communication, shared situational awareness, task prioritization, cross-checking, and disciplined decision-making. In cybersecurity operations, the analog is the security operations center and incident response ecosystem, where analysts, engineers, and business stakeholders must coordinate quickly with incomplete information. Applying CRM/SRM concepts in cybersecurity means establishing standard briefings and handoffs, using shared mental models for incident severity and risk, implementing explicit "challenge-and-verify" norms before high-impact actions (for example, firewall changes or account lockouts), and designing escalation protocols that reduce ambiguity during crises. These practices transform incident response from an ad hoc, personality-driven activity into a repeatable operational discipline that reduces errors, limits rework, and improves response quality under pressure.

A second aviation practice that cybersecurity can adopt is the systematic identification of human-error precursors, exemplified by the "Dirty Dozen" framework (for example, fatigue, complacency, inadequate communication, and lack of resources). The value of such a framework lies not in the list itself but in the disciplined habit of treating performance variability as predictable and manageable. In cybersecurity, a comparable approach would be treating conditions such as alert overload, excessive context switching, unclear ownership, ambiguous interfaces, and chronic time pressure as measurable risk factors that can be engineered down. This perspective supports targeted interventions such as alert rationalization, decision-support redesign, staffing models aligned to demand, and simplified workflows that reduce error likelihood. Instead of asking why an analyst "missed" a signal, the organization examines whether the system produced too many low-value alerts, insufficient context, or conflicting procedures that made the miss predictable.

A third aviation-derived practice is the establishment of strong reporting cultures supported by safeguards that encourage honest disclosure of mistakes and near misses. Aviation's safety privilege concepts and programs, such as voluntary reporting systems, illustrate how learning accelerates when people can report hazards without automatic punitive consequences. Cybersecurity can benefit from a similar "learning-first" model by implementing protected internal reporting channels for misconfigurations, near-miss phishing events, procedural breakdowns, and control failures, paired with rapid learning loops that translate reports into design and policy changes. This approach aligns with the logic of a just culture: inadvertent errors and system-induced mistakes are treated as opportunities for system improvement, while reckless or malicious behavior remains subject to accountability. The outcome is more complete event reporting, earlier detection of latent vulnerabilities, and faster mitigation before adversaries exploit recurring patterns.

Implementing aviation-like safety privilege in cybersecurity requires careful alignment with legal and regulatory obligations, because cybersecurity incidents can trigger mandatory external reporting under sectoral rules, privacy regulations, and state breach-notification laws. The practical lesson from aviation is that protections and transparency can coexist when the boundaries are clearly defined: privileged internal learning channels can be designed to promote reporting and analysis, while separate processes handle legally required external notifications. Cybersecurity organizations can pilot hybrid models that combine confidentiality protections, anonymized or de-identified reporting where appropriate, and tiered accountability standards that distinguish human error from negligence or intentional misconduct. Over time, these pilots can generate evidence about what increases reporting, what reduces repeat errors, and what governance structures maintain trust without undermining compliance.

Overall, the aviation case demonstrates that sustainable risk reduction emerges from systems thinking, disciplined operational practices, and engineered learning mechanisms, not from exhortations to "be more careful." Cybersecurity can apply these human factors lessons by designing security controls that are usable in real working conditions, training teams in coordinated decision-making and cross-checking, measuring and reducing error precursors such as workload and ambiguity, and building non-punitive reporting cultures that convert near misses into concrete improvements. In this sense, aviation's human factors history serves as a practical blueprint: it shows how a high-stakes domain moved from blaming individuals to engineering resilient systems, and it provides a tested set of practices that can strengthen cybersecurity performance, reliability, and organizational learning.

The strategic value of human factors engineering

HFE carries strategic value in cybersecurity because it reduces organizational risk at the point where most security controls succeed or fail: human interaction with security technologies, policies, and operational workflows. Here, "strategic value" denotes quantifiable enterprise outcomes associated with risk management, including reductions in incident likelihood and incident impact, improved operational continuity, improved decision quality during high-tempo events, stronger regulatory and audit performance, and preservation of stakeholder trust. Strategic value, in this framing, also includes measurable improvements in control effectiveness, because control effectiveness depends on reliable human execution under real conditions of time pressure, interruption, workload, and ambiguity. HFE advances risk reduction by treating human limitations and predictable weaknesses as design constraints and then engineering socio-technical systems so that secure actions remain comprehensible, efficient, and resilient under normal performance variability.

Cybersecurity strategies often focus investment on technical capabilities such as encryption, endpoint protection, and threat detection, yet organizational exposure persists because human-system interaction remains underdesigned. Authentication prompts, access provisioning, configuration interfaces, alert queues, incident workflows, and policy enforcement mechanisms collectively form a risk-bearing surface where errors, workarounds, and delays become predictable. HFE connects directly to cybersecurity strategy by translating evidence about human cognition and behavior into concrete design requirements and governance decisions that reduce failure probability across the surface. Examples include reducing cognitive load at decision points, increasing the clarity and specificity of warnings and confirmations, improving signal-to-noise in security operations tooling, standardizing terminology and interaction patterns across platforms, and embedding recovery pathways that limit the blast radius when mistakes occur. Each design choice serves as risk control because it alters the probability distribution of error and the time-to-detection and time-to-recovery profiles that determine impact.

A human-centered cybersecurity strategy requires more than general system design language; a human-centered cybersecurity strategy requires an explicit integration of HFE into risk governance, operating models, and performance measurement. HFE-informed strategy links security requirements to task demands and operational constraints, aligns security policies with real workflows, and structures accountability around system design decisions rather than solely around individual compliance. A mature strategic posture, therefore, treats usability, workflow fit, and decision support as core components of security architecture, alongside technical safeguards. Under such a posture, organizational risk reduction becomes traceable to specific interventions, such as fewer misconfigurations, fewer unsafe access practices, fewer erroneous approvals, reduced alert fatigue, improved incident coordination, and improved recovery outcomes.

Using human factors to achieve human-centered cybersecurity

Technical defenses such as firewalls, encryption protocols, and access control systems anchor many cybersecurity programs, but these controls address only part of the risk. As threats grow more sophisticated and connectivity expands, organizational outcomes increasingly depend on how people interpret prompts, follow workflows, and make decisions under pressure. Human-centered cybersecurity treats the human element as a strategic lever rather than a residual vulnerability by designing security around human behavior, interaction patterns, and cognition. For example, an organization can deploy strong multifactor authentication, yet a poorly designed approval prompt can still lead employees to approve fraudulent "push" requests during busy work periods, enabling account takeover.

Cybersecurity, therefore, functions as a behavioral and organizational challenge as much as a technical one. Every control ultimately depends on human action: a manager approves access, an analyst triages alerts, a developer configures permissions, and an employee decides whether to click a link or report a suspicious message. Users bring different experiences, cultural norms, and cognitive styles, and those differences shape how users perceive risk and respond to security guidance. When organizations ignore these realities, they often create systems that users resist, misunderstand, or bypass. For example, when a secure file-sharing tool requires too many steps, teams frequently revert to emailing attachments or using unsanctioned platforms to meet deadlines, thereby increasing exposure even when the "approved" tool is available.

Grobler et al. (2024) offer a practical way to structure human-centered cybersecurity through the "3U's" framework: user, usage, and usability. The framework helps leaders and designers ask three complementary questions: who must make security decisions, how does the organization expect security to function in day-to-day work, and how easy does the system make it to behave securely? The following paragraphs explain each "U" and illustrate how each appears in real cybersecurity contexts. Refer to Figure 1.1:

Figure 1.1: The 3U's of human-centric cybersecurity (Grobler et al., 2024)

User refers to the people who interact with security controls, including employees, administrators, developers, vendors, and security operators. Users differ in role, experience, authority, stress levels, and familiarity with threats, and these differences shape how users interpret risk cues and act on instructions. For example, a finance employee who processes invoices all day can become a prime target for invoice fraud, while a new hire may not recognize subtle signs of a credential-harvesting email. Human-centered cybersecurity designs with these differences in mind by tailoring friction, guidance, and safeguards to role-specific tasks. For instance, the organization can require stronger verification steps for wire transfers or privilege escalation than for low-risk actions, and it can provide in-context prompts that teach users what to check at the moment of decision rather than relying only on annual training.

Usage describes how people actually use security controls within real workflows, including authentication, access requests, secure sharing, patching, incident reporting, and compliance activities. Usage focuses on the operational reality of security: whether controls integrate into daily work or collide with it. For example, a complex access request process can push teams to share accounts "temporarily," and unclear remote access steps can lead staff to adopt unmanaged tools for speed and convenience. Human-centered cybersecurity designs for sustainable usage by embedding security into normal routines and reducing unnecessary steps. For instance, the organization can automate device updates during low-disruption windows, integrate least-privilege access requests into a familiar ticketing workflow, and use just-in-time access with clear expiration and renewal prompts to reduce permanent overprovisioning.

Usability evaluates the user experience of security controls, including clarity, effort, feedback, and confidence. It addresses whether users can understand what the system asks them to do, complete actions efficiently, and recognize when something goes wrong. Poor usability increases error rates, habituation, and workarounds. For example, frequent non-actionable warnings train users to click through dialogs without reading them, and confusing administrative consoles increase the likelihood of misconfiguration that exposes data. Human-centered cybersecurity improves usability by reducing cognitive load, presenting actionable information, and providing clear feedback. For instance, number-matching MFA can reduce accidental approvals, phishing warnings can explain the specific risk signal detected, and configuration interfaces can include guardrails, safe defaults, and reversible changes to prevent a single mistake from becoming a major incident.

Together, user, usage, and usability provide a practical foundation for designing cybersecurity that reduces organizational risk and strengthens resilience. User considerations ensure that security aligns with role-based decision-making and real human variability. Usage considerations ensure that controls fit operational workflows and remain sustainable under productivity demands. Usability considerations ensure that interfaces and processes make secure actions understandable and straightforward, especially when time pressure and distraction increase error likelihood. When organizations integrate all three, cybersecurity shifts from a reactive function that depends on vigilance to a proactive system that guides secure behavior by design.

By applying the 3U's framework, organizations can operationalize the chapter's broader human-centered cybersecurity argument: security outcomes depend on engineered human-system interaction, not only on technical capability. This approach supports measurable organizational outcomes, including fewer policy violations and workarounds, fewer successful social engineering events, lower misconfiguration rates, faster incident response, and stronger trust in security processes. In an environment where threats evolve quickly, organizations gain a durable advantage when they design security systems that empower users to make correct decisions in the flow of work rather than expecting users to compensate for poor design.

Integrating organizational culture with human factors engineering

Security behavior is inseparable from organizational culture because culture shapes how employees perceive risk, interpret policies, respond to authority, and decide whether security requirements feel legitimate or burdensome. A human-centered cybersecurity strategy, therefore, treats culture as a risk variable, not as background context. When leaders align security expectations with how work actually happens, employees adopt secure behaviors more consistently; when leaders ignore cultural realities, employees often respond with disengagement or informal workarounds that undermine otherwise-sound technical controls.

Culture influences security outcomes through three practical mechanisms: users, usage environment, and usability. Users vary in norms around deference, collaboration, and communication, which affects how they interpret security messages and escalation guidance. The usage environment includes local workflow pressures, team expectations, and implicit "how we do things here" rules that determine whether secure steps get followed or bypassed. Usability determines whether tools and processes support secure action in the flow of work or create friction that employees circumvent. For example, during an executive-impersonation phishing campaign requesting an "urgent document review," employees in a highly hierarchical workplace may comply immediately to avoid appearing unresponsive, while employees in a consensus-driven environment may first confirm the request in a team chat; a human-centered approach addresses both contexts by implementing a required secondary verification step for high-risk requests, publishing a clear "verify-before-comply" workflow, and routing verification through the communication channels that each group actually uses.

Organizations strengthen resilience when their cultures encourage reporting, learning, and shared accountability rather than blame. Employees report suspicious messages, near misses, and process breakdowns more frequently when leaders reinforce psychological safety and treat reports as inputs for system improvement instead of occasions for punishment. A practical example is an organization that rewards early reporting of suspected phishing and uses that feedback to tune email filters and update prompts; the organization reduces repeat incidents because the culture accelerates learning, and the tooling improves based on real user experience. In this way, culture becomes a strategic asset that amplifies HFE's impact by increasing adoption, reducing resistance, and turning everyday behavior into an active component of organizational risk reduction.

Synthesizing HFE as a strategy for cybersecurity resilience

Across today's digital environment, organizations face cyber threats that continue to grow in sophistication, speed, and scale. Many programs respond by adding technical controls and expanding training, yet breaches persist because failure often occurs at the point of human interaction with those controls. HFE strengthens cybersecurity by reducing organizational risk through design. HFE aligns security tools, workflows, and communications with how people actually perceive information, allocate attention, and make decisions under time pressure. Rather than asking employees to compensate for confusing systems, HFE reduces error likelihood, improves decision quality, and builds operational resilience through usable interfaces, manageable cognitive demands, and feedback-driven improvement.

HFE advances human-centered cybersecurity through three mutually reinforcing levers. First, HFE improves user interaction by streamlining workflows, clarifying prompts, and providing immediate, actionable feedback so that the secure path is the easiest path to follow. Second, HFE reduces cognitive overload by organizing information around the decision at hand, limiting unnecessary complexity, and presenting guidance in formats that users can execute quickly, even during interruptions and high workload. Third, HFE strengthens organizational performance by shaping training and communication around real tasks and by establishing feedback loops that continuously refine controls based on frontline experience, rather than relying on static policies and one-way awareness messaging. Together, these levers shift cybersecurity from a compliance-driven model to an engineered, learning-oriented system that supports secure behavior in daily operations.

Let's consider a scenario of SOC alert overload and missed escalation. A security operations team receives thousands of daily alerts, many of which lack context and require analysts to pivot across multiple tools to determine the severity. Under this cognitive load, analysts begin triaging by habit, close "routine" alerts quickly, and miss a true-positive signal that indicates lateral movement. An HFE-informed redesign changes outcomes by reducing noise and improving decision support: the SOC consolidates alert views, standardizes severity labels, suppresses low-value alerts, and presents key context at the first point of review (asset criticality, user activity, known threat pattern, and recommended next step). The team also implements a structured handoff checklist and a clear escalation trigger so that analysts do not rely on memory or intuition when fatigue increases. The result is not simply a better interface; it is lower risk because the system reduces the probability of omission errors and shortens the time to detection when a real incident occurs.

Let's now consider another scenario, around access provisioning friction and insecure workarounds. An organization requires employees to request access through a complex approval process that takes days, uses unclear forms, and provides little status feedback. Teams under delivery pressure share credentials or grant broad access "temporarily" and forget to revoke it, creating persistent overprivileged accounts. An HFE-informed approach redesigns the workflow to fit real operational constraints: role-based access templates reduce decision complexity, requests route through a familiar ticketing interface with clear status updates, and time-bound access expires automatically unless reapproved. The system adds simple, contextual guidance that explains why a request requires additional approval and what alternatives exist. This redesign reduces workarounds by lowering friction, while also reducing organizational exposure by limiting excessive permissions and making secure behavior the most efficient option.

These scenarios illustrate the central point of this section: cybersecurity resilience improves when organizations engineer security around human performance rather than expecting perfect compliance. HFE enables that shift by improving usability where decisions occur, reducing cognitive burden where errors concentrate, and embedding communication and learning mechanisms that keep controls aligned with evolving work practices and threats. When leaders integrate HFE into their cybersecurity strategy, organizations reduce avoidable incidents, improve response reliability, and create a culture in which secure behavior becomes a supported capability rather than a constant struggle against poorly designed systems.

Summary

This chapter argued that cybersecurity's most persistent weakness is human behavior, with major industry reports indicating that many breaches arise from human error amplified by poor usability and misaligned security workflows. The chapter contended that technical controls remain necessary but insufficient unless the cybersecurity strategy incorporates human cognition, context, and real-world work practices.

The chapter positioned HFE as the evidence-based discipline for reducing these risks, drawing lessons from aviation's shift toward designing systems that anticipate human limitations. It clarified HFE's definition, introducing core principles (for example, user-centered design, reduced cognitive load, accessibility, and a just culture) and applying frameworks such as the 3U's (user, usage, and usability) to align security with actual behavior.

Finally, the chapter emphasized organizational culture as a determinant of security behavior and recommended culturally aware design, feedback loops, and participatory methods to move beyond compliance toward resilient, intuitive security ecosystems that reduce risk and strengthen trust.

The next chapter, Chapter 2, Current Challenges in Cybersecurity, will delve deeper into the modern threat landscape.
