2
Current Challenges in Cybersecurity

Cybersecurity professionals, whether analysts, engineers, or architects, are managing increasingly complex and challenging technical ecosystems, not to mention emerging and highly sophisticated threats. Cyber teams are under immense pressure to make rapid and accurate decisions to protect their organization. This pressure comes from both boards and regulators due to the risk of data breaches and full organizational compromise. As adversaries gain ground with more technology and easier-to-exploit vulnerabilities, the demand on cyber teams continues to grow.

This chapter focuses on the current challenges for cyber professionals, including modern technology and complexities, security fatigue, mental workloads, and burnout. Cybersecurity challenges are not simply technical but also have a psychological and cognitive factor. The intersection of human cognition and technical infrastructure is where the newest challenges facing cybersecurity teams will be seen.

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

Modern challenges in cybersecurity
Social engineering
Mental workloads and cognition
Insider threats deep dive
Tool sprawl and linkage to burnout
Modern challenges in cybersecurity

Before diving into exactly why it is so pertinent to integrate human factors research into cybersecurity, let us first examine the current landscape of cybersecurity. With the rise in ransomware, phishing, and targeted spear phishing campaigns to carry out credential theft, account compromise, and vulnerability exploitation, it is becoming easier to infiltrate systems.

With the use of advanced attack techniques and the inherent complexity of systems, attackers have an advantage when trying to access networks or steal credentials. In most areas, organizations implement technical and process-based controls to combat those types of attacks. But if the technical factors alone were stopping the threats, there would be fewer data breaches and successful ransomware attacks.

Updated statistics

In the 2025 Proofpoint Threat Report, we see a marked uptick in social engineering attacks via email fraud and phishing, denoting a continued increase in psychological techniques to trick users. Each year, we see a rise in the number of successful attacks on large organizations, showing the need to continue to grow our cybersecurity posture and maturity level as attackers gain sophistication.

Along the same lines, the IBM X-Force Threat Intelligence Index report from 2025 highlighted that phishing was the initial access vector in 41% of the attacks that the group studied. In the same X-Force Threat Intelligence report, business email compromise (BEC) was identified as one of the fastest-growing threats, although that type of attack has been used for many years.

BEC attacks target chief executive officers (CEOs) or other high-level executives at organizations by compromising their email accounts and monitoring them for months, or sometimes years. The individual who compromised the account will learn the patterns, behaviors, and frequency of the emails exchanged to understand the executive's language and email style. Once ready, they will send an email to the chief financial officer (CFO) requesting funds or a wire transfer to be sent to an account owned by the attacker. But this is just one example; BEC attacks can target anyone to deceive employees, customers, or vendors into transferring money or disclosing data.

Most common attack vector

Given those statistics, it is no surprise that phishing continues to be the most common attack vector for conducting a multitude of attacks. Phishing refers to email-based attacks that use a variety of techniques to encourage users to click on links, download documents, or give away their usernames and passwords.

Phishing attacks have a variety of goals, including the following:

Encouraging users to click on a malicious link that will download malware or ransomware
Providing a Word document or PDF that contains malware or ransomware, encouraging the user to open or download the document
With targeted phishing, or spear phishing, attacks, focusing on specific individuals to either request them to send money to offshore bank accounts or provide confidential data to a spoofed account
Conducting spam-bombing attacks to distract users, using known good domains and links, from noticing other types of phishing attacks

Phishing emails have become so sophisticated that it can be very difficult for users to identify that emails are from spoofed domains or contain malicious links. Scammers will use familiar-looking sender names, urgent language, or fake login pages to attempt to gain information from the user.

One of the reasons this continues to be the most common attack vector is because of the low cost and potential high reward for successful phishing campaigns. It costs very little to craft one email or smishing (text-based phishing) message and send it to thousands or tens of thousands of users.

It remains relatively easy to bypass common security controls, for example, accounts that do not use multi-factor authentication (MFA). Even with technical controls in place, such as endpoint detection and response (EDR) and firewalls, attackers can exploit the human layer of any organization. This common attack vector alone highlights the need to understand users, the tools they use, and how security impacts the business.

Most common exploits

There are numerous vulnerabilities and misconfigurations exploited regularly, and based on data from 2023 and 2024, we will continue to see similar exploit patterns. Exploitable vulnerabilities such as Log4j use remote code execution, server-side request forgeries with email servers, and privilege escalation. These types of attacks are seen again and again across a variety of vendors and products.

Other common exploitable vulnerabilities include SQL injection from external-facing systems and memory corruption, which can be implemented through phishing attacks. An example that the industry continues to use year over year is the Equifax breach from 2017, where an Apache Struts vulnerability remained unpatched and was exploited, allowing the attackers to steal over 147 million records. This breach is a cautionary tale to patch systems that have known exploited vulnerabilities and typically show up in the cybersecurity and infrastructure security agencies (CISA) known exploited vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog.

As shown in the following figure, there are multiple avenues that bad actors will use to exploit vulnerabilities, each leading to consequences such as credential theft, malware, or cloud account compromise. These threat vectors provide an example of how one may exploit vulnerabilities to gain access to data or compromise credentials:

Figure 2.1: Common attack patterns for highly exploitable vulnerabilities

For commonly exploited misconfigurations, cloud environments continue to give attackers a foot in the door, for example, open S3 buckets in an Amazon Web Services (AWS) environment, default credentials across any account or system, and overly permissive identity and access management (IAM) roles. There has also been a rise in the misconfiguration of assets being externally facing that should be internal only, as well as exposed administrative interfaces or web applications that could give access to data that should only be accessed with administrative permissions.

Most common attack patterns

One of the most common tools in our cybersecurity toolbelt is the MITRE ATT&CK framework. It provides a comprehensive view of how attackers enter networks and the common patterns they follow as they conduct a variety of different techniques. The primary attack patterns that are used give us an indication of why human factors is the missing link in preventing complex cyberattacks.

The following figure shows several different techniques used to conduct multiple attacks. For example, an insider threat could result in a number of consequences, just as credential stuffing could be used to take over an API or a user account. Each type of attack vector, as shown in the MITRE ATT&CK framework, results in numerous challenges, whether exploiting a vulnerability or choosing to steal credentials. Based on current trends, credential theft continues to be one of the main motivations for cyberattacks.

Figure 2.2: Examples of attack vectors and consequences for systems/accounts

Some of the common attack patterns described here include insider threats, credential stuffing, and supply chain attacks. Insider threats are a very well-known example of a cyber threat with a human factor, being the result of an employee with administrative access leaking business or proprietary secrets. Each of these threats has been conducted against a multitude of organizations, some being highly successful. For example, credential stuffing was used in the Zoom data breach in 2020, which leveraged previously leaked credentials, resulting in thousands of accounts being taken over.

Supply chain attacks have risen since 2020, including multiple high-profile examples such as Log4j and Kaseya VSA in 2021. In the Kaseya VSA example, the Revil group compromised remote management software that the company used, leaving over 1,000 companies susceptible to ransomware. This impacted multiple supermarket chains and caused outages worldwide, showing the linkage and impact of misconfigured software on our access to food and supplies. This impacted over 50 different Internet Service Providers (ISPs) and over 800 businesses, with some reports indicating that as many as 1,500 businesses may have been impacted by the ransomware attack.

Social engineering

Given the extensive list of exploitable vulnerabilities and misconfigurations, understanding the full attack picture is essential. As an example, if default credentials are used in a web application, it could be possible for an attacker to leverage those credentials to conduct privilege escalation and gain access to internal systems.

But the examples of credential theft and privilege escalation primarily show technical controls and vulnerabilities that are exploited; they don't address the psychological aspects of how these attacks are conducted. Social engineering is the practice of bad actors leveraging psychological techniques to understand users and the way they operate. They are commonly carried out due to the simplicity and scalability of these techniques, as well as the attacker's ability to abuse and exploit people's trust.

In this section, we will explore the use of social engineering in a number of ways and where the human factors discipline becomes important. As technology becomes inherently more complex, primarily due to the interconnectedness of systems and infrastructure, so does the need to understand people through the human factors lens. Our cloud environments and mobile devices now provide a number of entry points into organizations, and not just access points, but paths directly into business-critical systems.

The prevalence of social engineering

There are numerous forms of phishing used today, and they each have their own specific use case or form of contact:

Phishing: Mass emails that may look like they are from a social media or gaming company or a bank or credit union
Spear phishing: Targeted phishing to an individual or group of individuals at an organization, for example, a Human Resources (HR) team
Whaling: Similar to BEC, targeting high-level executives
Vishing: Phishing through voice, which is now more common with deepfake technology that can mimic individuals
Smishing: Phishing through text, either to have people click on malicious links or prompt MFA to induce MFA fatigue in users
Clone phishing: Duplicating real emails, which replace legitimate URLs with malicious ones

Phishing and targeted attacks such as whaling and spear phishing continue to be relatively easy for attackers to carry out. Phishing requires very little cost, and even targeted attacks, while they take much more time to conduct, are still low-cost. While URL spoofing techniques already exist, AI-generated visuals can make phishing pages and emails appear more legitimate, increasing the likelihood that users will trust malicious links. But AI isn't just used in image and video generation; it is now prevalent in formatting emails, leading to fake websites that appear very much like the real websites that they are spoofing. It is increasingly more difficult for humans to distinguish between AI images and true content.

Smishing – a rising tide

Smishing, a text-based form of phishing, has grown exponentially due to the widespread use of texting. SMS-based technologies also lack additional spam filters like the ones in the email services that are commonly used in organizations, leaving individuals open to text-based attacks. While mobile carriers do provide their own phishing and scam protection, most organizations need to add additional layers of defense to protect users from scams and fraud. Smishing attempts are also harder to detect while being just as simple to conduct as traditional phishing with emails.

According to Proofpoint's Threat Report from 2023, smishing and text-based attacks increased by almost 330% in that year. According to the FCC, almost 70% of mobile device users have received suspicious or malicious text in the past year. But the most common smishing scenarios leverage the same attack techniques as phishing, leaving users with another technical mechanism to steal information or credentials.

Some of the most common smishing scenarios from 2024 include package delivery scams, banking or tax scams, MFA or 2FA bypass, and employment scams. Account lockouts for streaming services and shopping sites are also a common technique used in both smishing and phishing. Attackers will pretend to be a representative from a bank or government entity, urging users to act quickly by either filling out a form with personal information or verifying login credentials on fake websites.

Vishing and why it is successful

Like smishing, vishing uses scams and notifications to attempt to steal information from users. Unlike phishing and smishing, though, vishing can be highly sophisticated and leverage several psychological and social engineering components to trick users. There are three main reasons why bad actors like to use vishing: to either steal sensitive information such as social security numbers (SSNs) or MFA codes, to transfer money, or to provide remote access to laptops or other systems.

Vishing can be highly successful due to the prevalence of hybrid work cultures, AI voice cloning, and Voice over IP (VoIP) spoofing. AI voice cloning now allows bad actors to use AI-generated deepfakes to mimic executives or someone of authority in the workplace to fool users. VoIP spoofing allows individuals to impersonate banks, government officials, and IT departments, for example, a helpdesk support technician asking for a password or credential rotation.

According to IBM's X-Force report in 2023, vishing attacks have increased by over 50%. Due to the combination of vishing and BEC-style attacks, the FBI's IC3 report from 2024 estimated that businesses lost over $2.4 billion. Because vishing and AI-based deepfakes now allow attackers to mimic executives or even loved ones, it is even more critical for defenders and users to understand psychological and engineering practices.

Examples of successful social engineering attacks

Phishing, smishing, and vishing continue to provide a source of strain for cybersecurity teams and users. There are multiple ways that technology can help prevent them, including implementing MFA, strong networking, and IAM best practices. But without proper implementation, attackers can continue to gain access to systems or exploit vulnerabilities.

Technical controls are not the only components essential to helping our cyber teams. To explore this topic in more depth, we will investigate some examples of cyber breaches where human factors engineering may have helped detect or prevent them before they caused too much damage.

Fake employees

While there are numerous recent examples of fake employees either being offered full-time positions at companies or pretending to be employees, there are three notes that highlight the sophistication of these attacks.

To start, there is the example of AI voice-cloning calls against a UK energy firm in 2023, where the CEO's voice was cloned using publicly available recordings from speeches and other engagements. The voice that the attacker used was able to convince a financial director to send over 200,000 pounds to a foreign bank. Using AI voice cloning is the modern equivalent of the traditional BEC attacks using emails.

Another example of a successful social engineering attack is the use of IT helpdesk calls that stole MFA codes from users during the MGM Resort breach in 2023. The attackers pretended to be IT staff while on calls with employees and HR, claiming that there was an account issue. The attackers then either installed remote access tools or requested that the MFA codes sent to phones be read off by the unsuspecting employees. The attackers were able to convince users to reset MFA, which allowed them entry to systems to conduct a ransomware attack.

There are multiple companies that have reported fake job candidates who used deepfake videos during interviews or some type of voice manipulation. In 2022, the FBI issued a statement alerting individuals to the way that attackers are applying to remote IT jobs. Through publicly available data, the attackers seem most interested in roles where elevated permissions would be required, such as developer or administrative positions.

In some of these attacks, common technical controls such as MFA were in place, but they were easily bypassed by attackers using psychological and social engineering techniques. But designing systems that minimize reliance on trust-based communication could be a positive step to combat these types of attacks. This is where human factors engineering principles can help us to design more effective systems.

Missing technical controls and misconfigurations

While multiple examples of missing technical controls have been mentioned, there is one in particular that highlights how easily technical aspects can be bypassed. The cyberattack against Microsoft Power Apps in 2021 used a combination of discovering open cloud storage, pulling sensitive or confidential data, and potentially selling or ransoming the data.

During this breach, there was a default misconfiguration in an application that exposed over 38 million records. This was a common application used by governments, healthcare organizations, and private companies. There was over 1,000 applications created using Power Apps that ultimately exposed the data across multiple sectors.

The availability of this application gave users access to privileged accounts to create applications that could result in misconfigurations and introduce data security risks. Because of this, a large amount of data was left unsecured and available for the taking. Even with the implementation of a common application, users were still able to introduce misconfigurations, which ultimately led to significant data loss.

Mental workloads and cognition

There is an eternal struggle between IT and cybersecurity teams, explored in the book Mind the Tech Gap: Addressing the Conflicts between IT and Security Teams by Nikki Robinson (2021), around usability versus security. Technical and security teams can be combative or disagree on technical controls or implementation based on user requirements, business needs, or even security initiatives.

In a classical sense, organizations feel as though they will sacrifice usability for their user population if they implement security enhancements. But this simply is not true. Adding security at the right layer will enable product usability and design, without adding burden to the users. In this section, we will cover the common areas where products and applications struggle to identify the benefits of security over usability and functionality.

Cognitive bias in systems design

Cognitive bias, the first human factors concern in designing systems, is how specific applications or tools favor information that confirms beliefs from the user. One example is when users have settings enabled for their history and other preferences, a search engine will rank content higher that aligns with their preferences. For example, a user may see an article that suggests that there are fewer ransomware attacks these days, so they may not consider it a threat. There are also AI-based diagnostic tools that rely very heavily on historical data, which may reinforce stereotypes or misinformation.

Another case of cognitive bias is availability heuristics, which is the tendency to overestimate the importance of specific or recent events over factual information. For instance, a threat detection system might overreact, or isolate systems based on the most recent type of data breach, versus up-to-date threat intelligence information. A system may also use a type of feedback mechanism to elevate or focus on the opinions from users with the highest comment or interaction rate.

Anchoring bias, a parallel notion in cognitive bias, is relying too much on the first piece of information received, versus using additional resources or research to back up findings. In a system designed for shopping experiences, a pricing tool might rely on the first quote, which may add bias to additional or subsequent results. A more specific example is in a hiring application, the first applicant's scores might alter or skew the results of other candidates.

A particularly interesting case, when it comes to the increase in automation for manual or repetitive tasks, called automation bias, is the ability for decisions or AI outputs to be overly trusted by users. For example, doctors use AI diagnostic tools without researching them more heavily or validating the findings with additional resources. A more cybersecurity-centric example is Security Operations Center (SOC) analysts who accept a finding stating that no threat was detected and don't carry out any additional investigation to validate those findings.

Perception and cognition

It is critical to cover the high-level concepts before understanding the impact across the cybersecurity space. The discipline of human factors introduces both psychological and engineering practices into cybersecurity, offering an understanding of humans in combination with the technology we consume.

SOC analysts and security engineers monitor large volumes of data, including logs, dashboards, and alerts, from multiple consoles. To determine a true positive versus a false positive, analysts depend on visual perception to direct their attention, cognitive load to understand how much data can be processed at once, and pattern recognition to find anomalies in detections. A SOC analyst requires high-risk indicators with perceptual clues, heuristic reduction to understand why an alert is important, and recognition over recall, which reduces the risk of focusing on the history and memorization of tasks.

Cybersecurity teams are under a lot of pressure to make quick, high-impact decisions, such as isolating a machine or rotating someone's credentials automatically. Because cyber analysts, engineers, and architects are only humans, they are also potentially influenced by cognitive biases, stress, incomplete information, or time constraints to make the best decision for risky situations.

A final note on perception and cognition, though they will be explored in more depth in future chapters: security professionals typically manage multiple applications and consoles, adapt to constantly changing industry trends, and use custom scripts or automation to manage their workloads. This provides a potential avenue for misconfigurations in systems, missed updates, or mishandling of credentials, secrets, or API keys, leading to account compromise and privilege escalation.

Poor interfaces

When the user interface (UI) or user experience (UX) is poorly designed across applications, it can lead to poor user decisions, which could impact risk management activities in the organization. UI is about the look, feel, and layout of an application; this includes the buttons, colors, and icons that a user will interact with. UX refers to the overall journey and emotion a user has while interacting with it, which is more about how easy, efficient, and satisfying it is to interact with said application. Similar to cognitive bias, a decision can be affected by how the choices are presented in an application.

Specific to application design, a user may see the wording "confirm deletion" for files and select it, choosing to remove their files without understanding the impact. But if a system shows "You will remove your work forever if you delete from the recycle bin," it will alert the user to the serious implications of removing those files forever. It provides the user with a warning and the consequences of taking an action, versus confirmation of an action.

Password and MFA policies

Most individuals maintain dozens or hundreds of passwords for work, entertainment, banking applications, shopping, and healthcare. Because we live in an app-centric world, users must create logins, passwords, and some sort of MFA implementation across each application. This creates several security challenges:

Passwords are reused in personal and corporate applications, leaving business accounts susceptible to account compromises
The use of password lockers or vaults to maintain numerous accounts and logins mean leaving users' most valuable credentials in a third-party application, which could be breached
Users will look for workarounds that compromise security because of the cognitive overload of trying to remember the various password strengths and complexities across multiple applications

Platforms will have unique and sometimes conflicting password requirements. Some applications will require a symbol, while others will not let you enter the password if a symbol exists. Other systems will require a forced password reset after 30 days, while others will request a token challenge through an authenticator application or an SMS token for the MFA component.

Another complexity for users is that password advice continues to change year to year. For years, users were told to change passwords often, use complex combinations, not reuse passwords, and not write any passwords down. But based on the most recent password requirements from NIST, users shouldn't have to reset passwords unless there is evidence of compromise and passphrases are better than a complex string of symbols.

Unfortunately, humans are relatively predictable when it comes to password logic. Users commonly swap letters with numbers, for example, using a "3" instead of an "e," or use memorable numbers, such as the current year or their birth year, at the end of a password. Since most of these patterns are expected, attackers will build scripts and automation to predict passwords and use them in brute-force or credential-stuffing attacks.

Lack of human-centered design

Just as with password management, a lack of human-centered design can cause users to compromise security in favor of "ease of use." Because of the multitude of MFA solutions present today across applications, including SMS, email validation, authentication apps, biometrics, and hardware tokens, users are forced to learn and maintain a variety of options. For example, some applications may only work with one provider's authenticator app, while another only works with a competitor's, meaning that for only two submissions, the user must use two different authenticator applications.

Along with the multitude of MFA solutions, users must also deal with the various states and maturity levels of the authentication options. Some MFA systems will time out quickly (within 30 seconds), while others have poor accessibility or require application switching for copy and paste options. Several mobile devices have altered their OS-based controls to allow automatic copying of SMS-based codes for application submission.

Another example of poor design is non-intuitive security dashboards for administrators that lack clear prioritization of alerts or notifications. It is also possible for critical alerts to be missed in the console without context or understanding of their impact on the environment. Administrators can suffer from alert fatigue, leading to them disabling monitoring or tuning out alerts because of constant interruptions and noise.

A final example of making security overcomplicated in applications is when security prompts use language that is confusing for users. Prompts are typically used to help users understand why an action may be taken by an application, an interaction the user must decide to conduct, or maybe an organization-wide policy that applies to all users. If the language is vague or confusing, a user may, with all good intentions, select an option that they believe is secure but in actuality is not.

The following are two examples of prompts that could confuse users:

"Your session may have been compromised. Would you like to reset your password or generate a new token?"
"Please choose an option: 1. Regenerate Key 2. Delete Session 3. Cancel"

This is an example of a prompt without context of what each action will do

Users might not understand the threat, leaving them to choose the option that sounds least impactful, potentially opening them up to security risk if their account is compromised. Ideally, the user's credentials should be automatically rotated, forcing a reset rather than relying on the user to choose the appropriate action.

Insider threats deep dive

Insider threats continue to grow in sophistication, as demonstrated by the technique of a fake candidate applying for a job at an organization so that they can acquire insider information. Other examples are a disgruntled employee or someone who was fired or laid off, who has access to confidential information that could harm the organization if leaked. Insiders could also have access to business-critical systems that they could take down or disrupt.

Malicious versus negligent

Common mechanisms for insiders to act in a company include unintentional, negligent, and malicious behaviors. For the purpose of implementing human factors principles into cybersecurity, we will focus on negligent and malicious behavior. Negligent behavior refers to a user bypassing common security controls due to poor UX, a misunderstanding of policies, or even frustration with the constant security measures that impact their efficiency. Malicious behavior refers more to an individual motivated to steal data to gain something in return.

Some examples of negligent behavior related to attempting to bypass security measures are sharing logons, using personal devices for work because enterprise applications are too slow to support workloads, or copying files offline because access to tooling is not available. Each of these violations could have a serious impact on the organization, from data exfiltration to account compromise, even if the user is simply trying to perform their tasks.

Either of these behaviors can be influenced by internal or external factors. Negligent actions could be the result of constantly changing security policies, overly restrictive administrative environments that require multiple logins and slow access management processes, or even the pressure to succeed from executives or customers. Malicious behavior could also be derived from numerous sources, including extortion, bribery, beliefs that are misaligned with the organization, or even social pressures or experiences.

Behavioral warnings

Behavioral analysis can be a useful tool in understanding users and possible insider threats. Insider threat behavior can be determined by identifying user access to sensitive or unusual information, logins at hours outside of their normal scope, or downloads of an extraordinary number of files in a short amount of time.

Outside of the technical warnings for insider threat behavior, it is important to notice when individuals feel mistreated or disengaged from their work activities. It is possible they feel a lack of trust or unclear motivation for their work, leading to stress and frustration. An employee who feels frustrated may not act out in a malicious fashion; they could simply be looking for an easier way to conduct their work that meets deadlines and expectations from leadership.

Organizational influence on cybersecurity

A lot of research has been carried out over many years that focuses on the organizational culture around cybersecurity and the benefits of a positive security culture. Having shared values and beliefs encourages individuals to align with security policies, understand and report risks, and prioritize security over risky behaviors. Security does not have to be a blocker; it can be an enabler for the business and have a positive impact on systems and users when implemented using human factors ideals.

Security policies are critical to any organization; they provide guidance and focus on what risks are acceptable versus what is unacceptable. However, overly strict, confusing, or irrelevant policies could lead users to skirt policies or disregard them altogether. Human-centered policies address the cognitive load and context of complex tasking, which aligns with how their employees perform their individual job functions.

There is a common misconception in the cybersecurity and technical community that human factors in cybersecurity mean security awareness training. While that does become a subset of the discipline, the focus is more on psychology and how we can build better tools and applications for humans. Providing feedback loops for users can positively impact behavior reinforcement; humans learn better when feedback is available for positive or corrective actions. An example is a user who is reporting a phishing email but getting no response, never learning whether that phishing email was in fact malicious or whether it was a routine communication.

Tool sprawl and the linkage to burnout

The term shadow IT has been around for some years, and with the advent of AI systems, we now see shadow AI as part of our terminology and understanding of where unknown systems and applications may exist in the technological landscape. For example, an administrator may need to work on a development server before pushing to production. They may deem it unnecessary to include inventory systems or install EDR on the server, because it is ephemeral and only holds development data. This server may inadvertently expose vulnerabilities or misconfigurations externally, leaving the capability for a bad actor to move from the development system into production.

System and API sprawl

System sprawl continues to evolve with multi-cloud, hybrid cloud, including combinations of on-premises data centers and cloud or containerized applications. Businesses have adapted their technology stacks for scalability, resiliency, and speed for development to production. This means the interconnectivity between systems continues to grow more and more complex, allowing multiple avenues for entry by bad actors.

API sprawl is seen due to the ease of use and accessibility of APIs for developers and administrators, which streamlines access to data. Unfortunately, unlike servers and containers, APIs do not always have proper authentication mechanisms in place, may lack logging or monitoring, and may inadvertently allow enhanced privileges. There may also be limited insight and oversight into API connections, because they are not traditionally tracked in an inventory system.

With technology sprawl, there are a number of human factors challenges that start with the interconnectivity of systems. Administrators may experience task fragmentation due to switching between systems, as well as tool fatigue with the need to look at multiple log sources to track API usage. If systems come online quickly, teams may miss documentation or ownership, including role-based access control (RBAC), which could protect those API endpoints.

System and API sprawl are not solely technical debt with negative implications; they are cognitive issues. Each tool, endpoint, key, credential, and secret opens an avenue for vulnerabilities and misconfigurations to occur. The more tools, systems, applications, and infrastructure that we maintain, the more time we spend making decisions on the security of that technology.

DevOps and leaking secrets

DevOps and DevSecOps have both been used in large development teams, aiming to move away from the traditional waterfall style of waiting until the end of development activities to implement testing and security. DevSecOps introduces security practices into DevOps pipelines, but depending on the nature of the implementation, it could be a small team that is now responsible for development, operations, and security activities.

While this movement has encouraged development teams to implement security on the frontend of product development, security could still be pushed aside in favor of speed. Designing guardrails that help to encourage security by default could be seen as a detriment to speed, although more commonly, continuous integration/continuous deployment (CI/CD) pipelines automate security practices within testing and scanning processes.

Automation aids in implementing security in development pipelines; for example, static application security testing (SAST) and dynamic application security testing (DAST) can automate security analysis and simulate attacks while the application is running. But without proper documentation or understanding of what automation is occurring, it could produce quite a bit of noise for developers, who may in turn tune out those alerts.

Summary

Cybersecurity is no longer defined by solely technical components such as firewalls, EDR solutions, and encryption protocols. It is much more than that; it is about the mental workloads, the cognitive implications, as well as the burnout and stress that affect cybersecurity effectiveness. Technical debt, API sprawl, misconfigurations, poor UI/UX design, and many more aspects are just the start of the cybersecurity challenges. What is most clear, after evaluating the technical aspects of cybersecurity attacks and impact, is that the human factor is more important than ever.

As organizations continue to grow in both technology and complexity, the solutions designed for the users who rely on them must evolve as well. Cybersecurity attacks, threat vectors, and an increased threat landscape require more than technical solutions; they require an understanding of the humans who use said technology. Without expanding our knowledge and reach into the way we build, manage, and design systems and applications for our users, we will continue to see ransomware, phishing, insider threats, and technical debt.

In the next chapter, the focus shifts to a specific group within cybersecurity teams, the SOC. With an abundant amount of research and data around how security analysts experience stress, burnout, and fatigue, it is an area rich to explore how cognition and perception impact analysts within the SOC. Because the SOC is the first line of defense for any organization, it is crucial to understand the cognitive load, interface design implications, and team dynamics that, whether positively or negatively, impact performance.

References

IBM X-Force 2025 Threat Intelligence Index | IBM. https://www.ibm.com/thought-leadership/institute-business-value/report/2025-threat-intelligence-index
Business Email Compromise: The $55 Billion Scam | Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3), https://www.ic3.gov/PSA/2024/PSA240911
OWASP API Security Project | OWASP Foundation, https://owasp.org/www-project-api-security/
