3
Rethinking Incident Response through the Lens of Human Factors

Incident Response (IR) is the area within cybersecurity where IR analysts respond to potential security threats or act in real time to a cyberattack. IR is the act of protecting data, systems, and operations by detecting, containing, eradicating, and recovering from security incidents. IR is like firefighting; there is always a new incident, alert, or investigation that requires quick decision-making, fast action, and efficient resolution. Even with advancements in technology, several IR responsibilities and actions are manual or require human intervention. An IR analyst will have a wide range of skills and abilities, including carrying out malware analysis, leveraging multiple investigative tools, and understanding how and when to respond to a cyberattack.

This chapter will cover the basics of IR from a human factors perspective, rather than focusing solely on the tools and techniques that an IR analyst may use. A data breach or cyberattack is a technical event, but our analysts and responders are human. We will first dive into the individual components of IR, providing an introduction for each area of human factors.

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

The incident response life cycle
Limits of perception and cognition
Human factors intervention
Building learning and system resilience
A case (or two) for human factors
The incident response life cycle

There are standard frameworks for IR. NIST has defined an IR life cycle, which serves as an important reference for building an IR team and process. IR is not a singular technical activity or process; instead, it is a set of complex decisions and forensic investigations that must occur to determine the severity, breadth, and overall impact of a cyberattack. In the following figure, you can see the comprehensive steps in a modern IR program, spanning from preparation all the way back to iterating processes and improving workflows for the next incident:

Figure 3.1: IR life cycle

The first step, preparation, includes defining roles and responsibilities, along with the appropriate communication channels, in the event that an incident occurs. The second step, detection and analysis, is where IR teams, in conjunction with the security operations center (SOC), Threat Intelligence (TI), and Detection Engineering (DE) teams, identify potential threats and prioritize them based on relevance and severity.

The NIST guidelines group containment, eradication, and recovery together as one step, where cyber teams will actively reduce damage, remove malware, and bring systems back online if they are impacted by a cyberattack. The last step, post-incident activity, is the feedback loop that carries After-Action Reports (AARs) back into the preparation step, constantly honing and evolving IR processes and procedures.

Each of these steps blends perfectly into the next; however, in typical IR processes, the focus is on the technology or processes. There are many mentions of communications plans, but not always the information that lends itself to IR teams adding to or complementing their programs with human factors and psychological information. For example, consider the containment step. The goal is to stop an incident by whatever technical means necessary. But these guides don't typically talk about the pressure on the team during this step; what if teams accidentally isolate the wrong asset, leading to business or revenue impact? Another example is, during the recovery step, what types of mental workloads do teams need to balance to ensure systems are operational quickly, with enhanced security, and to ensure the incident never occurs again? There will likely be multiple mental workloads, with decisions that impact the business and the security of the organization, as well as ensuring teams interact optimally for a mutually beneficial outcome.

Without understanding human factors, IR guidance could potentially lead teams to miss improving practices or detecting gaps in tooling or even fail to identify where the problems lie in their own programs. Crafting a powerful problem statement is the best way to identify the real issues or concerns, rather than focusing solely on the technological aspects.

Problem statements help teams understand (in the simplest context) what is going wrong, possible symptoms or indicators, the impact or consequences, as well as the audience of the issue. A clear and concise problem statement can be the difference between successfully solving a problem and choosing a solution before understanding where the real issues are. If teams and leaders continue to believe that the problem is technology-driven, instead of looking at the people who manage that technology, their problem statements will continue to miss the mark.

During incidents, analysts operate under extreme cognitive load, increasing the likelihood of missed indicators and decision errors. Poorly designed security tools and interfaces create friction that slows investigations and contributes to fatigue. Communication failures between responders, leadership, and supporting teams further complicate coordination when remediation is critical. IR playbooks that do not account for real human behavior often fail under stress, fatigue, and multitasking. Over time, these pressures lead to burnout and high turnover, weakening organizational response capabilities and leaving systems more vulnerable to attack.

Preparation, policies, and roles

In every phase of the IR life cycle, there are policies and processes that must be followed. For example, if supporting or working for the federal government, there may be policies that state that an incident must be reported within a two-hour window. Or an organization may have internal processes that outline a robust reporting and communication chain leading to the executives, based on the severity of the incident.

During IR procedure planning, it is essential to define who will act during an incident. Typically, this may include establishing an IR leader, a communication coordinator, and, depending on the scope of the incident, an individual responsible for client communication. These roles are essential to ensuring proper messaging between teams, establishing a process for resolving an incident quickly, and limiting the "blast radius" of a cyberattack.

This type of coordination requires quick decision-making, highly effective communication, and technical expertise for a variety of incident use cases. Throughout the IR life cycle, human factors could provide a mature and enhanced response to any type of incident. Without a solid engineering and psychological framework, incidents could lead to finger-pointing, blame, frustration, and stress across teams.

Detection and analysis

The second step, detection and analysis, requires coordination across several technical tools and teams. IR teams may have automation in place to monitor logs, intrusion detection systems (IDSs), firewalls, networking devices, and endpoint detection and response (EDR). During this step, analysts will triage alerts for malware, unauthorized access to systems, or phishing with malicious links to determine Indicators of Compromise (IOCs).

But with so many tools, techniques, processes, configurations, and alerts to monitor and update, alert fatigue could set in for SOC analysts and incident responders. Alert fatigue can lead to missed alerts, false positives or false negatives, or delayed processing in detecting an incident. So, human factors comprehension becomes an integral part of setting up a proper detection and analysis workflow.

Depending on the size of the organization, tools may be fragmented, making the decision-making process for responders and analysts more complex. For example, a larger security organization will use multiple EDR solutions, network monitoring applications, and dashboards displaying alerts from multiple sources. Each of these panels may provide disparate information that an analyst needs to parse and understand quickly. Decisions for incident responders must be quick; they are typically acting after the alert of a cyber incident.

With information overload and cognitive workloads, analysts may struggle with effective triage and focusing on what matters most. For example, an incident responder may be handling multiple incidents at one time that include different system owners, alternating customer requirements, and possible legal or global restrictions on how to respond to that incident. Juggling all this information can be challenging to ensure that each incident is responded to within a specific timeline and without feeling the additional pressure of missing a containment action, leading to a larger breach.

IR requires pattern recognition to identify true positives, but with multiple layers of bias, both in the tooling and in humans, errors in detection and analysis will occur. While bias can exist from the responder's perspective, it can also exist in the tooling, for example, if it determines that an incident is a false positive versus a true positive. Many IR tools rank alerts using predefined scoring systems. If those scoring models are poorly tuned, responders may focus on high-scoring alerts while ignoring lower-scoring ones that are actually critical.

Containment, eradication, and recovery

As the third step in the NIST IR life cycle, containment, eradication, and recovery are compiled together into one component. This step is focused on limiting the blast radius and stopping the bleeding. IR teams isolate affected systems and work to contain the damage by shutting down ports, blocking malicious domains, and removing malware or other harmful files to restore system integrity. They also collaborate closely with system and application owners to ensure that systems are brought back online in a clean, secure, and fully operational state.

Incident responders will use a variety of tools to isolate hosts, remove unauthorized access to systems, rotate credentials, and ultimately monitor systems at a heightened state to ensure there is no reinfection. One example is ransomware, where ransomware reinfection rates can be high, due to its ability to hide in integral system components. For environments affected by ransomware, watching for possible malware or IOCs of bad actors attempting to gain access is a critical step.

But during these activities, responders and analysts must make critical decisions under time pressure and maybe even uncertainty around the type of attack. It can take precious time to understand the impact, and without higher levels of approval, there could be doubt when executing actions. There is a need for fast and effective communication from the security team to system owners, the legal team, and leadership. Any miscommunication could lead to delays in action and possible worsening of the impact across the environment.

The value of after-action reports

Once the dust has settled from an incident, threats have been eradicated, and systems are stable, it is common for IR teams to run an AAR to identify what worked, what did not, and how to change processes and playbooks for more effective and faster responses. Teams may choose to update IR plans and communications paths, share findings with leadership or even externally (where appropriate), and tune detections or notifications.

But if incidents are poorly handled, teams may resort to a blame-focused AAR instead of a constructive feedback-oriented approach. AAR should not focus on individual faults or blame teams for missing security controls but should leverage a systems thinking and engineering approach instead. Also, teams should leverage institutional knowledge to document, transfer knowledge, and ultimately learn from the situation. A culture focused on education and improvement will foster open and honest communication, which could help with detecting and preventing similar instances in the future.

Playbooks and action

Most IR teams operate with a set of playbooks that either automate processes and communication or provide a list of instructions to follow in order, depending on the situation. Security Orchestration, Automation, and Response (SOAR) may be used to automate processes that are recorded in playbooks. An example is auto isolating a server if malware is detected.

Playbooks include many factors that come from human experience or IR best practices, including the following:

Decision points – what, where, when, and why
Roles and responsibilities – who and what
Communication processes – who, when, and what
Tools and techniques – what and when
Containment and recovery protocols – dependent on the situation

IR playbooks must be designed with human usability in mind, not just technical completeness or dashboard metrics. During an active incident, responders are often operating under extreme time pressure, cognitive overload, and stress, which significantly affects their ability to process complex instructions. Playbooks that are overly dense, poorly organized, or filled with jargon can slow decision-making and increase the likelihood of errors. Instead, effective playbooks should prioritize clear structure, concise steps, visual hierarchy, and easily accessible decision points, so responders can quickly understand what actions to take next.

Although the playbooks are created using tools and automation, without a human factors engineering approach, they may be overly complex, lack appropriate timing, or lead to the wrong action being taken during an event. Another form of bias, action bias, refers to the human tendency to favor taking immediate action rather than pausing to analyze a situation, even when gathering more information before deciding would be optimal. Incident response playbooks are intended to counteract this tendency by providing structured guidance that encourages verification and analysis before response actions are taken. However, if playbooks contain unclear instructions, oversimplified workflows, or are not informed by real-world operational experience, they may inadvertently reinforce action bias by pushing responders toward immediate actions without adequate context or analysis. Any automation used by an IR team should provide visual coordination tools, shared views, and a common language for all engaged teams, while also supporting external cognitive scaffolding by allowing systems to track information and automate routine decisions rather than relying on human memory.

The dangers of tools and techniques

As mentioned in all steps of the IR life cycle, responders may be using a multitude of tools to determine the cause, impact, and IOCs. Figure 3.2 references the various tools and data sources that an IR team may need to utilize during an incident:

Figure 3.2: Tools required by an IR team

IR teams rely on a multitude of tools to provide support before, during, and after an incident. Responders must review all aspects of telemetry to determine the impact, exposure, and criticality of the incident. Tools and automation in IR are not only technical in nature but can also incorporate human factors principles that improve IR tooling by reducing confusion, response delays, and errors.

As shown in the preceding figure, IR teams use tools for detection, monitoring, network analysis, TI, forensics, and endpoint management. But so many tools will potentially provide too much data, which can make it difficult to prioritize data sources or correlate information across multiple systems.

Overwhelming interfaces make it difficult for responders to focus, leading to a reduction in situational awareness. Our attention spans are minimal, with some recent studies suggesting that our attention spans are only eight seconds when interacting with online content (https://sambarecovery.com/rehab-blog/average-human-attention-span-statistics). This means that every new dashboard, application, incident, and communication has the potential of distracting us from tasks. When dealing with a high-pressure situation such as data breach, incident responders must retain as much focus as possible.

Task switching can lead to mental fatigue or an increased error rate, which prevents a positive cognitive flow for responders. For example, when dealing with very sophisticated attacks, incident responders need to follow forensic evidence, document findings, and communicate with stakeholders, all while tracing the behavior of possible attackers. Any break in this logical flow could lead to missed indicators, lag time in remediation, or communication failures while context switching between tasks.

Limits of perception and cognition

Cybersecurity as a domain is often discussed as defenders and attackers at war against exploits, patches, and proper defenses. We focus on the technical aspects of IR but lack the inclusion of human factors, such as attention, perception, and our own judgment and experience. We cannot solely focus on the networks and endpoints in an environment; we must also understand and integrate the minds, experience, and intellect of our cyber-defenders.

Human cognition has its limits. Our brains can only process so much data at once, our focus and attention can be split in multiple directions, and our memory is far shorter than that of a computer. Our decision-making, especially in an IR context, is full of biases, blind spots, and limited knowledge or awareness, even with years of experience. In IR, responders are asked to triage multiple alerts in real time, correlate information from multiple tools with different datasets, and interpret signals in a short amount of time.

In some cases, humans can hold only five to nine items in their working memory. This means that if responders have to look at 10 different tools at a time, without automation or excessive notetaking, data points may be lost. When we are under stress, we may experience the phenomena of attention narrowing or decision fatigue, leading to increased time to make decisions or making the wrong call, leading to a larger incident. This does not mean that the human mind is weak or incapable but understanding how our brains work will make sure that we create the best possible tooling and processes to support cognition and perception.

Limits of automation

Without automation, we are left to sort through piles of data, which could mean losing precious time and delays in reacting to an incident. But with too much automation, we may miss true-positive alerts, leading to a serious cyber incident or data exfiltration. Security analysts and incident responders rely heavily on processing massive amounts of data quickly to detect and respond to alerts. However, too much automation could lead to automation bias, that is, the overreliance on automated decisions even when they conflict with our own judgment or we have evidence to the contrary.

Automation bias can reduce attentional vigilance, according to Parasuraman & Riley (1997), specifically in highly complex domains such as IR and cybersecurity at large. Almost 30 years later, in a 2024 paper titled Artificial Intelligence (AI) Safety and Automation Bias, automation bias is mentioned as a critical component of AI deployment and readiness. It could lead responders to ignore anomalies because "the system did not alert," even if it could be a true positive. The automation story is not all bad, though. If automation is built with transparency, responders can use their own judgment and knowledge of the environment to validate the findings.

Siloed knowledge bases

Another human factors challenge facing IR teams is the presence of siloed knowledge bases and fragmented information spread across multiple tools, documents, and teams, as well as varying skill levels. Because technology gives us the data, but not the comprehension of that data, teams may keep their knowledge siloed or maintain a close hold on their processes. In the technical fields, in general, there can be a feeling of wanting to hold on to knowledge to be a Subject Matter Expert (SME).

Tools may keep detections, logs, and TI reports siloed unless there is a large data warehouse that collects and stores all this information together. In larger teams, this may be the case, although there may be larger siloed teams that maintain their own data and use it based on their skillsets and objectives. For example, a TI team may keep their techniques siloed because of their own objective of leveraging Open Source Threat Intelligence (OSINT) data to identify specific IOCs or targeted areas of the business.

Researchers have introduced the distributed cognition theory, which explains shared information structures such as dashboards, playbooks, and incident timelines improve team performance rather than focusing solely on the expertise of a single individual. Without awareness across the IR team, there may be missed handoffs of data or details for an investigation, possible duplication of efforts, or delays in escalating a critical event to the next team. An initial component to help with using a shared vision is to use a collective dashboard or a visual timeline for all team members, providing cross-role visibility into tasks, actions, and case work.

Stress and time pressure

For many responders, action is the most critical component of an investigation, not necessarily thinking about policies or procedures during a critical event. Responders may need to juggle time and case work with the forensic investigation, communicate with technical teams and executives, as well as handling any other legal or regulatory compliance components that they must adhere to. All these technical and psychological challenges can be stressful, with pressure to complete tasks quickly, leading to higher levels of anxiety.

Responders may experience a variety of human factors components, including reduced memory capacity based on cognitive workload, attention narrowing, as mentioned earlier in the chapter, and increased reliance on heuristics, increasing bias. Stress can have a major impact on our cognition, including our memory retrieval and executive functioning, as well as causing challenges with making decisions, leading to decision paralysis or decision inertia.

Each dashboard, tool, alert, communication channel, and investigation will be unique, some with higher levels of stress. But in each case or cyberattack, responders must act efficiently and with excellent decision-making skills. With the proper tools and processes in place and human factors engineering awareness, teams will be able to handle heavy caseloads with reduced stress.

Cognitive workload

For many analysts and responders, their cognitive workload is constantly stretched and pushed, sometimes during an active attack or just by the sheer volume of alerts and technical details that must be reviewed constantly. Cognitive workload is the amount of mental effort that it takes our brains to process information, make decisions, and complete tasks. Even in smaller environments, the amount of data may be so large that it is difficult to automate or act on it all. Tools have their own built-in scoring or risk mechanisms that may not always align, for example, the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) for vulnerabilities versus a vendor's specific risk score within their tool.

Even evaluating those types of risk requires inherent knowledge, decision-making, and the skill of responders to understand the context for each alert. With multiple false positives or limited context around alerts, responders will need to triage and investigate across multiple systems and disparate risk-scoring mechanisms. Without intelligence alert grouping, baselined risk scores, or appropriate tags for systems, IR teams may struggle with cognitive or information overload. Alert fatigue, which will be discussed in future chapters in more depth, is the desensitization to events based on too many signals or false positives.

Human factors intervention

But it's not all bad news. IR teams can leverage a multitude of human factors skills and techniques to improve and refine their practices and processes. Before diving into solutions, IR teams should ask themselves some critical questions to gauge how effectively their systems leverage an understanding of human cognition and decision-making abilities:

How can responders make accurate decisions when stress levels are high during a cyberattack?
How can our IR teams understand the impact of perception on responding to and investigating alerts?
How can our tools or processes be aligned to reduce mental load without compromising sustained awareness during an incident?

Human cognition and perception are not signs of weakness; they are measurable functions of how our brains process large quantities of data. As humans, we are susceptible to automation bias, confirmation bias, and cognitive overload. These functions can be tracked and measured to reduce mistakes in highly complex environments.

Critical stages of decision-making

First, the basic decision of "is this a real alert, false positive, or potential noise or interruption" can keep a team from seeing a true positive. The technical inputs at this stage may come from EDR or a SIEM event, pattern matching and analysis, or TI or hunting activities. The specific human factors risks at this stage include possible alert fatigue, confirmation bias, or limited context of risk scores or vulnerabilities. To resolve this with a human factors engineering (HFE) approach, IR teams should use summarized or automated threat context within their tool stack, use confidence levels based on user or system activity (present and historical), and provide decision support with the use of playbook triggers.

A second and vital moment of decision-making is asking: does this need to be escalated to senior levels, executives, or our customers? At what point during containment or eradication do IR teams need to communicate with senior leadership based on their findings and actions? Most of the inputs at this stage are based on predefined severity levels, the scope of the incident, and business impact, either on internal systems or on clients. To combat potential issues at this stage, HFE principles would interject risk scoring with specific visual indicators, such as red/green flags, threshold-based prompts based on pattern matching, and clearly defined roles and responsibilities for escalation.

A third important area to focus on is remediation. This is the moment where decisions could impact how quickly systems are brought online to support the business or potentially put online before they are completely free of malware or ransomware, with an opportunity for reinfection. Teams are looking for the root cause of the infection or event and focus on eliminating the threat(s). IR groups look at malware analysis tools, logs, vulnerability data, and misconfigurations at either the host, application, or network layer. Potential psychological impacts include satisficing or deciding that a solution is "good enough"; overreliance on prior incidents or data points; and not having a full understanding of how the attacker was able to enter the network. But IR processes could include remediation checklists, artifacts from prior events, and notifications of dependency risks in systems.

Finally, a post-incident review or AAR is carried out after a cyberattack has occurred. IR teams will look at the timeline of the incident, the communication workflow and outputs, as well as any lessons learned from both positive and negative perspectives. A few of the human factors considerations are a culture that focuses on blame rather than a retrospective, memory decay if waiting too long after an event to review and document, and failure to follow up on recommendations and outcomes. But teams can use visual representations for decisions and impact within a graphical interface, provide prompts for AARs, and share revisions with broader teams, encouraging feedback and enhancements for better decision-making.

Cognitive-ready IR

Bringing IR teams away from solely focusing on technical reviews means adapting mental models to handle complex and dynamic situations. This can also elevate individual contributors and managers to hone their skills, make better decisions, and handle stress under pressure. Technical teams can learn to focus on situational awareness, pattern recognition, coordination, and mental modeling to improve decision-making.

For example, when looking at an incident, have an IR team take a step back and evaluate what they're looking at before diving into remediation. Many times, an analyst or engineer will delete logs or pertinent information before ever getting to forensics, thereby potentially erasing helpful evidence. Train IR teams to examine each step thoughtfully. While we can process high volumes of data quickly, to ensure we make the right decision, it's best to discuss it with teammates or even take a five-minute break to reset our thought process.

IR teams can use technology to their advantage by creating comprehensive, not overwhelming or distracting, dashboards that only show the top three or five most important bits of information. Alerts should be correlated and handled automatically with proper summaries. However, those summaries should be available in real or near-real time, with role-specific or team-specific data. As an example, do not have tools that simply list alerts; use automation to group similar alerts, reduce false positives, and ultimately tune out noise so that teams won't even see them. Reducing the amount of information coming into an analyst's feed will help them focus on the problems that matter. This provides a shared understanding across the team, whether team members are newer to the role or organization or have been with the team for some time.

A common theme among any cyber team will be training focused on adaptability and flexibility, instead of running through a list or merely treating it as a checkbox exercise. Teams should train with tabletop exercises that go off script and maybe deal with unique or uncommon scenarios. As an example, have your team start with a common scenario, such as a relatively simple data exfiltration incident with one known exploited vulnerability. Then, add some IOCs from an Advanced Persistent Threat (APT) group, which becomes a zero-day attack. Allow the ramp-up of stress and pressure on the team to push them to act quickly, tossing in unexpected events. It's not meant to stress the team out, but to prepare them to think and act quickly during unexpected events. But be sure to allow teams the opportunity to discuss and relax after the scenario and talk about the stress, the pressure, and any frustration, as well as how they handled it.

Powerful communication comes in many forms, but just like with alerts and notifications, IR teams will want their communication methods to be high-fidelity and trustworthy. Without a consistent and effective communication channel, teams will start to tune out potentially vital information because of alert fatigue. Any time incidents or data are passed between teams is a critical time to make sure there is structure and it is role-based and timely. IR teams can use mental models or taxonomies such as those from NIST or MITRE to add a more human factors approach to their IR playbooks and communication strategies.

Scenario-based exercises

Chaos is not something that would typically be recommended for IR teams during a tabletop exercise. However, there is power in testing and working through a chaotic situation as a team, especially when the stakes are lower and the situation is not as stressful. Tabletop exercises can even be fun and bring the team together, while also identifying possible gaps in playbooks or even how teams make decisions for escalation paths. To explore this further, let's cover a few example scenarios that may be fun for IR teams to test.

In IR tabletop exercises, the terms cold state and hot state describe the level of pressure and realism experienced by participants—in this case, our incident responders. A cold-state exercise occurs in a low-stress environment where participants calmly discuss how they would respond to an incident. A hot-state exercise simulates more closely the operational conditions of an actual security incident. From a human factors perspective, cold-state exercises focus on procedural understanding, while hot-state exercises test decision-making, cognitive workload, and team coordination under stress.

Tabletop exercises can be ineffective as they frequently fail to replicate the real cognitive, operational, and environmental pressures responders experience during actual incidents. Many exercises are overly scripted, allowing participants to follow expected answers rather than actively analyze evolving and complex situations. This can create a false sense of preparedness because responders are not practicing navigating incomplete information, conflicting data, or time pressure.

The first scenario for a tabletop exercise is considering a credential-stuffing account on a third-party vendor that has administrative access to internal systems and datasets. The primary goal would be to test the response to external authentication compromise or account takeover events. Teams should determine the best time and event to escalate to an incident, what identity and MFA techniques are implemented, how and when users are notified, and whether there are any laws or regulations that teams must follow. A fun twist on the exercise could be that the attacker is using spoofed IP addresses that look like customer domain logins.

Another very relevant scenario for a tabletop exercise could be a zero-day exploit in a third-party library or application in a business-critical system. This event focuses on software supply chain security and involves a number of teams, including engineering, security, and procurement or legal teams, depending on the chosen scenario. IR teams will want to determine what inventory solution would provide those application details, what approvals would be required for patching, and the potential impact on production, as well as any legal or notification communications that should be integrated. To add an extra element, teams could find that the application is not catalogued in their application inventory and is internet-facing.

For a bit of variety, another type of scenario that teams could focus on as a tabletop exercise is a phishing attack that led to a Business Email Compromise (BEC). IR teams can focus on the social engineering implications that led to a spear-phishing campaign, as well as any subsequent financial motivation impacting HR and finance teams. They will need to determine how quickly they can detect email or account compromise and email tampering, identify wire transfers and processes, and notify both internal and external teams. As an unexpected twist, teams could have to adjust for an attacker who has already spoofed the CEO's email address and sent payment requirements to the CFO.

Adding some variety to tabletop exercises can prepare teams mentally for numerous attack vectors and scenarios and processes for remediation and recovery. To make any tabletop exercise exciting and memorable for participants, consider having a facilitator who acts as a social engineering proponent, adding conflicting decision points to test playbooks, as well as a delay to communications. These activities will lead the team to find inconsistencies in processes, understand gaps in communication, and identify areas where roles or responsibilities should be better defined.

Psychological safety and a culture of openness

Another area for IR teams to implement human factors components is addressing the challenge of making mistakes and errors, which could lead to a poor perception of the IR team or processes. Incidents are considered to be issues with technical alerting or notification, but they could be greatly improved with a culture of acceptance and understanding of human error. Teams may choose to skip a process or fail to report an issue because of fear of repercussion or the perception that an individual, rather than the tools or technology, failed.

Blame culture can lead IR teams or even leadership to fear punishment or embarrassment, and it could even lead to the underreporting of potentially serious issues. IR teams may lack psychological safety, meaning they worry about misrepresenting data, misleading stakeholders, or even the blame that may be placed on them or other teams. To combat blame culture, IR teams should emulate an SRE culture that focuses on open communication for fixing systems instead of punishing teams for technical issues.

In an IR context, adopting SRE practices means treating incidents as opportunities to improve systems and processes, rather than assigning blame to individuals. This encourages psychological safety, allowing responders to report mistakes, near misses, and failures without fear of punishment. SRE culture also prioritizes clear runbooks, automation to reduce repetitive cognitive workload, and structured post-incident reviews that focus on systemic issues rather than human error alone. Another issue around human error that IR teams face is confusion between three facets of the response process: error, adaptation, and allowing for innovation. Crafting a proper problem statement allows the teams to focus on deviations as workarounds or even possible improvements to systems, rather than a mistake or an issue. Framing and scope are essential for errors, making sure that real errors are categorized as such, rather than something that was a technical control or configuration.

Confirmation bias

Confirmation bias, as described previously, is the way that we search for, interpret, and even choose information based on our perception or assumptions. This means that we could be missing or misinterpreting valid data because our own bias contradicts the evidence in front of us. Specific to IR teams, this could mean focusing on one explanation of an event rather than exploring other options, dismissing evidence that conflicts with someone's interpretation, or seeing new data only through a biased lens based on what they found or discovered previously.

IR teams must act quickly, making decisions around an alert, event, or incident that could have a major impact, but this could lead to confirmation bias. An example of confirmation bias in an IR situation is evaluating a phishing alert and determining that it looks similar to prior events. This is considered a cognitive trap, where teams may miss a spear-phishing or BEC event because it looked benign based on prior experience and patterns. The ticket or incident could be closed without a more thorough analysis, simply based on past harmless events that looked similar, even though there may be indicators of something else occurring.

As another example, IR teams may see a malware alert and determine it to be a false positive because the team saw something similar in their lab or test environment. This is another cognitive trap because a common alert could be hiding the initial stage of a ransomware attack. For example, the team may see a PowerShell script attempting to run with administrative privileges and reach out to the system owner, who may consider that normal behavior. But in fact, there was something else occurring, and the team missed it as they were solely thinking of their previous experience.

For our final example, consider an incident that begins with data exfiltration that is much larger than typical cases. A responder considers this the beginning stage of an APT attack and overlooks a possible insider threat scenario. A cognitive trap in a different direction, this one considers a more complex explanation over a simpler one that would track more closely with the behavior of the data exfiltration alert. The responder may miss warning signs, such as recent resignations, disgruntled employees, or accounts being misused, that would indicate a possible insider event rather than an external threat.

Building learning and system resilience

Within the IR space, our cybersecurity teams are typically measured on how quickly a threat is detected and eradicated, focusing on quantifiable metrics for Mean Time to Remediation (MTTR) or Mean Time to Detection (MTTD). But the technical aspects are not the only important parts of a resilient IR process or the systems that back them up. A resilient IR process is structured to continue functioning even when conditions change quickly, information is incomplete, or systems behave unexpectedly due to a breach. It emphasizes flexibility, clear decision-making structures, and the ability for responders to adjust actions, containment, or eradication based on real-time conditions. Effective IR teams are also measured by how quickly and effectively they learn from incidents, events, alerts, and possible missed detections.

Creating a cybersecurity training and process loop encourages teams to find gaps in systems, assumptions, and, very specifically, communication processes, which could mean the difference between a successful and unsuccessful ransomware attempt. But there are two key areas where IR teams can build human factors into their processes: the ability to adapt after an incident and sharing insights that build upon the institutional knowledge shared between teams. IR teams are responsible not only for detecting and containing incidents but also for absorbing disruption, adapting to new data, and recovering operations while focusing on investigative insights. With intention and collaboration, responders and their leaders can design resilient systems and a feedback learning process for continuous improvement.

The integration of human factors principles can help reduce fear-based responses toward reporting errors, allowing us to design systems that support our cognitive workloads and memory functions and prepare mental models based on experience. Human-centered design can ensure that learning is not a byproduct of an incident but a built-in mechanism for responders and cybersecurity team members. Building resilience in systems is not just about bouncing back; it is about truly improving all areas of the IR life cycle to futureproof the team.

Foundations of trust

To build resilient systems, IR teams must have trust—in themselves, their teams, and their reporting structure. The core IR team must be based on interpersonal trust, and not just between the people on the teams but with their tools and leadership as well. In an IR team with deep trust and focus, analysts and responders can speak up about errors or issues without concern about retribution. This leads them to report possible issues or incidents early, escalate when necessary, and positively impact AARs with honest feedback.

Similar to psychological safety, Edmondson (2004) discusses how teams can more quickly adapt and improve performance so that individuals feel safe. The original study was done for hospitals and in the aviation industry, where human lives are at stake, but the principles remain the same. Translating psychological safety to IR, processes, and tools should allow teams to escalate issues quickly, provide honest feedback even when it can be difficult to hear, and allow individuals to acknowledge mistakes or errors.

A real-world example involves responders who failed to escalate an incident after ransomware was discovered on critical systems. In this case, the responders were nervous about telling their leadership that ransomware was spreading due to the possibility of being fired or removed from their jobs. Instead, ransomware spread to critical systems such as databases, encrypting and locking out the backbone of the environment, delaying development work, and impacting customers, ultimately leading to revenue loss. If the team was supported or felt encouraged to share information quickly, they may have contained or even eradicated the ransomware before it spread too far within the network.

Communications handoffs and automation

Automation is all about balance. If automation is in place but it takes too long, is inaccurate, or ends up performing actions incorrectly, it can be a detriment to the IR teams that need trustworthy automation. If the automation fails to support the decisions of the IR analysts or responders, systems are considered to be more fragile than resilient. If the tools or automation lack transparency, have poor integration with other tools, or conduct work in silo, IR teams will not reap the benefits of a robust and trustworthy automation implementation.

For example, if alerts are lacking context or there is no explanation behind the actions, IR teams may lose trust or faith, reverting to manual or error-prone tasks instead. Hutchins, way back in 1995, discussed distributed cognition theory, where teams operate as cognitive systems that rely on representations. Even then, psychological studies have shown that individuals perform better when they can review a representation of data, as we do now with alerts, dashboards, and visualizations.

Using these types of distributed systems and information can help share an understanding of the IR process and teams. One of the best ways to use automation and communication tools is to adopt explainable automation that uses traceability and rationale for decisions around alerts and tasks. Using chat platforms where teams can collaborate quickly will allow the distribution of decisions across different groups, such as IR, to executives. A final recommendation: use user-centered flows that build automation to support reflection and even uncertainty in decisions, which encourages a focus on resiliency and reliability, versus the speed of carrying out tasks and resolution.

Feedback loops in tooling and procedures

Feedback loops are the bread and butter of any IR team to balance and improve communications, tooling, and processes. Given the ever-evolving landscape of cybersecurity threats, techniques, emerging technologies, and laws and regulations, IR teams must continuously improve to keep up. As mentioned previously, when a healthy platform of trust exists within an IR team, individuals can use reflection to turn an incident into an actionable insight.

During an incident, analysts and responders are focused on the event or restoring systems to production. But the incredible value of an incident is that once the incident is resolved, teams have the opportunity to evaluate it. If feedback loops do not exist in tooling or processes, organizations and IR teams specifically will be doomed to miss patterns of repeated alerts, as well as potential blind spots in detections or tooling, as well as lacking effective tooling or automation potential. Organizations must continue to expand their capacity to improve, or else they may be stuck repeating the same mistakes again.

A real-world example of this is an IR team that conducts a retrospective after a particularly devastating incident. In this scenario, the team missed a detection, which led to a massive data exfiltration of crown jewel data or Intellectual Property (IP). During the retrospective, the team focuses on blaming each other for the lack of detections, misconfigurations of alerts, and, ultimately, not focusing enough on the data, leading to mistakes across the team. This exercise leaves the IR team frustrated, angry, and pointing fingers at each other, instead of sharing true and actionable data.

Teams should instead focus on implementing some best practices to build robust feedback loops. First, teams should use structured AARs with scripted prompts around how decisions are made, how teams handle uncertainty, and the various paths for coordination and collaboration that were used. Structured AARs evaluate how the response was conducted, identify what worked well, determine what failed, and capture lessons learned to improve future responses.

Next, teams should integrate those lessons learned into everyday practices, such as alert tuning and building proper logic and transparency into automation and scripting. Finally, IR teams should keep a well-documented decision library that helps them learn, iterate, and detect trends in decisions that are either working or not working and require improvement.

Resilient learning systems

The focus behind improving IR teams through human factors is to turn an incident into a cycle of growth and development. Each incident, alert, detection, or event is an opportunity to learn, adapt, and build more resiliency in IR processes and tooling. As many academic and technical resources claim, it is not a matter of if an organization gets breached, but when. This means that we can either continue to repeat the errors or mistakes from the past or open a path to evolution.

Resilient systems for learning and education are built when both technology and humans work and grow together. Specifically, to an IR team, playbooks will be updated and adapted, tools will be updated and able to mold around user behavior, and past events will inform and update decision-making processes for the future. IR teams can pull from resilience engineering and the four capabilities that make up the process:

Anticipate possible disruptions or interruptions to systems and tools.
Monitor emerging threats and system or network health for anomalies.
Respond to real-time events that may be ambiguous or challenging.
Learn and respond to events or incidents from both failure and success, never fearing failure.

The following figure illustrates a five-level maturity model that describes how IR practices evolve within an organization. It shows the progression from a reactive state, where incidents are handled in an ad hoc manner, to a resilient, learning-driven culture in which feedback, training, and continuous improvement strengthen the organization's ability to manage cybersecurity incidents:

Figure 3.3: Resilience maturity model

Incidents are not the result of solely mistakes or poor decisions, but also when systems are poorly designed or ignore adaptive reasoning from mistakes or errors. Teams should consider a resiliency maturity model to improve system design and allocate time to continuously iterate and build more mature IR programs. Using the preceding figure, teams can start from level 1, a reactive mindset, and progress to level 5, a more resilient and robust IR model. Every security and IR team will be at a different level of maturity; the idea is to determine where the team fits based on current processes and efficiency, then build human factors capability in a way that is sustainable.

A case (or two) for human factors

There are multiple considerations for integrating human factors into IR teams, but let's put it all into practice. We will look at two example cases: the Capital One Breach in 2019 and the Maersk incident in 2017. These examples have been provided due to the nature and size of these incidents, not to cast blame or victim-shame. On the contrary, these events are not simply about missed detections or misconfigurations in systems but evaluating the human factors and psychological aspects.

The Capital One breach

To summarize this well-known breach, a former AWS employee exploited a misconfigured web application firewall (WAF) to gain access to systems. The actor was able to exfiltrate data from over 100 million user accounts. One of the unique aspects of the attacker in this situation was that they leaked a lot of the details of the data breach themselves in public forums, highlighting legacy data and poor patch management in the environment.

Ultimately, though, there were several factors that led to this event. First, alert fatigue was identified in the indictment from the United States (US) Department of Justice (DoJ). Another aspect was that the SOC experienced normalization of deviance, meaning critical alerts were missed because of the volume of noise in alerts. Finally, misconfigurations were undocumented and left unremediated, with a lack of communication between the cloud teams and IR and SecOps.

This case highlights the multiple recommendations that we have discussed and how they may have either prevented this event or provided early detection that something was amiss:

Reduce any friction between teams and improve communication
Tune and tailor alerts based on feedback loops
Provide open and honest collaboration on legacy vulnerabilities or misconfigurations—reduce blame or shame around patching
Maersk and NotPetya

A final example to highlight the importance of human factors in IR teams is the Maersk environment that was brought down by the NotPetya worm in 2017. This worm was able to infect a Ukrainian accounting system that was left unpatched, bringing down their global network. Laptops on the helpdesk started displaying strange notes about either files being encrypted or repairing the filesystem without prompting.

This event had multiple areas where human factors principles could have positively impacted the organization. First, there was a lack of segmentation of systems; the laptops and computers were impacted quickly because of their interconnectivity. There was an over-reliance on centralized systems, leading to a single issue impacting their network on a global scale. Finally, the recovery of the environment was due to the team finding an offline domain controller, a fortuitous find rather than a sign of resilience in their architecture.

With the addition of a few human factors components, this cyberattack may have had less impact or been detected faster. First, if there was a comprehensive and mature focus on organizational memory, processes and system data could have been catalogued and a robust response procedure created. Redundancy awareness or building resilient systems that are able to withstand outages or cyberattacks, would have provided a robust recovery plan.

Summary

IR is often framed as a purely technical process, but human factors ultimately determine its success or failure. Success is defined as the efficient resolution of an incident, while failure is the inability to resolve incidents efficiently. Cognitive biases, stress, workload distribution, and communication dynamics all play critical roles in how responders perceive, analyze, and act during an incident.

Poorly designed alerts, unclear roles, and fatigue (of all varieties) can slow containment and lead to mistakes. But with the assistance of well-structured processes, psychological safety, and effective collaboration, recovery can be accelerated and the overall impact reduced.

This chapter highlighted the importance of recognizing cognitive limitations, such as decision paralysis, and implementing strategies that offset them using scenario-based exercises and error reporting. Designing tools that align with natural cognitive processes and fostering trust within cross-functional teams are essential steps to strengthening resilience.

Transitioning to the next chapter, we will expand these ideas into the day-to-day reality of security analysts, the constant monitoring of high-volume data streams, the unique stressors of shift work in critical positions, and the systemic challenges of balancing automation with human judgment.
