4
Human Factors in Security Operations

Human Factors Engineering (HFE) has implications across many cybersecurity disciplines, but the backbone of any cybersecurity team is the security operations center (SOC). A SOC could be comprised of many different skillsets, tools, techniques, and processes. Whether supporting a government agency, a healthcare organization, or a manufacturing company, the SOC is the first line of defense against threats.

But like incident response (IR) teams, SOC teams commonly face challenges because of the sheer volume of alerts, notifications, tools, and the constant sophistication of attackers and threats. IR teams typically become involved after an incident has occurred, where a SOC team is considered the first line of defense for detection and prevention. To begin, let's cover the structure and organization of a SOC while addressing the possible pressure and stress of working in a security operations environment. HFE can provide insight into where gaps may exist and how to address them with a human-centric and engineering approach.

In this chapter, you will learn about the practical applications of integrating human factors in SecOps teams. We will cover some of the modern challenges, such as overloaded dashboards, complex tooling, and alert fatigue, that a SecOps team faces. Then, we will cover some of the easier and more practical ways to resolve these challenges, including color coding, using automation properly within tooling, and balancing structure and standard operating procedures (SOPs) with operational activities. Finally, you will learn how to integrate human factors into SecOps teams, including adaptive decision-making, incorporating human-centered analysis, and integrating human cognition limits into tasking.

In this chapter, you will cover the following topics:

Defining security operations
Key human factors challenging in security operations
Cognitive demands on a modern SOC
Beyond alert fatigue
Role of explainability
Interface and system design for analyst success
SOPs and the pitfalls of structure and rigidity
Incorporating human factors for SecOps teams
Defining security operations

A SOC's group of analysts is responsible for maintaining an organization's security posture. While SOC teams have evolved over the last few years, the mission remains the same: protecting the organization from ongoing cyber threats with early identification and investigation. SOC teams, depending on the size of the organization, may operate several different functions:

Threat monitoring: EDR, SIEM, XDR, and other tooling
Triage for alerts: Analysis of alerts to determine TP versus FP
Threat hunting: Depending on team size, may look for threats based on intelligence, including indicators of compromise (IOCs), anomalous behavior in logs, and misconfigurations or exploitable vulnerabilities
Incident escalation: Once a threat is validated, an incident will be created and passed to a separate IR team
Tuning and rules: Update detections to improve accuracy

Typically, a SOC team interfaces with the other aspects of a cybersecurity program, including the IR team, the threat intelligence or threat hunting teams, vulnerability management, as well as any IT or cloud operations teams. An SOC team may be internal to the organization or an external third-party entity, but its mission remains the same, provide 24/7 support to protect the organization. These security analysts can have a wide variety of skills, from data science backgrounds to development or IT operations experience.

Running a 24/7 operation

Most SOC operations are 24/7 or use a follow-the-sun model, having teams across the globe to support multiple geographies. In smaller organizations, SOC teams could operate in one or two time zones, but larger teams are most likely global; it all depends on the organization's needs and size. Regardless of team structure, most teams will still experience a shift handoff. In smaller teams, SOC analysts will most likely experience sleep disruption during high-alert times or even if there is certain expertise required on specific events.

Some of the other challenges of a 24/7 operation are analyst burnout, desensitization, team cohesion, or challenges in handing off alerts during shift changes. This is common for any 24/7 team, but in SOC, decision-making on possible critical alerts could have serious implications. Missed detections, lack of escalation, or critical alerts could all compound the stress and fatigue that analysts experience. SOC teams must monitor any number of tools, dashboards, events, and alerts, and use a combination of on-premise and third-party open source tools to conduct a comprehensive investigation.

Analysts experience a high volume of alerts, pressure to investigate events quickly, and limited closure on those alerts. Communication is a critical component of any SOC, and with the high volume of information, it can be important to use a standardized mechanism for sharing details. Without that, teams will experience frustration with limited information or even lack any kind of common communication channels. When teams have to collaborate in a consistently high-stress environment, it could lead to alert fatigue, cognitive overload, or even challenges in handing off details to the next group.

Key human factors challenges in security operations

SOC teams can benefit greatly from integrating HFE practices due to burnout, high-stress environments, and disparate tools leading to cognitive overload and alert fatigue. Including additional factors such as desensitization to critical alerts, SOC team members and leadership can identify gaps and solve them with the understanding of HFE and psychological principles. Instead of focusing on technology and tooling, teams can address decision-making and configurations with a focus on the individual and team structure instead.

Human factors challenges and implications include cognitive overload, alert fatigue, desensitization to cyber threats, and tool complexity within SecOps environments. These demands on SecOps teams can lead to attention management issues, missed detections, and high false positive rates, which can lead to memory workload issues.

Cognitive overload

Cognitive overload can occur for two main reasons within a SecOps team: either data is siloed, making it difficult to identify patterns, or dashboards and tools are fragmented, forcing analysts to log into too many different systems with unique interfaces and user experiences. This is where SecOps teams can benefit from building better visualizations or leveraging design teams to discuss possible improvements. HFE is not solely about the psychological implications of tool design, but also the engineering practices behind it and the visualizations that work better for humans. Without a full understanding of engineering practices, tools may be misconfigured, leading to missed detections, high false-positive rates, and ultimately leading to other cognitive overload challenges, such as attention management and fatigue.

For example, if a SecOps team is flooded with false-positive alerts day after day, individuals may start to feel like they are drowning in a sea of alerts. It can feel like an impossible mountain to climb, never reaching the peak. This impacts morale and the desire to continue working to close tickets. If one feels like they cannot ever have a positive impact, they may burn out, feel frustrated, and ultimately not see a point in continuing to do their work.

Understanding our cognitive limitations and functions can help identify gaps in technology or tooling. For example, a SOC group may use tools with different color schemes. Imagine a tool that uses red, yellow, and green to indicate a critical, medium, or low alert, and another tool that uses a heat map to indicate a possible critical event. For something that may sound simple, our brains must adjust and adapt every time we switch to that tool, leading to possible stress or frustration during context switching.

Alert fatigue and desensitization

Alert fatigue is a well-studied and documented event across multiple cybersecurity teams, but especially in a SecOps environment. The entirety of the mission focuses on automation and the manual review of alerts across the organization. But when the alert volume is high and the fidelity is low, it can lead to frustration or stress from seeing so many alerts that are not relevant. Not only that, but teams may feel defeated by the sheer volume of events; it can feel like climbing a mountain and never reaching its peak.

A great example of alert fatigue is a SOC analyst sitting at their desk for eight to ten hours a day, staring at multiple screens and tools, all showing alerts and notifications for potential cybersecurity events. Imagine that 95% of those events are false positives, meaning that the majority of their day is spent watching alerts that are due to operational activity, expected behavior, or benign administrative tasks. Repetitive and mundane tasks lead to burnout, frustration, and overall dissatisfaction with their job, feeling like climbing a mountain that has no peak. Without any engaging or innovative projects, an individual will start to feel bored, possibly miss alerts, and eventually leave their job, seeking projects and tasks with more complexity or that are far more fulfilling.

This section will simply outline the common challenges around alert fatigue and why it exists, but in later sections, we will cover how to combat fatigue with a variety of methods. Desensitization to alerts could happen for several reasons. It may be that there are far too many alerts coming into the teams; maybe there are multiple dashboards that display network, email, mobile device, and identity alerts. Looking at hundreds or thousands of alerts a day for eight to ten hours straight could make anyone become complacent.

Tuning and tailoring alerts is an essential tool to combat desensitization, and to help reduce the common events that may be false positives, or even if they are true positives, they may not be relevant given a pattern of operational behavior. For example, imagine a flood of alerts because a systems administrator is performing testing on their server. This may generate a ton of alerts for the SOC, requiring resources to spend time on reaching out to the system owner, understanding the behavior, and ultimately refining the alerts. This is only one type of alert that must be monitored; at scale, it can be challenging to review, remediate, and understand each alert while also running the operations center.

Tool complexity handoffs

Tools, applications, dashboards, and bears, oh my! Any operations team works with a variety of tools, but SecOps teams must use a variety of sources to gain insights into whether an alert is a TP or an FP. The larger and more complex the technical environment, the more tools that will be required. For example, an organization running in an AWS cloud environment may only opt to use some of the in-house tooling to manage SIEM and inventory. However, an organization that uses a multi-cloud approach may want to use a third-party SaaS product or even use multiple cloud platforms' unique tool offerings.

This tool complexity means analysts look at data streams from multiple platforms, often in different contexts and using a variety of fields. For example, one tool may use a risk calculation of low, medium, critical, and high, with a numerical value of 1-10, while another risk-scoring mechanism for the same data uses 1-100. Something simple, such as numerical scores require additional cognitive processing to determine the impact or criticality of the alert. To show just a sample of how SOC tooling meets cognition, Figure 4.1 displays an example of SOC tools, some of the cognitive concepts while analysts review alerts, and the communications, process, and documentation outputs expected during an alert review:

Figure 4.1 – Where cognition meets tooling

For example, take a SIEM, the center for logging and a trigger point to create alerts that may identify malicious behavior or patterns. Those logs must be parsed, reviewed, and contain some automation to provide a detection that funnels to the SOC. From there, an analyst must use playbooks, their own inherent knowledge and skills, and understand what each alert means, both in isolation and as a possible pattern or trend. Multiple forms of communication come from each alert, possibly a ticket that is closed or escalated to the IR team, communications channels for quick investigations, as well as possibly tuning for alerts or additional updates to Knowledge Base (KB) articles or playbooks. Another layer of complexity on top of tooling is the shift changes that occur between time zones or analysts who finish their day and hand off tasking to the next group. During this time, open cases may be re-assigned, whether context is provided or detailed notes, and another analyst must take the case and determine a path forward. In some cases, more analysis will be required, or the case may be closed, but without a proper and detailed description of the event, TP alerts may be missed.

Cognitive demands of a modern SOC

Modern SecOps teams rely on a variety of automation techniques to build detections, comb through mountains of data, and combat the stress and pressure that comes with a highly critical business function. There are many new findings in the academic and research communities that highlight opportunities to implement human factors understanding into building a high-performance SOC team.

Alert fatigue has been discussed, but this is just one of the many levels of fatigue that a SOC analyst or SecOps team may face. An article from Swimlane in 2022 highlighted that analysts receive thousands of alerts per day, sometimes spending hours performing an initial analysis, and can miss critical events. In the survey conducted, there were over 60% of analysts who reported fatigue as a possible indicator of missed detections. Another academic research paper from 2025 on alert fatigue in SOC teams remains a challenge, highlighting the need to evaluate other areas of human factors opportunities for analysts.

Attention management

Our cognitive functions only stretch so far, and with a finite number of cognitive resources available, analysts must research threats, document and present findings, and monitor tools and a variety of alerts. Paying attention to all these different factors while under constant stress makes it difficult for humans to pay close attention to every detail and aspect. Without an understanding of how our focus and attention work as part of our cognitive reasoning and decision-making for complex problem sets, we may miss a possible opportunity to bridge those gaps.

Specifically, attention management in a SecOps team could be impacted by interrupt-driven alerts or communications, context switching, a high level of vigilance during shifts, and inconsistent interfaces across a variety of tools. When analysts are constantly moving between signals, chat interfaces, and constant escalations, it can be difficult to maintain focus on a specific case or item. That, combined with the context switching between six to ten different tools, leads to ineffective focus.

There are multiple techniques that SOC teams can utilize to address attention management for SOC analysts in several practical ways. Most importantly, attention is something that we all struggle with, so some of these strategies may be useful across teams and disciplines. First, prioritize and filter alerts so that analysts are only presented with the most relevant and high-risk events, which will also help in reducing noise and preventing constant context switching. Second, design tools and dashboards that group related alerts and highlight key signals, which are critical components in both attention and burnout. Third, implement structured workflows and protected focus time, ensuring analysts can complete investigations without continuous interruptions from new alerts or messages.

Working memory limits

Working memory is where we hold the latest and most important information in short-term storage in our brains. In the cognitive psychology world, working memory can only retain about 4+1 chunks of data at a time. The original designation for understanding working memory was seven items, plus or minus two. This limit becomes incredibly relevant in a high-paced and multi-faceted environment such as SOC. Analysts must retain IP addresses, user information, business context, alert data, and signals, and develop hypotheses based on this data, simultaneously.

Modern cognitive science research suggests that the true capacity of working memory is closer to about four meaningful items, not seven. When people remember more, they usually rely on chunking strategies, prior knowledge, or external components and tools rather than pure working memory capacity. For example, remembering a familiar phrase or pattern counts as a single "chunk," even though it contains multiple elements. Because of this, modern human factors and interface design guidance have shifted away from the 7 ± 2 rule. Systems that present analysts with many simultaneous alerts, indicators, or decision points can easily exceed realistic cognitive limits, which, based on how current SOC teams operate, could be a huge detriment to the success of the team.

A few areas where working memory is challenged are in cross-tool overload, context loss, and interruptions for analysts based on escalations, because individuals must move between suspicious IPs, user logs, ticket details and descriptions, EDR or malware alerts, and network analysis. Context loss can happen because of the sheer volume of data that must be reviewed, as well as moving from one alert to another in a short time, which could lead to missing or losing key details for each case. In any security operations team, having to escalate alerts or investigations during a triage stage could mean losing context or understanding while juggling multiple investigations.

There are psychological studies from 2010 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2864034/) that show memory becomes error-prone when our brains try to hold onto multiple unrelated items, especially in a disruption-based environment such as a SOC. Information must be retained in our working memory, and when pieces of data start to leak out, analysts may misremember IPs, misinterpret logs, or make incorrect correlations based on bad data or the components that we put together out of order.

Cognitive overload and decision-making

SOC analysts must maintain and utilize multiple interfaces, for example, a SIEM for log events and an EDR dashboard or alerts from servers and systems. Cognitive overload is the phenomenon where an individual exceeds their working memory, described previously, and this can cause slowdowns, errors in documents or decision-making, and even lead to higher stress. When there are higher mental demands placed on a SOC analyst, the first line of defense of any cybersecurity team, it can be a driver to overanalyze critical alerts, experience burnout or frustration, and ultimately lead to missed signals or detections.

But cognitive overload can come from a variety of sources, including the need to enter data in multiple areas, mentally shuffling context, and inflexibility when it comes to checklists or processes. When analysts must enter the same information into a ticketing system, a security orchestration and automation and response (SOAR) platform, and maybe emails or notes to leadership, they can suffer from mental friction due to an overload of working memory demands. Experiencing inflexibility when it comes to checklists or playbooks can lead to checklist bias, where analysts follow a prescriptive process versus using adaptive reasoning to resolve incidents.

People have noticed that continuous partial attention to tasks can seriously impact and reduce task-switch resilience. Even in recent ACM studies and workshops, the result of multidisciplinary research, like we are doing here with human factors in cybersecurity, can provide meaningful solutions to cognitive-load and mental resiliency for high-stress and multi-tasking environments. Without understanding how analysts and operators experience psychological or mental workloads, organizations may struggle to identify and resolve common issues in a SecOps environment.

Beyond alert fatigue

Alert fatigue and mental workloads are one component of what a SecOps team experiences, but what about the other cognitive factors? Analysts and operators may suffer from other factors, such as a massive flood of noise or alerts, a barrage of false positives to sift through, and psychological aspects such as learned helplessness or confirmation bias. Some areas of human factors engineering are seen across cybersecurity disciplines, but they each have unique aspects that can erode our ability to make clear and quality decisions.

Signal to noise

SecOps teams must wade through a sea of alerts, hundreds or thousands of alerts and signals that diminish one's ability to determine a true threat versus noise, or irrelevant alerts. In human factors research, there can be a decline in perceptual sensitivity when an individual is experiencing cognitive fatigue, meaning that analysts and operators could start to lean more conservative (escalate everything) or more erratic (wild swings between true positives and false positives) in their reporting.

Vigilance decrement is when the attention of the analyst can deteriorate within 15 minutes of starting a task that requires incredible focus. This phenomenon can be a challenge when a SOC analyst may be on a shift for eight to ten hours, requiring vigilance on alerts for most of that time. Even if analysts take breaks every couple of hours or have a long break in between those hours, they may need to focus for several hours at a time. With an average of 4,500 alerts a day and false-positive rates anywhere between 67% and 99%, requiring focus for an entire shift through the noise would be difficult for anyone.

Wading through the constant barrage of alerts and signals can lead to decision fatigue. Imagine reviewing thousands of signals a day, only to have one or two that are meaningful or may lead to an incident. An analyst may go through an entire shift and never escalate an incident to the IR team. Individuals may be prone to taking shortcuts, ignoring complex or nuanced alerts, or reverting to defaults instead of providing a cohesive picture of an event. Decision fatigue could be the result of several human factors, but an onslaught of noise makes decision-making the primary duty of any SOC analyst.

False positives

False positives, false alarms, or looking at alerts in the wrong direction can make alerts and investigations take up to almost 40% longer without boosting detection rates for true positives. That statistic was based on a controlled experiment to determine, when false positive alerts are at a higher rate, how analysts in a SOC role perform. Essentially, the researchers found that analysts performed lower on handling false positive events because of the reduction in discerning true signals. This study alone highlights the need to provide an accurate picture of alerts and signals to analysts; otherwise, stress, burnout, and decreased performance could all occur.

Outside of alert fatigue and stress or burnout, analysts may also experience a loss of trust in the data and decreased morale for their overall job and duties. When analysts are constantly looking at false alarms, they are more prone to be skeptical or believe alerts are noise, rather than providing a true investigation. Not only does a high rate of false positives reduce confidence in the tooling and automation around alerts, but it can also make analysts feel a lack of purpose or that their work is not as impactful (which is simply not true).

SOC analysts are the unsung heroes of cybersecurity teams, but without the proper alerting, automation, and tooling in place, they may suffer from risk passivity, skepticism, and low morale. There are several possible human factors areas to improve the experience for analysts, including providing automation that makes sense, providing adaptive alert management, as well as training and workflows that reduce the burden and false positives.

Effective automation should handle repetitive and low-value tasks such as data enrichment, log aggregation, and basic triage so analysts can focus on higher-level analysis for more complex cases. When automation is transparent and predictable, analysts are more likely to trust and effectively use it, reducing cognitive load and improving overall efficiency. Bad automation will not be used and could even cause mistrust in the future for any subsequent development activities.

Adaptive alert management helps SOC analysts manage the volume and complexity of alerts by dynamically prioritizing and organizing them based on context, risk, and analyst feedback. Give the power back to the analysts, encouraging them to help tune, tailor, and refine alerts. Instead of presenting every alert with equal urgency, adaptive systems can group related alerts, adjust severity levels, and highlight the most relevant information.

Clear procedures for triage, investigation, and escalation reduce uncertainty and ensure analysts spend less time navigating ambiguous processes. Well-designed training programs and structured workflows can help analysts quickly understand how to evaluate alerts and determine appropriate responses. Empowering analysts to work independently requires robust documentation and process workflow to support their work. Provide them with background information and allow them the creativity and confidence to triage, react, and make good decisions.

Learned helplessness

Learned helplessness is a common psychological phenomenon where repeated exposure to frustration or stress can lead individuals to stop changing their position or situation, even when a possible solution is available or even right in front of them. This phenomenon goes way back to 1972 but was studied as recently as 2022 in organizational and cybersecurity contexts. What this means to SOC analysts is that when they face constant alerts that are either low priority or low fidelity, it means there are very rarely positive outcomes. Tickets are either deemed false positives, low risk, or irrelevant for the user behavior, marking analysts to either dismiss or escalate issues, with little room in the middle.

With the constant shift in priorities from threat actors or executives, skilled analysts may perceive that there is no reward or meaning to react to an alert. If there is a consistent event to respond to low-fidelity alerts and either no action is taken or it is deemed a non-incident repeatedly, individuals may disengage or feel as if their work is not meaningful. SecOps personnel could experience alert apathy, reduced initiative, or even burnout because of the previous two phenomena.

To remedy learned helplessness, cybersecurity teams can implement a few techniques to provide individuals with the tools to reduce it. Provide analysts with the authority to investigate, escalate, and remediate issues within defined boundaries. When analysts see that their actions directly affect outcomes, they regain a sense of control and effectiveness. Show analysts how their investigations contributed to stopping threats, improving detections, or strengthening defenses. Regular incident reviews and recognition reinforce that their efforts matter and lead to tangible improvements.

Perception and confirmation bias

Perception and confirmation bias are two related psychological functions that may overlap or have an impact when combined within a SecOps environment. Perception bias is when an individual evaluates data based on their own experience, which may impact how they understand a situation. Whereas confirmation bias is when an individual factors data based on their beliefs, even discounting evidence that contradicts their own beliefs.

When SOC analysts must handle ambiguous threats or alerts, they may unconsciously rely on their beliefs to filter data that they expect, or do not expect, to exist. This could manifest in a few ways; as an example, an analyst sees a familiar IP address that they know is a benign address, ignoring other indicators that could mean a system compromise. Or, another example, an analyst sees behavior for a user that seems like it's always a false positive when other indicators combined could mean an account takeover or full system compromise.

The cost of any form of bias within a SecOps setting could lead to misidentified alerts, reinforcement loops using past decisions or assumptions, and putting too much trust in tooling. When any team trusts in a tool too much, it could miss misconfigurations, have either too many or too few alerts, or give itself a narrow mental model. Threats change quickly, and overreliance on tooling without updating strategies and configurations could lead to missed detections or real threats.

To combat perception and confirmation bias, SOC teams can implement a few strategies to ensure clear communication and action on events. Leadership should provide analysts with standardized investigation steps that require checking multiple possible causes before reaching a conclusion. Structured workflows help prevent analysts from jumping to the first explanation from the first dataset. Incorporate training and procedures that encourage analysts to actively look for evidence that contradicts their initial hypothesis. This practice helps counter confirmation bias and ensures analysts evaluate alerts based on objective evidence rather than expectations.

Role of explainability

Digging further into the human factors aspects of any SecOps teams, the role of explainability focuses more on why a system or alert is triggered for analysis. A few components from the user interface (UI) and user experience (UX) side of tooling are feature attribution, confidence scoring, and logic paths, such as the MITRE ATT&CK framework. This is where cognition meets the analyst and the tooling they used to be successful in their work.

Feature attribution is the cue for an analyst to see an alert flagged for very specific anomalous behavior, for example, some network traffic identified as odd because it is outside of normal business hours. Using a confidence scoring mechanism allows a SecOps team to determine anomalous behavior within a certain degree of confidence. In combination with attack pathing or mapping, these techniques create a cohesive picture for analysts that shifts security tooling from a passive picture to a more collaborative system.

Automation bias versus automation distrust

Automation is a powerful component to reduce manual tasks, burnout, and frustration across teams. However, when automation is used incorrectly, SecOps teams could miss true positive alerts, be overwhelmed with false positives, and ultimately lose trust and faith in technology. Since SOC analysts rely on SIEM and logging solutions, SOAR, ML-based threat detection, and even user and entity behavior analytics (UEBA), these tools must operate at maximum efficacy. Without a trustworthy and reliable system, teams may experience automation bias or automation distrust.

Automation bias occurs when a team relies too heavily on automation or even AI outputs, whether they are fully complete or missing context. An example would be using an ML-based security tool that provides part of a picture, creating alerts that determine a TP versus FP without context. The analyst must trust the tool configuration to perform their work, but this trust could lead to accepting potentially false information. On the other side, automation distrust is the rejection of AI or ML-based tool decisions because of a lack of transparency in the decision-making process.

For a specific example, consider a SOC team that has been inundated with alerts, but has a smaller team of five analysts for a mid-sized organization. The team must automate initial detection and response as much as possible, which requires them to trust the outcome of alerts. The team initially started quite skeptically, but they soon learned how helpful it was and allowed them to work on more complex cases. But automation drift occurred, meaning that the AI tooling started to lean more towards identifying false positives, even when there were indications that an APT group may be targeting their systems. The team trusted the automation too much, ignoring possible indicators, leading to a large-scale ransomware attack. While this may seem like a dramatic escalation, relying too heavily on automation could lead to missed detections or incorrect assumptions about telemetry.

There are multiple human factors areas within automation bias and distrust, including cognitive load, uncertainty, and decision logic that seems confusing or opaque. This mistrust or bias is not just based on one analyst or individual, but rather a systematic problem when it comes to security tooling and configuration. As an example, a burnt-out or frustrated SOC team may overly rely on tooling decisions from an AI-based response simply because they don't have the time, energy, or support to work through any misconfigurations.

Explainability in concrete examples

Without explainability, security tools can become opaque (heavy with noise), untrusted, and ultimately reinforce bias, which, in the last section, we noted was a serious detriment to running a successful SecOps team. The first example where explainability is essential is in the tool procurement or transition phase. When a security analyst team is choosing a new SOAR solution, without a fully comprehensive decision matrix on which tools work and which ones don't, analysts could feel that they are left out of critical tooling decisions.

Another example where explainability could mean the difference to an effective SOC team is using training and teaching mechanisms for decision-making and automation trust. Providing the technical background and transparency around why a tool operates gives confidence to the analysts who use it. For example, if a tool simply states that an alert is "not significant" without any description or analysis, it could be easy to dismiss the selection entirely or trust it far too easily.

Consider a SOC team that manages five different dashboards, two EDR consoles, one SIEM, one ticketing system, and one consolidated interface. Depending on the type of alert, an analyst may need to look at any combination of the tooling to make a determination on how to proceed. While looking at five different tools is daunting enough, random combinations of telemetry without any set pattern can be frustrating or even confusing.

But SOC teams can use a variety of techniques to ensure explainability is part of their tool stack. First, teams should inquire about transparency in tooling during procurement, or even as part of technical support calls or engagements. Teams can also train and run learning sessions on how to use automation appropriately, whether that is from the development team or the SecOps team perspective. Ideally, SecOps teams will have some engagement and choices when it comes to selecting tools, choosing automation that works for them, and ultimately being part of the procurement process.

Dashboard overload

Another important human factors engineering challenge for SecOps teams is dashboard overload. This occurs when SOC analysts are utilizing multiple tools, applications, web interfaces, and usually a combination of dashboards. Multiple dashboards present a number of challenges, including a lack of context, disorganized or scattered information, and excessive data represented in multiple views. As mentioned previously, SOC analysts have between six and ten different dashboards or tools to monitor, and in larger organizations, this could be even more.

From a psychological perspective, cognition is impacted by multiple dashboards in the form of information blindness, context switching, and attention tunneling, which may result in slowness in response to detections. Information blindness is when analysts have trouble finding and understanding detections because of the sheer volume of data. Context switching is a well-known issue for any individual who must work back and forth between disparate tools and siloed data. Attention tunneling can also happen to anyone who must swap between tools, where one focuses solely on one screen, missing other alerts or detections in other applications.

There are some very real-world impacts to dashboard overload, the first being a possible delay in escalation for high or critical alerts. One well-studied consequence is the high turnover of analysts who are frustrated, overwhelmed, and tired of dealing with multiple tools. Even though this phenomenon has been well researched, teams continue to struggle with tool and dashboard overload, leading to a constant revolving door of training new personnel, analysts leaving due to frustration, and training again. With this constant rotation of new personnel, it could be increasingly difficult to identify more sophisticated attacks using lateral movement within the environment.

Interface and system design for analyst success

There are options to reduce the possibility of dashboard overload, the various forms of bias and trust, and other limitations that we experience. The technical tools and applications we use are highly customizable, or we can build controls around them to make them more usable for analysts. But even with integrated dashboards, user-centered design, and adaptive interfaces, these do not get to the core of tool design. By digging into some true examples of interfaces and color specifics, we will explore opportunities to create comprehensive interfaces for security analysts.

Poor UX in SIEM

Poor UX does not exist simply in the design of the interface, but can be present because of the configuration, or lack of configuration, done by the implementation team. For example, a SecOps team starts to see a flood of alerts that all have the same weight (all highs) or have no context for the alert. This flood of alerts may indicate ransomware or some other sophisticated attack, but if alert floods happen regularly with the same pattern, this may appear more like a false-positive flood. Alert floods could certainly be an attacker trying to gain access to systems, or it could be done as a technique to hide malicious behavior in a constant stream of noise.

Let's look at a real-world example of how poor design and inconsistent navigation affect the team when they are constantly switching between tools or dashboards. For example, a separate set of tools will each have different colors, fonts, pages, and locations for alerts, event logs, profiles, and even historical context for threat intelligence. This can cause cognitive friction, meaning the sheer amount of effort required for a user to understand and interact with a product. For a security analyst, this could mean frustration, leading to errors from the extra mental energy to complete tasks, close tickets, react to alerts, or even respond quickly.

Errors and frustration could also arise from the lack of visual correlation between events and alerts, either within one tool or across multiple dashboards. As an example, a SIEM may fail to link events together that span time, systems, or users and system accounts. This means that analysts may have to piece together information and timelines with siloed information, potentially delaying the response to a critical event. On top of that, if the tool has poor search capabilities, it could take even more time to query logs or filter alerts with regex or SQL queries. With a high turnover of analysts who may or may not be proficient in the development of complex queries, this could create bottlenecks or other areas of frustration. This would only compound the turnover rate.

Visual and cognitive aspects

In cybersecurity roles, and SecOps specifically, teams review and interpret complex information about threats, detections, alerts, and communications. There are many SIEM solutions that do not consider cognitive psychology or the intricacies of visual ergonomics, relying solely on data and query languages to build patterns and understanding around massive piles of information. But more specifically, there are visual aspects to consider when selecting a tool or configuration for SecOps teams:

Use priority-based color schemes – red, yellow, green (where appropriate)
Adaptive grouping – tagging for environments and systems
Heatmaps – for threat intelligence, modeling, detections, and alerts
Timeline views and graphics – for overall security tool interfaces and alerts
MITRE ATT&CK framework overlays – tactics, techniques, and protocols against detections
Threats escalated visually – up arrows or red versus green

The preceding examples help to resolve potential visual cues that could turn a pile of incomprehensible data into a gold mine of coordinated streaming alerts. If SOC interfaces rely too much on red, yellow, and green, they could also miss using heat maps or other types of visual effects to give context to the colors. Without the proper hierarchical structure or sizing of data, vulnerability or threat scans may take additional time, again, slowing the analysts from being able to find true positive alerts.

Teams should be aware of progressive disclosures, affordances, temporal visualizations, and context versus causality. Progressive disclosure helps SOC analysts view summary-level indicators at a high-level, allowing the individual to choose when they want more details. As an example, a SIEM alert initially displays only the alert type, severity, affected host, and timestamp. When the analyst clicks on the alert, the interface expands to show process trees, network connections, and grouped alerts. Further expansion provides raw logs and packet data, including headers. This prevents the analyst from being overwhelmed with large volumes of data immediately while still allowing deeper investigation when the analyst chooses.

Affordances refer to design elements that indicate how a user should interact with a tool or interface. A SOC-relevant example would be a dashboard that displays a large Isolate Host button next to a compromised endpoint. The button is clearly labeled and placed near the host details, signaling to responders that containment can be initiated directly from that location. Design elements like this help to reduce the cognitive burden on analysts and reduce the amount of time searching for functionality.

Temporal visualizations are related to heatmaps or the timelines mentioned previously, giving teams specific views and pattern matching for change over time. A practical example of temporal visualization in a dashboard is a timeline including a phishing email received at 9:02 AM, with the malicious attachment executed at 9:04 AM, detailing credential access at 9:06 AM, and lateral movement began at 9:12 AM. This sequential timeline offers an analyst a different view of events to determine where containment should occur.

Context versus causality; when reviewed incorrectly, analysts could draw conclusions about a true positive versus a false positive, or read indicators incorrectly, leading to missed events or time wasted on non-events. Context refers to events occurring around the same time, while causality means one event directly causes another. Say an analyst notices that a server reboot occurred shortly after a malware alert. The timeline might suggest the malware caused the reboot, but further investigation reveals that the reboot was scheduled maintenance.

Color coding

Color coding is the most common mechanism for individuals to determine which alerts are most critical, versus others that can be ignored or maybe glossed over in favor of more severe detections that require investigations. One of the major challenges, however, is that most tooling uses a red, yellow, green system to indicate where and when to look at the alerts. A number of studies address the visual load of tools on cognitive load, including an article by Design Studio (https://www.designstudiouiux.com/blog/what-is-cognitive-load-in-ux/).

Prior research has found that when color coding is done incorrectly, it can lead to alert fatigue or misinterpretation of data, especially in a high-paced environment such as a SOC.

Some of the common challenges with color coding in a security environment are the overuse of red or yellow, leading to desensitization, a lack of keys or guidance, and showing only color indicators and lacking icons or any label for the data displayed. Simply because SOC analysts must look at many tools, there may be inconsistent use of color across modules or dashboards. For example, some SOAR tools may show red indicators for critical alerts, while others may show a heat map with colors that bleed from yellow, orange, and red depending on the location of the threat, not the threat itself.

With the race for security tooling in the market, some tools overly rely on animations or blinking, swirling colors to indicate data moving between a system and a SIEM. Some of these animations might be challenging for SOC analysts or distract from the true data and severity of the alerts. Any additional visual components could add to the stress or frustration of an analyst during an event or incident. But security tools that stick to three to five primary colors and use accessible color palettes and consistent mappings across views can help to ensure color coding does not cause unwarranted cognitive load.

Alert grouping

Alerts for a SOC team come from many sources, including log events, EDR alerts, network traffic and mappings, telemetry from threat intelligence sources, and even more. But when the data comes in either inconsistently or comes from independent and isolated sources, analysts may lack the connection between those data points. If alerts remain unstructured or uncoordinated, SecOps teams will have to manually correlate logs with behaviors, anomalies, and IOCs. Alert grouping is essential to SecOps teams to cluster related alerts into either one incident or one cyber event stream for analysis.

Without grouping alerts into proper sections or streams, analysts may be looking into the same incident or the same telemetry, but for different reasons. High or critical alerts may be misclassified or missed entirely simply because of the large volume of data coming in from multiple sources. As an example, consider a multi-cloud environment with two different event types coming in with the same EDR data across both. The alert and decision fatigue mentioned previously are exactly the cognitive implications that could happen without proper alert grouping.

Proper alert grouping, even across security tools, helps to reduce the overall volume of alerts and can even provide a reduction in duplicative efforts. Analysts can focus their attention on the alerts that matter, which provides them with the proper time to triage alerts more quickly and focus on high-fidelity patterns. Any tool or automation that lacks the ability to correlate a high volume of alerts will continue to be a stream of disparate information that analysts will have to review manually.

Interaction latency

A last comment on the design challenges of security tooling for SecOps teams: interaction latency. This is when there is a delay between a user input and when the system responds or provides an output back to the user. An example would be running either simple or complex queries against a SIEM to investigate single or grouped alerts. Because SOC teams must act swiftly on alerts, any latency in data response could reduce trust in the tools, increase frustration, or even break an analyst's mental flow during heightened investigation.

Some of the most common areas in tools where individuals may experience latency are when opening an alert panel, loading data between or across tools, running custom and complex queries, and switching sites or views. Each action could lead to delays in making decisions on whether to investigate an alert further or not, adding seconds or minutes to each detection. Each of these latency components could lead to a partial or even abandoned investigation, simply because analysts must move from detection to detection. But if teams implement a couple of strategic tooling configurations, they may be able to combat latency issues. Running caching for local alerts could provide quick reviews and loading data asynchronously in between investigations and optimizing load times for specific datasets are opportunities to build human factors into cybersecurity tooling.

SOPs and the pitfalls of structure and rigidity

On the process side of security operations teams, the tools, interfaces, and design of the technology that analysts and engineers use are not the only human factors considerations. SOPs, playbooks, runbooks, and policies help to support consistency and compliance requirements. But threats and detections don't always follow the standards and procedures that are outlined by either corporate policy or laws and regulations. More often, cyber events are unpredictable, constantly evolving, and while actors may use the same types of techniques, they may come at different times and use unique signals.

Standardization and uncertainty

In the world of cybersecurity, IOCs are constantly changing, threats evolve and leverage new techniques, and security tooling provides a backbone for consistency and support to SecOps teams. However, there is a balance between rigid policies and oversight, and the ability to be flexible and adapt to the changing technology and threat landscape. If policies and processes are too standardized, teams may suffer from missing situational context or using their experience and expertise to investigate alerts.

Analysts may be required to follow a set of playbooks or incident response procedures, leading them to make conclusions that may be inappropriate or blend threats and alerts together, missing the most important context of a situation. For example, if a SOC analyst must escalate an incident to an incident response team only when a true system compromise is noted, they may miss a possible system compromise that could be a precursor to a more sophisticated attack. Ransomware attacks are typically a set of innocuous and long-form steps before the system encryption scripts are ever dropped. Analysts may miss those signs if they only escalate when a true-positive occurs.

There is a common misconception about security in general being a "check box" exercise, especially when it comes to compliance. But other areas of cybersecurity groups could be required to follow steps in such a rigid manner that they may skip using their expert opinion or judgment in a cyber incident. If SecOps teams are forced to comply with SOPs or policies, they may delay responding to incidents that seem out of the norm or escalate when it is not necessary.

As with excessive or misplaced automation, SecOps teams could suffer from learned helplessness or even "checklist fatigue," constantly going through the motions of running through steps instead of using their expertise. If playbooks and runbooks are designed for older threats and not updated with the emerging threats and IOCs, analysts may respond in classic ways to threats, rather than evolving their processes to align with current and emerging threat trends. Automation can either assist or compound the challenges with process workflow, including relying too heavily on automation decisions, devaluing human insight and expertise, and relying on playbooks when threat actors may be able to subvert or avoid them.

Incorporating human factors for SecOps teams

Even with common challenges such as alert fatigue, dashboard overload, and cognitive workloads, human factors engineering design and principles can help detect the real problem areas for SecOps teams. It is not just the tools that we use, but how we use them in a cybersecurity context. The world of cyber threats and the techniques that are being used require us to be flexible, adaptive, and innovative in our approach. Consistency must be balanced with the ability to move quickly and pivot when threat actors change tactics and techniques.

Adaptive decision-making

If teams can adopt a more adaptive decision-making framework, security analysts will be able to dynamically review and respond when in highly stressful situations. Individuals will be given the autonomy to focus more on context using their expertise and collaboration with others, instead of following strict rules or processes. To support this, leadership must encourage innovation and deductive reasoning for decision-making, allowing a culture of growth and support before and during incidents.

For an adaptive mindset to be applied across the team, a few principles must be staked in the ground for this to be successful. Leadership should encourage teams to interpret signals and detections based on context clues or experience, provide elastic and flexible workflows or a process to update SOPs and policies in an iterative fashion, and encourage SOC teams to discuss and rethink playbooks as new threats emerge. A culture of collaboration can bring a SecOps team to an even higher level of maturity. This can also reduce burnout and turnover for analysts, giving them a voice and decision when it comes to how best to respond and build playbooks.

Human-centered analysis

Creating a human-centered analysis of the SecOps team comprises the alignment of processes, tools, and cognition. With a deep focus on cognitive strengths and limitations, reduction of fatigue in both decision-making and mental workloads, and a collaborative culture, analysts will be given opportunities to contribute and grow their skills within their team. Human factors engineering principles are not just focused on design and UX, it is more about building cognitive reasoning and understanding into an operational environment. A SecOps team's ability to prioritize tasking and make decisions quickly could be the difference between a small, contained incident and a much larger cyber event.

SecOps leadership should incorporate alignment of how humans process and connect data streams using context-based solutions and grouping of information. Flexibility is the means to success across tooling and processes, allowing updates and adaptability to changing threats and the explosion of AI and ML-based tools that threat actors are using today. The SOC is the first line of defense against attacks, and if we shift our focus from detections to human cognitive experience, teams may be more prepared to handle sophisticated and well-coordinated attacks that may evade common detection techniques.

With the integration and understanding of human cognition, the SecOps team moves from a reactive environment to a knowledge-based and resilient environment. Decision-making is the cornerstone of any successful integration of human factors engineering into SecOps teams, technology, and processes.

Summary

Inside every SecOps team, a quiet battle unfolds each day. Analysts stare at glowing dashboards filled with alerts, logs, and dashboards across multiple tools, trying to separate real threats from the routine ones and false positives. Every beep, color, and flashing indicator demands attention, but this can distract security analysts from the real cyber threats. This chapter looked beyond technology and into the minds of those behind the monitors, our front lines of defense, the SecOps team – the people who consistently and deftly navigate chaos, fatigue, and the constant hum of threat data. Here, human factors take center stage in solving some of our more complex cybersecurity operations challenges, including cognitive overload, bias, and balancing memory workloads. When a tool design or interface confuses rather than clarifies, detections or real threats can be missed, increasing risk.

Beyond the technical landscape, this chapter underscored the critical importance of culture, collaboration, and resilience within SecOps teams, regardless of the size of the organization. Psychological safety, communication dynamics, and shared situational awareness all influence how teams coordinate and recover from high-stress events. Through human factors engineering principles, organizations can design operations that reduce cognitive friction and encourage adaptive decision-making. Ultimately, the chapter argued that the future of SecOps lies not only in smarter tools but in systems intentionally engineered for the humans who defend them. In our next chapter, we will cover how vulnerability management teams and activities can be enhanced with human factors principles.
