5
Human Factors in Vulnerability Management

Unlike SecOps and incident response teams, vulnerability management is a skill that most technical teams require. SecOps teams focus on triaging alerts and determining true positive events, while incident responders take over when an incident has possibly occurred and requires further investigation. SecOps teams are at the core of any cybersecurity program; the firefighters and initial decision makers protecting an organization.

While vulnerability management is a core cybersecurity function, the tasking and remediation portions could be handled by system owners, developers, cyber engineers, or IT operators. Since vulnerabilities are released consistently across all levels of the technology stack, including the operating system (OS) layer, network equipment and devices, applications and tools, browsers, software as a service (SaaS) applications, and more, responsibility for addressing them often extends beyond a single role. Vulnerabilities could be considered on a wide-ranging scale, from low risk to zero days, with no patch or configuration available to remediate, leaving systems open to exploitation.

Since vulnerability management activities are handled across multiple teams, human factors engineering is a valuable component to add to all aspects of the Vulnerability Management (VM) lifecycle. To understand where cognition, bias, design, and risk scoring can be aligned to resolve complex vulnerability challenges, this chapter will cover the full VM lifecycle, what a typical VM analyst or engineer experiences, as well as the prioritization and remediation challenges experienced in VM activities. Understanding human factors in vulnerability management will lead to better remediation practices, faster time to remediation, and ultimately to focusing on the vulnerabilities that matter and reducing the extra noise.

In this chapter, you will cover the following topics:

VM lifecycle
A day in the life of a VM analyst
Vulnerability detection and mitigation
Cognitive demands on VM teams
Cybersecurity versus IT – friction
Prioritization and bias
Human-centered vulnerability management
VM lifecycle

The VM lifecycle is critical to all teams that perform patch management, secure configuration, continuous integration/continuous delivery (CI/CD), and risk management when it comes to what technology to update, decommission, or uplift. The VM lifecycle aids cybersecurity teams in starting with a framework from the identification of vulnerabilities all the way through remediation. Since each aspect of technology includes some type of VM activity, the VM lifecycle gives a comprehensive framework to follow to identify, assess, prioritize, remediate, and monitor vulnerabilities. Using the lifecycle offers teams a proactive vulnerability discovery approach, aligning resources strategically, and a more consistent and repeatable process for VM remediation. There are many iterations of the VM lifecycle, but this chapter follows the lifecycle shown in Figure 5.1:

Figure 5.1: VM lifecycle

Unfortunately, vulnerabilities are released at such a high rate that it can be difficult for any team to catch up and remediate everything. Using the lifecycle and with an eye on human factors, teams can understand exploitable vulnerabilities, pinpoint the most critical or exploited vulnerabilities, and automate workflows around remediation. For example, teams can leverage automation for patch management and discovery of rogue assets to cut down on the mountain of vulnerabilities and focus on the ones that matter the most.

Leveraging human factors within the VM lifecycle will help engineers select the vulnerabilities of the most importance with the benefit of context. For example, teams may be so overloaded with thousands or tens of thousands of vulnerabilities that it is impossible to remediate them all. With a more scientific approach, teams can build a repeatable process and reduce the noise of low-impact or no-exploit vulnerabilities. This reduces the risk profile of the network while also helping teams eliminate the possibility of burnout and spending too many hours solely on remediation activities that do not move the security needle. Exploring each step of the VM lifecycle will help to address the current state of vulnerability activities and how human factors can impact and mature each step.

Identification

Identification and keeping a robust inventory of assets, tools, applications, and third-party SaaS products is the first step to any VM process. Identification is the process of using either an inventory or vulnerability scanning tool, maybe even EDR tooling, to determine what assets exist on the network. Many teams struggle with this first step because of shadow AI, shadow IT, and other ephemeral assets, such as containers or serverless architecture, that require a variety of different discovery mechanisms. Shadow AI is the use of AI tools, LLMs, or other implementations without inventory or proper monitoring in place. Shadow IT is any IT system, network device, application, or tool that is implemented but not catalogued and monitored through regular inventory or detection capabilities. Because assets may change IP addresses, be brought online and offline at will, and engineers may be working on multiple environments without cataloguing assets, it can be very difficult to get a full and complete inventory picture of the environment.

To learn about vulnerabilities in far more depth, there are numerous resources available, including books titled Effective Vulnerability Management by Chris Hughes and Nikki Robinson, as well as Practical Vulnerability Management by Andrew Magnusson. To be able to assess and prioritize assets, there must be a clear picture of what assets are online versus offline, discovery of rogue assets or shadow IT, and any cloud accounts that are managed throughout an organization.

Asset discovery is the first step to ensure that as ephemeral assets (such as containers) come online, they are scanned, catalogued, and documented to understand possible risk. As an example, most system owners will suggest scanning only the image for a container, versus the container itself, so that they can resolve any issues with the image, which will be propagated to all the containers.

Assessment

After all assets have been catalogued, the assessment step includes running automated vulnerability scans, hopefully daily or every other day, if necessary. Assessment is the critical step to understanding what assets, vulnerabilities, and configurations matter the most to an organization. This step will look different at almost every organization because of varying security frameworks, regulations, or legal implications for data retention. Vulnerability scans are used to help understand the vulnerabilities, configurations, and compliance of all assets available for scanning. It is essential to run these scans consistently and automatically to find vulnerabilities quickly and to be able to move to the next step, prioritization, as vulnerabilities are released. These scans can be done by either on-premises or SaaS products, both cloud-based by the platform (examples: AWS Inspector) or purchased as a separate product (examples: Tenable, Wiz, or Qualys).

Vulnerabilities are not just common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVE) IDs hosted in the national vulnerability database (NVD), but they are also configuration issues, IAM settings, or overly permissioned accounts, as well as network settings such as open ports or external-facing assets. Building a comprehensive picture of assets means understanding which ones are most vulnerable, what they are connected to, and the overall risk to the environment. The more connected the systems, the higher the risk, and the larger the radius for exploitability across the network.

Prioritization

While every step of the VM lifecycle is important, prioritization can mean the difference between keeping up with vulnerabilities and potentially missing something that could lead to ransomware or other types of malware. Not every vulnerability is built the same, for example, a SQL injection vulnerability could mean direct access from an attacker to the SQL database. Whereas an overly permissioned AWS account could mean full account compromise if the account has been subjected to an account take over (ATO).

Prioritization means context – not simply understanding the common vulnerability scoring system (CVSS) score or the exploit predictability scoring system (EPSS), but what that system means to the business or the organization. For example, to prioritize effectively, teams need to understand the criticality of the system, business context, whether it is internal or external-facing, what applications are installed, and many other technical factors. As far as compliance, teams will also need to know what benchmarks they must meet, if they need to adhere to any federal or national regulations, and any specific technical controls required.

An internal-facing application server that only two or three people have access to will not rank high on the priority scale. However, an internet-facing application with business-critical data that has multiple critical, highly exploited vulnerabilities would be the top priority for remediation. Each decision when it comes to prioritizing vulnerabilities reduces risk, allows teams to focus on what matters, and ultimately encourages automation for the systems that can withstand updates.

Remediation

Remediation is the final step for fixing vulnerabilities. That could mean patching, resolving a misconfiguration, or moving an application behind a firewall, but full remediation is not always possible. At times, security engineers must get creative to protect assets if patching cannot be conducted. For example, there may be a legacy application for which no modern replacement exists, meaning that system must be protected with clever network segmentation and zero trust principles for account management. Even if a full fix is not available when a vulnerability is found, organizations can take action to protect assets while a patch or configuration is being created.

Remediation encompasses a wide variety of activities, including patching, configuration updates, segmentation of networks or applications, and even automation. For example, remediation for one system might mean actually applying a patch, but on another, it may be removing any external-facing access until the system can be fully patched, to avoid impacting business operations. Remediation is not just about adhering to whatever guidance is provided by a vendor, but may require creative solutions or compensating controls versus specific remediation steps.

With the massive increase in vulnerabilities that are found and remediated every day, organizations must focus on the vulnerabilities that matter the most. The previous step, prioritization, and remediation work hand in hand to ensure organizations focus on the protections that matter most. The first step for remediation is to automate wherever possible. Even if production systems cannot be patched automatically, dev and test environments should be patched automatically within a standard maintenance window. Setting expectations for users during maintenance windows, such as patching and configuration resolution, is just as important as the remediation itself. Without proper communication about downtimes or maintenance, users may be resistant to change or concerned about impacts, which could ultimately lead to change fatigue if too many changes are made at random times.

Continuous Monitoring (ConMon)

The ConMon step is the final piece of the VM lifecycle, and it also loops back to the first step, identification. ConMon can mean a lot of different tasks, processes, or tools that identify newly discovered assets, vulnerabilities, misconfigurations, servers, and applications, or cloud accounts that are not categorized or monitored by the vulnerability scanning process. But ConMon activities span across cybersecurity teams and are put directly in the hands of the IT, development, or engineering groups for remediation. ConMon is the act of continuously monitoring systems for vulnerabilities, configuration drift, or new security issues. ConMon activities are typically performed using vulnerability scanners, EDR tooling, and other operations tools for observability or configuration management (e.g., ServiceNow).

ConMon could be the action of scanning new vulnerabilities daily across the environment, or it could be the validation that patches and secure configurations remain in place. For example, setting up robust IAM processes initially in a cloud account does not mean they will stay in a secure manner for long. It is possible that processes break down, individuals are added to groups with heightened permissions, or account owners do not automatically remove users after a certain amount of time or when they are offboarded. Another common ConMon area is the consistent identification of rogue assets or shadow IT. Dynamic asset discovery should be done frequently, even hourly, to identify new assets as they come online to ensure they are hardened, contain a secure baseline, and have vulnerability monitoring tools installed, for example, EDR or Tenable agents.

A day in the life of a VM analyst

A VM analyst doesn't simply follow the VM lifecycle to the letter; it lays a solid foundation, but zero-day vulnerabilities, emerging technology, and threats, as well as advanced sophistication around tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs), require anyone who handles VM to be flexible and adaptable. As seen in the previous chapter, rigid processes and frameworks don't always align with a mature and resilient cybersecurity team. To understand some of the complexities in a VM program, let's dig deeper into the daily life of someone who manages VMs, whether that's an analyst, engineer, SRE, cloud expert, or developer. Alerts and reporting are essential to a VM analyst's workload to help prioritize, remediate, or identify new risks that require additional analysis.

Human factors impact a wide range of what occurs daily for anyone dealing with vulnerability management activities. For example, risk perception influences how analysts interpret vulnerability severity, sometimes leading them to underestimate or overestimate the real-world impact of a vulnerability. Decision-making and prioritization are critical human factors areas because vulnerability management programs often produce large volumes of findings that require analysts to determine what should be addressed first. Cognitive load and information overload can occur when analysts must review thousands of vulnerabilities, which can lead to missed issues or delayed remediation. Tool usability and interface design affect how easily analysts can interpret vulnerability scan results, understand context, and identify remediation steps.

Vulnerability alerts and reports

The bane of any vulnerability management team is reports. Whether for audit purposes, tracking remediation, or prioritizing vulnerabilities, reports must be created and analyzed. Depending on the organization, some VM teams will create and customize reports for IT and development teams. Reports could also be required for audits and assessments (internal or external), to fulfil technical control for either ConMon or inventory management requirements.

But the sheer volume of vulnerabilities could result in massive spreadsheets, reports, and metrics with little context. Solely looking at a list of vulnerabilities lacks context and only shows the number of vulnerabilities and their score or risk, not the potential impact on the environment. For example, an organization with 100,000 vulnerabilities across two cloud environments would be an incomprehensible list to review manually. Fortunately, with the advent of multiple open source and available large language models (LLMs), vulnerability reports can be reviewed quickly, and prioritization can be given based on a number of factors.

For example, an organization could build autonomous AI agents that inspect vulnerabilities, match them against inventories, and provide feedback as if they were a VM analyst to provide remediation actions, or even take actions themselves. Technologically speaking, organizations are on the verge of consuming and prioritizing vulnerabilities at far larger numbers at higher speeds, making those massive reports much easier to digest. But this is still a future state; currently, teams are still struggling with mountains of vulnerabilities and reports to prioritize remediation actions.

Alerts for vulnerabilities fall very much in the same category as what SOC analysts deal with, although VM analysts focus more on the remediation of the vulnerabilities than the attack patterns and TTP's associated with them. That is not an all-or-nothing statement; however, depending on the maturity of the VM program, they may use threat intelligence (TI) or OSINT to prioritize and focus on the vulnerabilities that matter the most. For example, healthcare organizations have been classically targeted by ransomware, so based on intelligence, they may choose to remediate exploited vulnerabilities associated with ransomware groups or threat actors (TAs).

Workflows

The backbone of any VM practice is its workflows. Workflows could be set up for several reasons, such as automatically sending reports to cloud engineering teams or submitting a ticket for a vulnerability found through a change management tool such as ServiceNow, to track remediation. Each grouping of automated tasks allows for a faster flow of information and up-to-date vulnerability remediation guidelines, and encourages speed when it comes to identification and prioritization. Getting vulnerability data to the right people quickly can help reduce the possible exploitability window. For example, there are usually very small windows of opportunity once vulnerabilities are publicly disclosed to remediate before TAs leverage those exploits to gain access to systems.

A few example workflows that may be seen across vulnerability management activities are sequential, state machine, rule-based, and parallel workflows. A sequential workflow is done in a linear order, one step at a time, which is the way vulnerability scans are done sequentially throughout systems, servers, and applications. A state machine workflow transitions between different states, just like when vulnerabilities are handled from discovery to closure, including states such as Open or Resolved. A rule-based workflow would be automated based on predefined rules, such as when vulnerabilities are prioritized based on CVSS scoring or specific conditions. Parallel workflows are when multiple tasks can be done at the same time, such as when multiple development teams need to work together on a series of vulnerabilities.

Regulatory or legal compliance

One of the other large factors in the prioritization and remediation of vulnerabilities includes any regulatory or compliance aspects. Some organizations may face federal cyber regulations, while others may need to comply with a specific framework to do business in a specific area. Each of these frameworks have their own technical controls, requirements, and expectations for everything from encryption mechanisms to data retention.

Whether organizations are complying with the payment card industry data security standard (PCI-DSS) for credit card transactions, the health insurance portability and accountability act (HIPAA) for healthcare data, or the general data protection regulation (GDPR) for the personal data of individuals who reside in the EU, there are sometimes unique and custom technical configurations that must be applied to be compliant. For example, for federal organizations or contractors following national institute of standards and technology (NIST) guidelines, they must follow the NIST 800-53r5 for patch management standards. Each of these regulations requires technological controls that must be validated during audits or assessments, and vulnerability management is common across all of them.

Human factors fit into the regulatory and compliance area of VM within policy interpretation, communication of compliance efforts, coordination between teams, and audit readiness. While many researchers and organizations consider security and compliance as different areas, they make parallel efforts to support the organization. Because of the vast array of policies and regulations, understanding and interpreting these controls quickly and correctly is essential to operational actions and remediation timelines. Communication and coordination between teams, whether with structured reports or to provide status, directly impact the risk posture of the organization and compliance with laws and regulations. And finally, audit readiness requires all components of VM to be aligned with logs, ticket trails, and record-keeping across all technical teams.

Timelines to remediate

Remediation is the benchmark for the success of a VM program, including human factors components such as expertise, behavior, and measurement to build better detections and ultimately make the best decisions based on risk. Most service-level agreements (SLAs) for VM frameworks require critical vulnerabilities to be remediated within 2-3 days, high vulnerabilities within seven days, and medium to low vulnerabilities within 30 days or more. Some of the most common metrics within remediation are mean time to remediate (MTTR), average time to action (ToA), vulnerability recurrence rate, and patch compliance. While these are not the only metrics that matter, these are some of the most interesting ones to follow for VM program health in a cybersecurity program.

MTTR is the amount of time to fix a vulnerability or exposure, where human factors concerns such as cognitive workload, delays with coordination between teams, and the qualitative quality of tickets and vulnerability details can impact the ability to resolve risks quickly. The time between the discovery of a vulnerability and assigning or determining the owner responsible for action could lead to alert fatigue and miscommunication between groups, potentially causing friction.

The amount of time between the public disclosure of a vulnerability and final remediation or mitigation could be delayed due to unclear ownership of assets or applications. Patch compliance, an important metric for a number of regulations, is the number of assets patched within an SLA, where it can feel like climbing a mountain that one can never reach the peak. All of those compounding factors could lead any individual (whether on the cybersecurity side or in development/IT) to frustration, misaligned goals, and possibly negatively impact risk scores. However, with better detection and mitigation strategies that include human factors engineering principles, organizations can work towards a more mature VM lifecycle.

Vulnerability detection and mitigation

Detecting vulnerabilities relies heavily on the prior steps of identification and assessment to be successful, and even then, detecting vulnerabilities is a moving target. Vulnerabilities may be upgraded in CVSS score, severity, or determined to be exploitable after they have been identified, sometimes weeks or even years later. Once detected, a strategy must be determined for remediation or mitigation – how to ultimately remove or reduce the risk of said vulnerabilities. Mitigation could mean many different activities, leading to additional data points, time required for analysis, and ultimately the decision to remediate or provide compensating controls.

Detection and mitigation activities are some of the most complex components within the VM lifecycle. Teams must be able to find all systems and applications, understand their risk posture, and determine what remediation is possible given a complex technology landscape. Whether it is infrastructure as code (IaC), multi-cloud environments, or a combination of on-premises and cloud infrastructure, developers and engineers could be juggling a lot of vulnerabilities. For example, developers may be responsible for container patching, OS-level (Linux or Windows) configurations, as well as networking within a cloud environment (either network devices or security groups within the account). To combat some of the complexities within detection and mitigation, cybersecurity teams can implement some understanding of cognition and design into their VM strategies. Cognition in this context is the amount that any one individual would need to understand about vulnerabilities, including the depth of knowledge required for remediation activities such as scoring, context, mitigation, or compensating control implementation.

A practical way to integrate human factors into this process is to design vulnerability dashboards that present information in a clear, prioritized format aligned with how we process information. For example, an organization might implement a dashboard that highlights vulnerabilities affecting critical systems using simple visual indicators, contextual risk explanations, and clear remediation steps rather than long lists of CVE entries. By reducing cognitive load and making prioritization easier to understand while juggling multiple priorities, analysts can more quickly identify the vulnerabilities that require immediate mitigation and coordinate with system owners to address them.

CVSS and EPSS scoring

CVSS is the common score given to vulnerabilities with a CVE ID hosted in the NVD. Over years of both academic and technical research, it has received some criticism for the lack of context that users see when they use it in their own tech stack. However, this should be considered a base score, something to start with. Otherwise, teams may be chasing an endless number of vulnerabilities and never feel like they are accomplishing risk reduction on any kind of large scale. CVSS is not intended to be used alone; it is an opportunity for teams to start with a number of pre-calculated metrics to help them focus on what matters.

For example, if teams solely focus on high and critical vulnerabilities, they may miss lower-scoring vulnerabilities that have a serious impact or could be used in chained vulnerability attacks, but without context, the vulnerability scoring could leave developers, engineers, and analysts stressed out, cognitively overloaded, and experiencing multiple levels of security fatigue. Since vulnerabilities come out at a rate of roughly 130 a day, according to Maze (https://mazehq.com/blog/2025-wrapped-the-year-vulnerabilities-broke-every-record), if teams must fix every vulnerability, it could become a full-time job, leading to the same burnout that SOC analysts feel with an overwhelming amount of tickets.

The EPSS offers a prioritization of exploitable vulnerabilities based on the likelihood or predictability that a vulnerability will be exploited, but even with this and the CISA known exploited vulnerability (KEV) catalog, teams may still feel underwater with vulnerabilities. If a team must comply with federal regulations to patch every vulnerability within a predefined window, teams will need to identify, resolve, and catalog every aspect of remediation. For full-time developers or cloud engineers who have other development or operational activities, without automation or an alternative way to remediate items quickly, they may be stuck with end-of-life or legacy tools and applications, piling onto their already full workloads.

Vulnerability scanning and dashboards

Scanning for vulnerabilities is the backbone of discovery and categorization, leading to prioritization and remediation steps in the VM lifecycle. While there are many scanning products on the market, this section will focus on human factors challenges of dashboards and scan results that could lead to cognitive overload and security fatigue. Without a good interface to interact with vulnerability data, teams may be stuck with massive spreadsheets without context or information to help prioritize remediation activities.

For example, an environment with over 20,000 vulnerabilities is required to comply with NIST controls in an environment with federal data. All vulnerabilities must be remediated within a thirty-day window or require a waiver or exemption while remediation is ongoing. If there are critical vulnerabilities that are related to legacy applications, the team must identify a way to either update or remove that software from the systems. If the application itself cannot be updated because of an End-of-Life (EoL) operating system, teams may be stuck in a loop of legacy applications until they can upgrade the OS. The amount of time spent on understanding, planning, and ultimately resolving those vulnerabilities means additional time not spent on the other 20,000 risks that sit open.

Dashboards, as seen in the two previous sections, can either be incredibly helpful or a massive burden on analysts and engineers. If a VM analyst solely works from a dashboard, the design and visualizations need to support the risk-based approach and remediation work the team is conducting. For example, if teams do not remove vulnerabilities using "exclusions," they may continue to see the same type of noise that a SOC analyst will see when triaging alerts. If the dashboard is cluttered with multiple tables, diagrams, and graphs, any analyst or engineer may find it difficult to parse through the vulnerabilities that matter.

A team should consider views that are either "Top Five" or "Top Ten" vulnerabilities as mattering the most. For example, a "Top Five Most Exploited Vulnerabilities" dashboard will help teams prioritize the vulnerabilities that could have a serious impact on their network. Another consideration is that teams may want to use urgency-driven hierarchical views, using font size and color to show which vulnerabilities are overdue. Context-aware filters are critical, showing the likelihood of exploitation, assets, and owners, whether external-facing or business criticality, to avoid multiple facets of fatigue and bias. A final important note, role-sensitive views offer a deeper level of context filtering, showing the data that matters to the individual roles or groups that the team cares about, reducing noise and distracting data.

Volume of vulnerabilities

According to a recent article (https://www.sentinelone.com/cybersecurity-101/cybersecurity/vulnerability-management-metrics/) by SentinelOne in 2025, almost 38,000 vulnerabilities were disclosed in 2024, with 2025 trending to be even higher. Since VM activities take place across an organization at a continuous, high-paced rate, teams must work together to identify who owns, responds to, and ultimately remediates vulnerabilities. Some of the challenges associated with that sheer number of vulnerabilities are that tools can vary dramatically, depending on what scoring they use, maybe leveraging their own proprietary risk scoring. In some cases, the scoring metrics are shared, helping teams understand the meaning, but in others, the scoring mechanism could lead to confusion or misconception around what vulnerabilities matter the most.

Some of the most common vulnerabilities, according to the 2025 Verizon DBIR Report (https://www.verizon.com/business/resources/reports/dbir/), are buffer overflows, overly permissioned accounts, cross-site scripting (XSS), SQL or command injections, and improper input validation. To compound these common weaknesses in systems, misconfigurations (such as an exposed network port) and unpatched EoL or legacy applications are the most common attack vectors. There is a wide range of vulnerabilities used to gain access to systems, and even if they are exploited in the wild, system owners really have minutes or hours to patch before they could possibly be exploited. This means, regardless of operational activities, workload, or project deadlines, all of these tasks must be dropped to patch or resolve critical zero-day vulnerabilities.

A common challenge for security teams is the sheer volume of vulnerabilities, either because of limited staffing, inconsistent tools or scores across products, or lack of context for the vulnerabilities that matter the most. VM teams may miss critical issues, miscalculate or misclassify vulnerabilities and risks, or delay remediation because of misunderstanding simply because of the volume of vulnerability details. A subject that the co-author of this book, Nikki Robinson, has written about extensively is that teams may ignore or overlook vulnerabilities scored as low or medium simply because of their score, even if they are exploited in the wild. With a heightened focus on high and critical vulnerabilities because of regulations or guidance, teams may miss the vulnerabilities that are on internet-facing or exposed systems that contain business-critical data.

To integrate human factors techniques directly into the compounding list of overwhelming data, consider grouping vulnerabilities by affected system, exploit availability, and business impact, and the dashboard may highlight the "Top 10 remediation actions this week" rather than thousands of individual findings. This reduces cognitive load and helps teams focus on manageable tasks. One real-world example is organizations that integrate vulnerability scanners with asset criticality and threat intelligence to automatically reduce thousands of findings into a few dozen high-risk remediation tickets for patch teams.

Cognitive demands on VM teams

Overwhelmed with piles of vulnerabilities, strict remediation guidance, and small technical teams to conduct manual activities, it's no stretch that VM teams are feeling the stress. VM teams experience heavy demand on their cognitive functions due to working memory load, automation bias, decision fatigue, and potential impacts on morale and motivation to resolve all the vulnerabilities. One example of automation bias in the context of VM teams is whether patching for production systems should be automated. Yes, automated patching would reduce the amount of manual inputs and mental workload, but it could have a serious business impact if patches are corrupted or cause undesired outages or availability issues.

With these aspects in mind, human factors engineering principles may drive to the root of the problem. VM teams are under cognitive and psychological burden to identify, prioritize, and remediate vulnerabilities and misconfigurations quickly.

Some interesting vulnerability metrics that are compounding the challenges internally for cybersecurity teams include the average amount of vulnerabilities seen per asset in an enterprise, at around six (Peer Benchmarking Dashboard, 2025, https://source.whitehatsec.com/help/sentinel/secops/dashboards-peer-benchmarking.html#:~:text=Across%20all%20industries%2C%20the%20average,this%20customer%20a%20fruitful%20target). The number of critical vulnerabilities that were exploited within the last month after a patch was released, as of 2023 data available, was over 40% (Mandiant 2024 Report, https://services.google.com/fh/files/misc/m-trends-2024.pdf). Just with these two statistics, VM teams could be struggling to constantly patch and resolve vulnerabilities solely to protect them from exploitation, not even addressing the other hundreds or thousands of vulnerabilities that could be leveraged in a vulnerability chaining attack. The 2023 report from the Ponemon Institute found that the average length of time to patch critical vulnerabilities could range from 60 to 120 days, meaning that teams are struggling with either technical or organizational challenges to remediate vulnerabilities.

Blame culture

Blame culture was addressed in previous sections, such as with the IR teams who may build a blame-focused after-action report (AAR) versus focusing on the impacts on the organization and what can be done to fix them in the future. However, the impacts of blame culture on vulnerability management are more on the possible impact to production systems with patching or configuration changes, the reduction or lack of reduction of risk in the environment, or who conducts remediation activities. For example, a VM team may spend time trying to identify system or application owners, who point the finger at others for remediation activities, leading to possible blame on the VM team for ineffective remediation.

The same can occur in VM teams, where a missed vulnerability during a scheduled scan or an unresolved patch could result in negative actions taken by leadership. For example, if a team is subject to federal regulations and fails to patch systems within the remediation window, they could be at risk of losing a contract, where leadership may provide disciplinary actions, including performance improvement plans (PIPs), writing an individual up, or even termination if it impacts the contract. If teams are subjected to a blame culture versus an organization that aims to gain an understanding of the complex VM landscape, engineers and analysts may suppress reporting, react with risk avoidance, and ultimately lose trust in either their cybersecurity teams or individuals who are responsible for VM actions.

Individuals may fail to report vulnerabilities or even misconfigurations that are overdue because of punishment, being written up, being put on a PIP, or embarrassment related to other team members. Risk aversion could occur when team members avoid making updates or changes because of possibly introducing new vulnerabilities, facing operational issues or downtime, or additional technical debt being discovered during the process of vulnerability identification. Ultimately, when teams lose trust in either the procedures or their teammates, they may continue to hide vulnerabilities or even refuse to work with others and share information, leading to hidden risks and exposures.

But building a comprehensive engineering discipline and complementary design solution for VM analysts can help to avoid a blame culture. For example, after an incident or during an investigation into how a vulnerability was missed, teams should ask questions such as "What technical or procedural controls led to a possible missed vulnerability?" Investigations should focus on problem statement creation, building on where the challenges exist, whether process-based, budget, resource management, or possibly because of conflicting guidance. If teams must remediate everything but also manage operational and functional components of systems, they may have competing goals to make sure the systems are online following an SLA uptime, versus improving the security of their systems.

Psychological safety

Psychological safety is another human factors component that traverses multiple groups within cybersecurity, including VM teams. But safety looks different depending on the role, processes, and tools that individuals use. For example, VM teams are under different types of pressure for the remediation of vulnerabilities quickly across IT and engineering teams based on regulations, while SOC analysts are hyper-focused on triaging and resolving alerts and detections, such as isolating systems with malware installed. From a VM analysis perspective, if individuals fear reporting missed vulnerabilities, re-prioritizing remediation activities, or sharing potential gaps in knowledge, teams could be prone to higher amounts of errors or missed findings.

The definition of psychological safety in this context is that teams can feel confident that they will not be punished or publicly shamed. For a VM team, that is a foundational component to identify vulnerabilities, share their findings, and ultimately make the best decisions to reduce risk. For example, say a junior VM analyst is nervous to share findings about a vulnerability that may not match the classic criteria for a high or critical exploitable risk, even if they feel it is the right thing to do based on their experience. If evidence is not shared of high or critical vulnerabilities, and remediation is delayed, if the vulnerability is exploited, that team will be directly responsible for the outcomes, whether full system compromise or a data breach. It is also possible that cross-functional teams that work together to reduce risk may not share findings because of prior disagreements or dealing with a blame culture, leading to unremediated vulnerabilities and increased risk.

To avoid potential psychological safety issues, organizations can implement a number of strategies across teams. The first is to normalize uncertainty; there should be no expectation that every technical individual is an expert in vulnerability management in depth. Security teams can help to support technical teams by prioritizing the vulnerabilities that matter the most, providing context, and ultimately helping make decisions for mitigation or remediation. Retrospectives should be open and honest, removing the blame and finger-pointing, focusing solely on what happened, not to punish or shame individuals. Finally, rewarding the practice of asking thoughtful and insightful questions, whether they agree or disagree with each other, allows for open discussion. Psychological safety is a critical component of the VM lifecycle, but without understanding how individuals are motivated, we are only solving a part of the human factors challenge.

Motivation theory

Not commonly explored within cybersecurity topics, motivation theory is the understanding of what motivates individuals to drive certain behaviors. Unfortunately, it can be easy to slip into mandates or procedures that force remediation or use fear-based incentives, such as being fined or fired, to force a specific action. Some of these tactics may provide a short-term motivator, but ultimately can lead to all the issues listed previously in Psychological safety, such as the use of a blame culture.

For VM teams to be successful, motivation is an inherent and important function to understand. Because of the massive piles of vulnerabilities, it can be increasingly easy for individuals to ignore or even delay patching and other remediation activities. To provide a more specific example, self-determination theory (SDT) provides a comprehensive framework for understanding what motivates individuals based on three drivers that could be leveraged across VM activities (developers, IT teams, and cybersecurity groups).

Instead of framing remediation purely as compliance work, leadership can introduce positive reinforcement mechanisms such as team recognition, internal leaderboards, or performance metrics tied to remediation goals. Consider a practical scenario where a company integrated vulnerability remediation metrics into engineering performance dashboards and highlighted teams that achieved rapid patch turnaround on critical vulnerabilities. This created a sense of ownership and competition that increased engagement with the vulnerability management process. Teams that are rewarded for tasks that may otherwise be repetitive and mundane can help encourage remediation activities as a positive action, versus a compliance checkbox activity.

Cybersecurity versus IT – friction

The areas of responsibility can sometimes be muddy for remediation activities; therefore, IT and cybersecurity teams tend to be at odds, which can often cause friction. This friction leads to missed deadlines for remediation, disagreements on the path to resolution, and ultimately a lack of cohesion between teams. One of the most pivotal areas of divergence between technical teams is their main mission. A cybersecurity team cares about the amount of risk on systems and, ultimately, about reducing that risk. A development team is focused on speed, meeting customer requirements, and building products and applications that work well for their systems, and IT teams, whether networking, cloud engineering, or SRE-focused, are dedicated to the uptime and operations of those systems.

These goals can be conflicting, leading to frustration, confusion, and misaligned priorities. For example, a cybersecurity team that requires four days to resolve critical vulnerabilities may find themselves at odds with IT teams who have once-a-week maintenance windows, meaning they will always be behind on vulnerabilities, leading to tension. These teams may end up arguing, not trusting each other, or getting frustrated with the constant communication on delays and missed vulnerability remediation dates, but human factors can help to understand the root of the issue. These teams have individual priorities, incentive structures, and goals that may be misaligned. To understand both teams more in depth, we will cover how both IT and cybersecurity teams, in a general sense, experience vulnerability management activities, including roles, responsibilities, and mission.

Information technology view

Whether running large multi-cloud environments, performing IAM, network engineering, infrastructure, or operations activities, IT teams are typically juggling multiple environments, levels of software, applications, and technology stacks. IT professionals are measured on availability, stability, and the most common metric, uptime. Usually, uptime is dictated in an SLA, such as 99% or 99.999%, within a contract to ensure that systems remain online and expectations are set for the consumers or customers. IT teams' focus will be on enhancing resiliency, building new systems for projects or architectural changes, and reducing operational or manual tasks.

The largest difference between teams is how they define and describe risk. A major risk to IT teams is if systems go down because of patching, upgrades, or any other disruption to service or operations. An unplanned outage could mean loss of trust in the team, possible disciplinary action, or even the loss of a contract if it continues to happen frequently. A change or a patch without testing thoroughly could mean a major liability for the team. The infrastructure or area of responsibility (AOR) for the technical stack means that any patches or upgrades will need to be done in a development environment and tested thoroughly, adding time to remediation for any patches or vulnerabilities.

Developer view

In our modern world, development is the bread and butter of most organizations that are building or shipping products. Given the speed at which teams are developing and pushing code to production, vulnerability management is one of the most important components of their work. Shipping a product that has inherent vulnerabilities or has not been scanned could put an organization at serious risk. Developers may or may not also manage the infrastructure that hosts their tools and applications, meaning they may be DevOps or DevSecOps teams, operating and maintaining a number of different containers, OS levels, and applications.

Developers are inherently focused on their pipelines, repositories, processes, and ultimately the requirements for whatever product or application they are tasked with. They may have short deadlines, multiple open source libraries and tools, as well as infrastructure to manage if they are part of a DevOps team. Depending on the team structure, developers may not only be responsible for patching and managing their applications, but also the containers or cloud environment as part of the infrastructure. The concept of DevOps encourages collaboration between software developers and the IT teams that manage operating systems, containers, and cloud services, but that may not always be the case. If the teams are small, developers may end up holding responsibility for all components, meaning they must patch and manage from the infrastructure through the applications.

In contrast to IT teams, developers view risk as any slowness or impediment to their timelines, development work, or security tasking. If teams must bake security into their applications or use a DevSecOps approach, they could perceive this as a negative impact on the timelines of the project. For example, teams who are working on building AI or agentic approaches would need to build in guardrails for prompt injection before they can push to production. Security tools such as vulnerability scanners, whether static application security testing (SAST) or dynamic application security testing (DAST), could mean delays in development because they must focus on remediation instead of quickly building and deploying.

Cybersecurity view

Finally, let us discuss cybersecurity and cybersecurity teams' unique perspective. Whether on a red team or blue team, managing threat intelligence (TI) or threat hunting (TH) missions, or defending networks against misconfigurations and vulnerabilities, cybersecurity teams have a very different view on risk. Risk to a cybersecurity team means gaps or holes in the walls that protect the organization. Sometimes, technical teams consider cybersecurity teams impediments or blockers for productivity, but cybersecurity professionals simply want to protect the organization. If systems are down, applications or accounts are compromised. Cybersecurity teams are just as concerned with the availability of systems as they are with integrity and confidentiality (the common CIA triad).

Risk is everywhere that a cybersecurity analyst looks, from unpatched systems to misconfigured APIs, and insecure code being pushed to production applications. But security tools, blocks, network segmentation, patches, and other applications of controls could be seen as conflicting with IT and development efforts. That can lead to tension, friction, and frustration when attempting to implement those controls across environments. Even using a user-centered design within security tooling will not resolve the tension or disagreements between teams; this one is purely psychological and requires some innovative thinking. Human factors is composed of engineering, design, and psychology principles. As part of the design part of HFE, design thinking can provide a meaningful vehicle for open discussion and creative problem-solving for tough cybersecurity challenges.

When bridging the variety of missions, roles, and responsibilities, as well as the different technologies and drives of IT, developers, and security teams, it's easy to see how human factors can integrate to resolve common vulnerability challenges. Even if each team is driven by very different motivations, there are many mechanisms to help teams collaborate, understand each other's priorities, and focus on the prioritization of vulnerabilities.

Prioritization and bias

Vulnerability management may seem relatively straightforward: find a vulnerability and patch or apply the proper configuration. However, due to the overwhelming flood of new vulnerabilities, reliance on old or legacy technologies, and overly complicated cloud and development environments, it isn't quite that simple. Finding, assessing, and resolving vulnerabilities becomes more challenging when teams must add business context, competing goals, and project deadlines, as well as limitations in tools and automation. Outside of the technical challenges, human factors can get to the root of the issues, including prioritization and various types of bias.

Cognitive load, availability bias, anchoring, and groupthink are all common human factors components that can impact VM actions. With increased cognitive load, teams may look for shortcuts or overly rely on CVSS scores or automation versus using contextual risk scores. Availability bias could make recent or critical vulnerabilities look more urgent, even if the systems are behind firewalls, not externally facing, or maybe even only apply to one specific version of a product. Anchoring is when severity rankings from various scanners and VM tools influence decisions, whether context exists in the scoring or not, and groupthink, an issue that occurs when teams are stressed and under serious pressure to remediate vulnerabilities, and make poor decisions by leaving in legacy applications instead of ripping them out and replacing them with new infrastructure.

To integrate human factors into regular VM activities, they can start by implementing structured prioritization frameworks that combine vulnerability severity with asset criticality, exploit availability, and business impact. This reduces reliance on individual judgment and prevents analysts from focusing only on familiar systems or high-profile vulnerabilities. Next, organizations can use standardized triage workflows and checklists that guide analysts through consistent evaluation steps, helping to reduce inconsistent decision-making across teams. Third, training programs should focus on cognitive bias awareness to aid analysts in recognizing common biases, such as anchoring on CVSS scores or recent incidents, and encourage more balanced decision-making.

Risk fatigue

Like alert fatigue with security analysts in a SOC, risk fatigue means feeling overwhelmed and conditioned to think that every risk is not really a risk, meaning that nothing is urgent or important. The difference with risk fatigue is that it is not simply a cybersecurity problem; it could impact any team that has to deal with VM assessments, prioritization, and remediation. Teams that are exposed to critical and highly exploitable vulnerabilities repeatedly lead individuals to experience desensitization and burnout. If everything is critical, nothing is critical. Since vulnerabilities come out at such a high rate, analysts and engineers may feel that they are constantly firefighting with no real perceived benefit or progress.

Risk fatigue can also lead to decision paralysis. When there are far too many competing priorities and risks, teams may be stuck in deciding the best course of action for prioritizing vulnerability remediation. For example, using tools that only say critical, high, medium, or low, leveraging language that is tiered or hierarchical, including user impact, likelihood of exploitation, and a countdown for remediation timelines, could help reduce risk fatigue. Without a feedback loop of information, teams may not realize the impact of their actions on the environment. For example, if teams continue patching their systems every month without any indication that they are reducing risk, they may feel as though their actions are futile. One consideration would be to show vulnerability reports as "patches applied" versus "vulnerabilities remaining." Shifting the visualization from a negative to a positive can help teams see the impact they're having on risk reduction efforts.

Fear-based response

Fear is a common and natural human reaction, but in a cybersecurity context, it can impact judgment and decision-making. If IT, development, and security teams are concerned about being blamed for missed patches, being in the news for a data breach, or regulatory compliance fees and impacts, they may make decisions that hide potential risks. Decisions made for the short-term perception of remediation could lead to higher risk overall, and actually lead to a data breach, making those worst fears come true. But the fear of technical teams could be shown via multiple avenues, displayed in fear of failure, blame, or living in a culture where it is not tolerated to make mistakes.

Fear of failure could lead security teams to push overly restrictive controls or force systems online for a perceived risk, versus validating that the risk exists. Fear of blame could be a developer not vocalizing EoL or end of support (EoS) concerns, leading to possible exploitation of vulnerabilities or even loss of a customer when they find massive security vulnerabilities. Finally, when a culture of fear takes over a team or organization, people may hide old technology (shadow IT) rather than discuss and fix legacy applications. This culture of fear could have other impacts that ultimately lead to system compromise, credential theft, or a data breach. Implementing blameless postmortems and psychological safety practices could be the first step to combating fear.

Contextual risk scoring

Scoring systems such as CVSS and EPSS were created to help prioritize the remediation of vulnerabilities, misconfigurations, and other risks due to possible threats and exploitation. Those scores attempt to quantify risk as a baseline, but more often, technical teams use them as the backbone for prioritization, missing business context, interpretation of risk, and limitations within the environment. Risk scores are subject to a number of human factors concerns, including poor decision-making and misaligned priorities across teams, leading to the tension and friction mentioned previously. Scoring systems can suffer from a static view of risk, a lack of context, and over-reliance on the same scores, possibly missing the real risks in the environment.

For example, if teams only focus on a CVSS score, they may miss lower-scoring vulnerabilities on systems even though they are internet-facing and could be exploitable or lead to vulnerability chaining attacks. If cybersecurity and technical teams do not question the score or add threat context from threat intelligence, teams may not see the most important risks. As another example, healthcare systems have classically been associated with a higher threat of ransomware, so those cybersecurity teams should not solely focus on the critical and high vulnerabilities but also follow the MITRE ATT&CK framework to see which attack chains are associated with advanced persistent threat (APT) groups to determine which risks are most critical.

Static vulnerability and risk scores could come with other human factors complications, such as anchoring bias, misalignment of mental models, and leveraging automation in incorrect ways. Anchoring bias is when individuals rely too heavily on the first piece of information presented to them, meaning that technical professionals could see a vulnerability score from a security tool and use that as the basis of remediation without context. When IT, development, and cybersecurity teams have various goals and perspectives, they leverage different mental models that could be misaligned easily. Finally, when teams use too much automation solely based on CVSS or EPSS, teams could miss blind spots in risks that are considered low, without understanding their impact on systems. For example, a SQL injection vulnerability may be considered a low for a variety of metrics within a risk score, but it could be critical for a web application that is internet-facing, and the backend SQL database contains other vulnerabilities.

Human-centered vulnerability management

So, how can vulnerability management teams experience a more contextual-based prioritization and remediation VM program? Through a clear understanding of context and organizational risk, an understanding of psychological safety and motivation, as well as the empathy to understand that each team is focused on the same thing, the success and security of the organization. When security teams are struggling with data quality, security tool overload, and issues with fear-based responses and trust, teams can leverage human factors insights to determine the root cause of scoring, risk, and threat concerns.

Human input and automation

The topic of automation will continue to be an important component of cybersecurity teams and the intersection of human factors: a delicate balance between automating manual tasks for reduced human error and burnout, but too much, and it could set individuals into a desensitized loop. Automation should not be considered a singular activity, to automate patching for a development environment, for example. When automated patching is enabled, a continuous monitoring process should be put in place to evaluate that the automation is working as intended, that it continues to meet the initial requirements, and that it does not need to be tuned or enhanced over time.

If a development environment is patched automatically at the OS layer, but never for applications, tools, or libraries, this could still lead to manual intervention and the same types of frustration. Automation should be leveraged in a maturity model, adding more automation in patching when systems are tested and validated to be resilient. But that is not the only continuous monitoring step for automation; does it still need to be automated? Could the team instead remove unused or outdated libraries? Automation should not be a static yes or no, but an evaluation of why it is needed, what it is used for, and ultimately, whether there is another, simpler option that would improve security.

Cross-team feedback loops

The importance of collaboration between teams cannot be overstated. The ability to share honest comments within a trusted feedback loop between teams can mean the overall success or failure of a VM mission. When developers can share the stress or frustration of security controls, and security teams can be open-minded to find creative solutions, it is possible to find a resolution quickly and efficiently. On the other side, if security teams can be trusted partners for IT and engineering teams, they can reduce risk and be an ally when challenging technical problems occur.

Role-based dashboards

Specifically for vulnerability scanning and reporting, dashboards are not a one-size-fits-all solution for IT, operations, developers, and security professionals. Developers should be able to see vulnerabilities specific to their repositories and code, clear remediation paths that include secure code options, integration into their own tooling, and secure baseline support from trusted code bases. IT and operations teams need something different, a dashboard that shows exposure and impact, dependencies, or impact, such as rebooting a system to apply a patch, and trends over time for risk reduction. The view means everything; it could mean looking at patches applied to improve morale, versus looking at a massive pile of unremediated vulnerabilities.

However, cybersecurity teams also need a specialized view. Simply looking at misconfigured systems or insecure code will not give them the holistic security view they require. Cybersecurity professionals will want to see TI feeds, continuous monitoring reports, attack paths, and impact, as well as possible exploitable vulnerabilities. Teams will need to be able to see the organizational assets and risks across the enterprise, while balancing business context and where to focus the most. Simply viewing 20,000 vulnerabilities will not aid remediation efforts, but a dashboard with the most critical risks, the most valuable assets, and overdue vulnerabilities will be a helpful start. Human-centered vulnerability management focuses on aligning teams and focusing on collaboration, visibility, and empathy for how other teams operate, and aligning automation and tooling.

For example, a large enterprise using a vulnerability scanning platform may generate 40,000–60,000 findings each week. Instead of presenting the full dataset to all users, the organization can create separate dashboards for vulnerability analysts, patch management teams, and leadership. The vulnerability analyst dashboard would focus on triage and investigation, displaying a limited amount of data based on exploit availability, asset criticality, and external exposure. The interface might highlight "Top Critical Vulnerabilities Requiring Analyst Validation" with visual indicators and provide quick access to exploit intelligence, affected assets, and recommended remediation guidance. The patch management dashboard would instead show grouped remediation tasks such as "Windows Servers-Critical Patch Required on 12 Systems," allowing administrators to focus on actionable patching work rather than vulnerability scoring. Security leaders would only see the information most pertinent to them: their business unit, organization, or section of the business.

Vulnerability validation

Validation of vulnerabilities focuses on the deep understanding that VM is about reducing risk, not just fixing every vulnerability. With the increased number of vulnerabilities released daily, it is important to focus on the vulnerabilities that have the most impact. If teams continue to try to remediate everything, they will be plagued by false positive detections, a lack of context, and noise from detections that could lead to wasted time and effort. But with human factors insights, teams can build a trustworthy and repeatable process to identify, assess, and remediate vulnerabilities quickly. Any team responsible for VM may experience knowledge silos, cognitive load, and a lack of trust of both tooling and teammates.

However, with this awareness, VM teams can focus on exploitable vulnerabilities, validation playbooks, and automation to build step-by-step automatic remediation and workflows that operate between teams. Validation playbooks help teams to ensure they are focused on the right vulnerabilities, workflows provide automation from identification to remediation, and team collaboration hinges on highly effective communication channels. Removing siloes between teams will build better trust and relationships, which will ultimately lead to lower risk and faster remediation.

Summary

In this chapter, we explored how vulnerability management is as much a human system as it is a technical one. We started with the reality that security teams are flooded with vulnerabilities and misconfigurations, and yet the real challenge cyber professionals face is how people perceive, prioritize, and act on that information. We reviewed the cognitive load placed on analysts who must constantly triage noisy data, the impact of bias on decision-making, such as over-prioritizing "shiny" critical CVEs or recent high-profile vulnerabilities over lower-profile or medium-profile vulnerabilities, which may be highly exploitable. When roles, workflows, incentives, and communication paths are misaligned with the actual risk landscape, exposures and threats continue to grow.

We then examined how to redesign vulnerability management to be human-centered instead of tool-centered. That included building clearer mental models for teams, including helping people understand not just what is vulnerable, but what happens if this is exploited in our environment. We discussed making risk more tangible and contextual through better visualization, language, and storytelling: translating raw scan outputs into narratives that matter to system owners and executives and using dashboards that support decision-making instead of overwhelming it. The chapter also covered how to create healthier workflows between security, IT, and business stakeholders, from more thoughtful ticketing and escalation paths to feedback loops that prevent the same issues from being overlooked or missed.
