6
Human Factors Engineering-as-a-Service

Human Factors Engineering-as-a-Service (HFEaaS) in cybersecurity provides an innovative and scalable mechanism for integrating human factors engineering and human-centered design expertise to address persistent human-element challenges such as burnout, fatigue, human error, and alert fatigue. A central premise of HFEaaS is that organizations reduce risk more effectively when leaders intentionally design security systems to align with real human capabilities and limitations. This alignment reduces predictable errors and friction while enhancing safety, performance, and resilience. HFEaaS operationalizes this premise by embedding usability, cognition, and behavioral considerations into security tooling and workflows, thereby reducing vulnerabilities created by decision fatigue, security fatigue, and human–system misalignment. Through on-demand access to human factors engineers, organizations can incorporate human factors principles, cognitive science insights, and user-centered methods into system development lifecycles without maintaining a dedicated in-house HFE team. This approach is especially salient in high-stakes domains such as cybersecurity, healthcare, and artificial intelligence, where the gap between system complexity and human cognitive or physical capacity can quickly translate into operational failure. HFEaaS further relies on iterative design and evaluation to minimize human-centered friction, strengthen trust in security controls, and enhance operational resilience.

Despite HFE's relevance to cybersecurity risk reduction, implementation has lagged behind the field's technical investments. Human Factors Engineering (HFE), originating in the 1940s and grounded in experimental psychology, is therefore essential in cybersecurity to reduce human-driven risk, empower people as active defenders, and deliberately design cyber environments that align with human constraints, cognitive functions, and behavioral tendencies in ways that enhance end-user performance and secure behavior (Nobles, 2019). When organizations deploy non-intuitive security technologies or restrictive controls that do not fit real work, friction increases between users and security mechanisms (for example, authentication, access requests, and secure-sharing workflows). As a result, compliance with required policies declines (such as MFA use, least-privilege access, and data-handling procedures), and users adopt workarounds (for example, password reuse, credential sharing, bypassing approved tools, or shifting work into unsanctioned applications) that undermine security objectives. Human error arising from poor interface design, cognitive stress, and insufficient training remains a major driver of breaches and disruptions, with some estimates attributing roughly 90% of incidents to human-related factors (Hill, 2020).

HFEaaS addresses this gap by supplying a structured, interdisciplinary capability that many security teams lack, particularly given limited exposure to HFE principles and the scarcity of human factors specialists, psychologists, and cognitive scientists within cybersecurity functions (Nobles & Robinson, 2024). In addition, because conventional awareness efforts often yield limited durable behavior change (Coffey, 2017), HFEaaS positions expert consultation, modeling, and evaluation as a practical pathway to embed human behavior and decision-making into system design, training, and operational processes. Although HFEaaS remains a novel, emergent model, it offers organizations a practical mechanism to access human factors practitioners and specialized expertise without having to build and sustain that capability internally. By enabling systematic integration of human factors expertise into cyber operations, HFEaaS supports the engineering of security environments that protect people by design rather than relying on end users to compensate for flawed or misaligned controls.

In this chapter, the following four topic areas will be covered:

Scope and impact of the HFE talent shortage
What is as-a-service?
What is HFEaaS?
The differentiation of HFEaaS
The positive and negative aspects of HFEaaS
The business imperatives of HFEaaS
Use cases for HFEaaS in cybersecurity
Driving behavioral change through HFEaaS
HFEaaS in artificial intelligence in cybersecurity
The distant future of HFEaaS
Scope and impact of the HFE talent shortage

The cybersecurity sector faces a pronounced imbalance between the demand for specialists in human factors, psychology, behavioral sciences, cognitive ergonomics, task design, usability testing, and human error analysis, and the available talent pool, resulting in critical operational shortcomings and increased organizational risk. While human factors practitioners, psychologists, behavioral analysts, industrial engineers, and cognitive scientists are distinct professions, we will refer to this group collectively as human factors practitioners to highlight the talent gap in cybersecurity. Not only is the lack of human factors practitioners working in cybersecurity concerning, but the omission of human factors expertise also exacerbates the issues surrounding human-related challenges and friction.

The lack of HFE expertise often leads organizations to overly rely on security tools and controls that introduce excessive complexity, create non-intuitive workflows, and erode user trust, thereby compromising usability and increasing risk exposure. The following examples show how these issues manifest in real-world cybersecurity contexts:

Excessive complexity:
Authentication burden: Users must manage multiple logins, frequent MFA prompts, and short session timeouts across different systems, which encourages password reuse, approval fatigue, and delayed updates to reduce interruptions. HFE would have prevented this by designing authentication around real task flow and cognitive load, consolidating sign-on where feasible, tuning re-authentication to risk, and using humane session management that preserves security without imposing constant attentional switching.
Overengineered security steps: Secure file transfer requires several approvals and manual classification steps for routine sharing, prompting staff to send attachments via email or use unsanctioned cloud tools to meet deadlines. HFE would have prevented this by mapping routine sharing workflows, reducing unnecessary steps, automating low-risk classifications, and designing a "secure-by-default" transfer experience that meets deadlines without pushing users toward workarounds.
Non-intuitive workflows:
Access provisioning friction: The access-request process requires navigating unfamiliar forms with unclear terminology and no status visibility, leading teams to share credentials "temporarily" or request overly broad permissions to avoid repeated delays. HFE would have prevented this by applying user-centered form design, plain-language permissions, guided requests, transparent status tracking, and progressive access that aligns with least privilege while minimizing repeat requests.
Incident reporting confusion: Employees cannot easily determine how to report a suspicious email or device behavior, so they forward messages to colleagues instead of using the reporting mechanism, delaying containment and reducing visibility. HFE would have prevented this by embedding reporting into the primary work surface (for example, a one-click "report" in email), providing clear prompts and feedback, and using recognition-based cues that reduce ambiguity at the moment of action.
Diminished user trust:
Alert and warning habituation: Frequent low-value security pop-ups and generic "suspicious activity" warnings train users to dismiss prompts without reading them, increasing the likelihood of approving a fraudulent request. HFE would have prevented this by designing for signal quality and attention management, reducing false alarms, prioritizing alerts by severity, and presenting actionable, specific messages that support correct decisions rather than conditioning dismissal.
False-positive fatigue in operations: Analysts repeatedly investigate benign alerts with limited context, leading to skepticism toward alerting systems and slower response when a true positive appears. HFE would have prevented this by improving alert usability and decision support, enriching alerts with context, establishing triage-oriented interfaces, and calibrating detection thresholds so that the analyst's time and trust are protected as scarce operational resources.

Security tools designed without incorporating human factors principles often generate adverse implications that compromise both usability and security resilience. In such systems, the cognitive demands placed on users are often misaligned with their actual capabilities, resulting in increased error rates, task fatigue, and diminished situational awareness. Rather than addressing these root causes through thoughtful design, many organizations adopt a reactive posture, prioritizing compliance enforcement and user training after errors occur, rather than proactively engineering systems that account for human strengths and limitations (Bada & Sasse, 2015 IBM Security, 2020). This approach not only fails to prevent recurrence but often exacerbates user frustration and disengagement.

Moreover, security mechanisms that are unintuitive or inconsistent with user mental models tend to be circumvented through informal workarounds or outright policy violations. Such behaviors are not mere indicators of negligence or organizationally induced; they reflect a systemic design failure in which security defenses, policies, processes, procedures, or tools obstruct rather than support secure behavior. These adverse outcomes are further intensified by a critical shortfall in human factors expertise within cybersecurity operations. Without human factors specialists capable of evaluating cognitive load, usability constraints, and behavioral feedback, organizations struggle to implement user-centered design principles that are foundational to robust security architectures. Consequently, the absence of human factors integration not only perpetuates technical vulnerability but also erodes user trust and operational effectiveness across the cybersecurity landscape.

Without embedded human factors expertise, security teams often struggle to understand how cognitive processes, behavioral tendencies, and human factors principles influence interactions between users and technical controls, rendering defensive architectures brittle and prone to human-induced failures. HFEaaS could bridge this talent gap by providing on-demand access to specialized practitioners, such as psychologists, cognitive scientists, HFEs, and usability experts, who conduct cognitive workload analyses; cognitive, physical, and ergonomic assessments; and task-based usability testing. This approach enables organizations to shift from reactive error management to proactive human-centered design interventions. Furthermore, HFEaaS is based on bringing expertise and a scientific approach to drive an iterative methodology grounded in evidence-based human factors principles throughout all phases of cybersecurity operations, thereby reducing friction and organizational risk and vulnerabilities.

What is as-a-service?

The "as-a-service" model has become a popular approach for organizations looking to reduce upfront costs and simplify access to technology and expertise. Instead of making significant capital investments in infrastructure or in-house capabilities, businesses can pay for services as needed. This allows them to focus on their core operations while leveraging advanced tools and support from external providers. While this model encourages innovation and flexibility, it also presents challenges, including data privacy concerns, regulatory compliance, and the risk of becoming overly dependent on a single vendor. These issues require careful planning and clear strategies to address.

One emerging application of this model is HFEaaS, which gives organizations access to specialized human factors expertise without the need to hire full-time staff. HFEaaS can help design systems and processes that account for how people think and behave, reducing mistakes and improving performance. It offers a way to strengthen cybersecurity programs by making them more user-friendly and resilient to human limitations. Until human factors professionals become a standard part of cybersecurity teams, HFEaaS offers a practical and scalable solution for organizations seeking to address the human aspect of cybersecurity risk.

What is HFEaaS?

HFEaaS is an emerging concept that provides organizations with access to human factors practitioners to help enhance institutions' cybersecurity operations. HFEaaS is a strategic approach that provides on-demand, scalable access to human factors experts, integrating ergonomic assessments, cognitive workload analyses, and user-centered methodologies into cybersecurity workflows without the need for permanent in-house teams. Embedding HFE as a core design principle enables the anticipation of human limitations, the reduction of system vulnerabilities, and the transition from reactive to proactive cybersecurity strategies in an increasingly complex threat landscape. Rather than treating human error as an unpredictable risk, HFEaaS enables organizations to proactively monitor, analyze, and optimize human-system interaction across the enterprise.

As a new conceptual framework, HFEaaS aims to integrate human-centered design and human factors principles into the overall cybersecurity ecosystem, encompassing policy guidance, data-driven decision-making, technology decisions, human performance (including fatigue, stress, and burnout), automation, and employee experience, among others. HFEaaS operationalizes the integration of human factors knowledge and concepts into high-stakes situations through systematic, scalable, and purposeful methods by utilizing approaches from cognitive psychology, behavioral science, and usability engineering. This method reframes human interaction as a crucial and flexible layer of system resilience rather than just as a source of mistakes. Usability tests, interface redesigns, cognitive burden improvements, and iterative feedback systems that support corporate objectives are all standard features of HFEaaS solutions. HFEaaS offers a proactive approach to overcoming human limitations and weaknesses through scientific human factors methods, as cyber threats increasingly exploit human vulnerabilities.

The differentiation of HFEaaS

People who are familiar with IaaS, SaaS, and PaaS often expect on-demand access, scalability, pay-as-you-go pricing, and self-service. HFEaaS can be on-demand and modular, but it is typically not self-service because human factors outcomes depend on practitioner-led methods and interpretation rather than a purely automated interface. HFEaaS should also not be framed as eliminating the need for skilled staff, since organizations still need capable teams to implement and operationalize changes; instead, it provides scalable access to specialized HFE expertise and repeatable methods that internal teams can apply across the system lifecycle.

HFEaaS presents an integrative framework that centers on empirical, scientific evaluation of human–system interaction, differentiating it from conventional as-a-Service (aaS) models that emphasize system health through operational performance indicators such as error logs, uptime metrics, and aggregate usage statistics. HFEaaS prioritizes human factors evidence, such as task complexity, workload demands, performance variability, and recurrent cognitive error patterns, to diagnose how design features, workflow constraints, and environmental conditions shape security-relevant behavior and decision-making. By foregrounding this data, HFEaaS enables behaviorally informed and context-specific interventions, including the redesign of interfaces and workflows, modifications to decision support, and adjustments to procedural controls to reduce predictable error pathways. This methodological shift reframes human input not as a primary source of system instability but as a modifiable and supportable component of socio-technical resilience, thereby establishing human performance as an engineered capability that can be strengthened through deliberate design, targeted training, and continuous operational feedback.

HFEaaS creates operational environments that prevent propagation mistakes and maximize user engagement under pressure by integrating behavioral science, based on cognitive load theory, decision heuristics, and stress-performance relationships, directly into technical workflows. Organizations can reframe human capability as a flexible yet crucial asset to reliability thanks to its emphasis on iterative usability engineering, ergonomic system design, and dynamic feedback loops. This is especially true in high-stakes, complex domains such as cybersecurity, aviation, and critical infrastructure. In doing so, HFEaaS systematizes the improvement of human performance as a foundation for risk mitigation, security, adaptability, and strategic value in sociotechnical ecosystems, broadening the scope of conventional service delivery.

Table 6.1 illustrates the differences between the new HFEaaS paradigm and established "as-a-service" models, such as Software-as-a-Service, Infrastructure-as-a-Service, and Artificial Intelligence-as-a-Service. By directly integrating human factors principles into cybersecurity operations, HFEaaS focuses on optimizing human-system interaction, whereas standard "as-a-service" models concentrate on delivering technological functionality through tools, platforms, and scalable infrastructure. HFEaaS focuses on decision-makers, analysts, and operators who are responsible for performance in high-stakes situations, rather than just end users and technical teams:

Feature

	

Traditional aaS (e.g., SaaS, IaaS, AIaaS)

	

HFEaaS




Primary Focus

	

Technology delivery and functionality

	

Human factors principles are integrated into cybersecurity operations to optimize the human fit, aligning with and achieving desired behaviors




Service Output

	

Tools, platforms, infrastructure

	

Human factors expertise to reduce friction around the human element in cybersecurity, cognitive workload analysis, design recommendations, and training




Target Beneficiaries

	

End users and technical teams

	

Organizational executives, cybersecurity professionals, analysts, operators, and decision-makers in high-stakes systems




Error Mitigation Strategy

	

Automation, redundancy, and scalability

	

Intentional design, behavioral adaptation, cognitive load reduction, and UI/UX rework




Data Use

	

Logs, usage metrics, performance benchmarks

	

Human factors risk assessment, human factors data, cybersecurity reporting data, help desk data, etc.




Operational Domains

	

IT, DevOps, Software Development

	

Cybersecurity and IT domains




Customization Approach

	

API integrations, configuration options

	

Tailored human-centered interventions based on organizational context

Table 6.1: HFEaaS versus traditional as-a-service models

HFEaaS uses deliberate system design, cognitive load management, behavioral adaptation, and UI/UX refinement to address vulnerabilities in human behavior, rather than the traditional emphasis on automation and redundancy for error mitigation. Data consumption also differs: HFEaaS includes contextualized user interaction data, such as help desk records and behavioral indicators, as well as human factors risk assessments, whereas traditional models prioritize performance metrics and logs. From an operational perspective, HFEaaS encompasses not only software development and IT but also cybersecurity, aviation, and other fields where human performance is essential to resilience and safety. Crucially, unlike the generic application programming interface settings typical of traditional "as-a-service" offerings, its customization approach emphasizes customized, human-centered interventions that correspond with the particular demands and behavioral dynamics of the business.

The positive and negative aspects of HFEaaS

HFEaaS has both positive and negative aspects because it functions as a socio-technical capability that can reduce risk when implemented rigorously, yet it can also introduce new dependencies, costs, and governance challenges if adopted superficially or without integration into cybersecurity operations. On the positive side, HFEaaS can enhance organizational resilience by providing on-demand expertise to redesign high-friction security workflows, reduce cognitive overload and alert fatigue, strengthen secure-by-default interactions, and improve training transfer through task- and context-relevant interventions.

On the negative side, HFEaaS may create implementation complexity through stakeholder coordination and change management, introduce vendor and data-handling risks, increase short-term operational friction during redesign and rollout, and produce limited benefits if leaders treat it as a stand-alone service rather than embedding it into governance, engineering lifecycles, and performance measurement. You need to understand this balance because it clarifies what HFEaaS includes, such as task and workflow analysis, usability evaluation, cognitive load assessment, interface and alert redesign, human-centered policy and training design, and continuous feedback mechanisms. You also need to understand this balance because it establishes the conditions under which HFEaaS reduces risk versus when it may shift risk or fail to scale. The subsections that follow define the HFEaaS concept and service components, describe implementation models and integration points across cybersecurity and development lifecycles, examine governance and measurement approaches, and conclude with practical considerations for sustaining value amid evolving threats, technologies, and organizational constraints.

The positive aspects of HFEaaS

HFEaaS provides human factors capability that enables organizations to embed human-centered design and evaluation into cybersecurity initiatives without maintaining dedicated in-house specialist teams. By simplifying interfaces and workflows at key decision points, HFEaaS reduces operational friction, strengthens usability, and lowers predictable human error while increasing system adoption and adherence to required security policies. HFEaaS also supports sustained secure behavior by addressing cognitive overload and burnout through alert rationalization, workload-aligned processes, and training and feedback mechanisms that reinforce secure actions within everyday work.

The subsections that follow describe the positive aspects of HFEaaS across four dimensions. Specifically, they address how HFEaaS provides scalable expertise without full-time staffing, enhances usability to reduce human error, supports secure behavior change through human-centered design of training and policy, and improves overall system adoption and policy compliance by aligning security mechanisms with user needs and organizational workflows.

Scalable expertise without full-time staffing

HFEaaS offers a scalable and flexible alternative to traditional staffing models, providing specialized expertise in HFE without requiring organizations to maintain in-house professionals. HFEaaS is essential due to the high cost of recruiting and retaining full-time staff with expertise in cognitive ergonomics, behavioral science, and usability engineering, which can be prohibitive, especially for small and medium-sized enterprises. HFEaaS offers a solution by making expert consultation and implementation support available on demand, thus enhancing organizational agility and responsiveness to emergent usability or security challenges. HFEaaS relies on a dedicated team of HFE consultants who provide expert support to the organization whenever needs arise. HFEaaS enables the cost-effective integration of human-centered principles into cybersecurity operations, eliminating the long-term financial commitments associated with full-time employment.

Enhanced usability and reduced human error

A primary strength of HFEaaS lies in its ability to enhance system usability and mitigate human error through the application of human-centered design and cognitive ergonomics. For example, enhanced usability in SOC operations occurs when a SIEM alert screen includes essential context and an obvious next step. For example, the alert view shows asset criticality, the user involved, a brief correlated timeline, and a one-click link to the correct playbook action, which reduces tool switching, speeds triage, and lowers missed true positives. Many cybersecurity breaches originate from human-system interaction failures, such as misconfigurations, weak authentication practices, and accidental data exposure. By embedding usability principles into system architecture, such as intuitive interface layouts, error-tolerant workflows, and user-friendly authentication processes, organizations can implement context-rich MFA, such as number-matching prompts that display the application, device, and location, to reduce accidental approvals. Organizations can streamline access by using role-based request bundles with automatic expiration to limit overprovisioning and reduce delays. Organizations can strengthen email defenses by adding phishing banners that identify the specific risk signal and provide a one-click option to report the message. HFEaaS reduces friction between users and security technologies. This alignment minimizes the cognitive burden on users, promoting smoother task execution and reducing the likelihood of security-compromising mistakes. The result is a more resilient cybersecurity posture built upon human-system compatibility, rather than reliance solely on technological countermeasures.

Support for secure behavior change

HFEaaS contributes significantly to promoting secure user behavior by incorporating psychological and behavioral insights into training and awareness programs. Traditional security training often fails to account for individual cognitive differences or learning preferences, resulting in limited behavioral change. In contrast, HFEaaS employs evidence-based methods, including microlearning, adaptive content delivery, and behavioral nudging, to facilitate informed decision-making. These interventions are designed to align with users' mental models and real-world contexts, thereby increasing both engagement and retention. By aligning training with how people process information and make decisions under stress or time constraints, HFEaaS improves the translation of knowledge into consistent, secure behavior, thereby strengthening the human layer of defense in cybersecurity ecosystems.

Improved system adoption and policy compliance

Systems and policies that reflect human-centered design principles are inherently more likely to be embraced by users. HFEaaS facilitates the creation of security solutions ranging from software interfaces to procedural guidelines that are intuitive, context-aware, and compatible with users' workflows. When users find systems easier to use and more relevant to their operational context, resistance to adoption decreases, and policy compliance increases. Moreover, well-designed systems reduce the temptation to circumvent security measures, which often occurs when controls are perceived as burdensome or obstructive. Thus, HFEaaS not only enhances the user experience but also reinforces compliance with organizational security protocols, resulting in more robust and sustainable cybersecurity outcomes.

Mitigation of cognitive overload and burnout

In high-intensity environments such as security operations centers (SOCs), analysts are frequently exposed to continuous alerts, rapid decision-making demands, and high cognitive workloads. HFEaaS addresses these challenges by introducing ergonomic solutions and cognitive support tools that help manage mental strain. HFEaaS addresses these challenges by introducing ergonomic solutions, such as optimized workstation layouts, adjustable displays, and fatigue-reducing shift and break design, and cognitive support tools, such as context-rich alerts, decision checklists, standardized incident playbooks, and just-in-time prompts that guide secure actions and reduce mental strain. Techniques such as interface simplification, intelligent task prioritization, and automation of routine operations enable analysts to maintain situational awareness without becoming overwhelmed. These enhancements reduce fatigue, decision fatigue, and burnout, which can compromise vigilance and increase the likelihood of errors. By optimizing the human-technology interaction in SOCs, HFEaaS fosters operational resilience and supports long-term workforce sustainability in cybersecurity functions.

HFEaaS offers several positive attributes that strengthen cybersecurity performance by reducing human-centered risk and improving how security systems function in real operational conditions:

Reduced human-centered friction: HFEaaS decreases cognitive, behavioral, and procedural barriers, enabling users and security teams to complete secure tasks with less confusion, delay, and unnecessary effort.
Stronger secure behavior: HFEaaS increases adherence to required security practices by aligning controls and workflows with real decision-making patterns, reinforcing secure habits through design rather than relying primarily on reminders or enforcement.
Lower error rates and greater resilience: HFEaaS improves reliability through clearer interfaces, improved task structure, and contextual decision support that prevents predictable mistakes and enables safe recovery when errors occur.
Expanded human factors capability: HFEaaS extends specialized expertise to organizations without dedicated internal HFE staffing, accelerating progress on persistent human-element risk drivers such as alert fatigue, misconfiguration, and unsafe workarounds.
Better sociotechnical fit: HFEaaS integrates technical controls with human compatibility, strengthening cross-functional collaboration and ensuring that security tools and processes perform effectively within everyday work environments.
Challenges with HFEaaS

HFEaaS adoption often stalls because many cybersecurity leaders prioritize technology-centric defenses and underestimate the role of HFE in reducing organizational risk, thereby limiting budget allocation and executive sponsorship. Integration can also prove difficult because HFEaaS requires cross-functional coordination to redesign workflows and embed human-centered practices into development lifecycles and security operations. Finally, organizations must manage privacy and governance risks associated with collecting user interaction data and relying on external providers, while also addressing the challenge of demonstrating return on investment with metrics that credibly link usability and behavior improvements to reduced incidents and lower operational disruption.

Limited awareness and organizational buy-in

HFEaaS continues to face notable barriers to organizational adoption, particularly within cybersecurity contexts that prioritize technology-centric defenses. Many decision-makers remain unaware of the strategic contributions HFE can offer in mitigating security risks. As a result, budget allocations often favor traditional safeguards such as network monitoring, encryption, and access control, while human-centric strategies are underfunded or overlooked. The limited awareness contributes to a narrow perception of cybersecurity that overlooks the underlying behavioral and cognitive vulnerabilities frequently exploited by adversaries. Building awareness through case studies, executive education, and empirical demonstrations of HFEaaS effectiveness is essential for advancing adoption across industries.

Integration complexity

Integrating HFEaaS into established cybersecurity operations requires cross-functional collaboration that is often difficult to achieve in practice. Coordination among departments such as information technology, human resources, compliance, and cybersecurity is necessary to embed HFE into secure system design and operational workflows. In organizations where silos are entrenched or communication pathways are underdeveloped, HFEaaS integration efforts may be fragmented or misaligned. Additionally, the absence of standardized integration protocols or governance frameworks can hinder the seamless implementation of HFEaaS principles into the software development lifecycle or risk management processes. Overcoming these challenges demands strategic alignment, leadership support, and a systems-thinking approach to change management.

Data sensitivity and privacy concerns

The application of HFEaaS often involves collecting sensitive data, including behavioral metrics, cognitive load indicators, and user interaction patterns. While such data enables more effective customization of cybersecurity interventions, it also raises serious ethical and legal considerations. Without appropriate safeguards, the collection and use of personal or biometric data can violate user privacy, lead to compliance violations, or erode trust among stakeholders. Ensuring transparency, informed consent, and adherence to regulatory standards such as the General Data Protection Regulation is crucial. Organizations must establish ethical data governance frameworks that balance innovation and individual rights, and clearly define how user data is handled, stored, and used in HFEaaS deployments.

Dependence on external providers

Reliance on third-party vendors to deliver HFEaaS introduces potential risks related to quality control, contextual relevance, and organizational continuity. Not all vendors possess the necessary cybersecurity domain expertise or an understanding of industry-specific regulatory environments. Misalignment between the service provider's approach and the organization's operational realities can lead to suboptimal outcomes. Furthermore, dependence on proprietary tools or frameworks may create vendor lock-in, limiting organizational flexibility and innovation. To mitigate these risks, organizations should establish transparent service-level agreements, invest in knowledge transfer, and prioritize partnerships with providers that offer adaptable and evidence-based HFEaaS solutions.

Underdeveloped metrics for return on investment

Quantifying the value of HFEaaS remains a persistent challenge due to the qualitative nature of many of its outcomes. While traditional cybersecurity investments can be measured by metrics such as threat detection rates or reduced response times, HFEaaS often yields improvements in areas such as user experience, cognitive efficiency, and behavioral compliance. These benefits are inherently more challenging to capture with standard performance indicators. The lack of widely accepted evaluation frameworks for human-centered interventions complicates efforts to justify long-term investments in them. To address this gap, future research should focus on developing validated metrics that correlate HFEaaS implementation with measurable improvements in cybersecurity posture, workforce performance, and organizational resilience.

HFEaaS offers clear organizational advantages, including reduced human-centered friction, fewer predictable errors and workarounds, improved adoption of security controls, and more sustainable performance in high-tempo environments through lower cognitive overload and burnout. At the same time, HFEaaS introduces practical challenges that leaders must manage, including integration complexity across functions, privacy and data-governance obligations, dependence on external providers, and the need to demonstrate return on investment with credible metrics that connect human-centered outcomes to reduced risk. Recognizing both the benefits and constraints positions HFEaaS as a business-relevant capability rather than a discretionary enhancement: it directly supports core imperatives such as enterprise risk reduction, operational continuity, regulatory readiness, workforce retention, and protection of customer and stakeholder trust. When organizations govern HFEaaS rigorously and embed it into cybersecurity and development lifecycles, they can convert the human element from a recurring source of incidents into a managed source of resilience that strengthens long-term business performance.

The business imperatives of HFEaaS

The adaptability of the HFEaaS model is a crucial feature that enables businesses to scale human factors interventions according to project requirements. To respond quickly to emerging challenges such as zero-day cybersecurity threats, urgent regulatory changes, and shifting user requirements, providers maintain interdisciplinary teams that can be rapidly redeployed across concurrent engagements. This operational agility facilitates iterative design and ongoing user feedback loops, where prototype testing and real-time behavioral analytics inform system improvements well in advance of full deployment. End-to-end development cycles thus become more effective and robust against usability problems that might otherwise materialize as expensive post-launch fixes.

In terms of finances, HFEaaS transforms the conventional capital-intensive investment in human factors knowledge into a variable operational expense model that lowers organizational risk and improves budget predictability. Organizations use HFEaaS suppliers on a use basis instead of hiring, training, and maintaining a permanent staff of usability engineers, cognitive psychologists, and ergonomists. Before investing more resources, this approach enables small-scale pilot studies of human-centered treatments, such as task analysis workshops or heuristic evaluations, to confirm their efficacy.

Another essential component of the HFEaaS value proposition is the ability to adapt designs rapidly. HFEaaS suppliers ensure that usability tests and UI changes occur within accelerated delivery cycles by coordinating human factors procedures with lean and agile development approaches. Teams can identify problems early in system prototypes by continuously integrating task performance data, behavior tracking, and user input. Targeted interventions, such as streamlining intricate operations, enhancing alert displays, or eliminating unnecessary cognitive steps in incident response playbooks, are informed by these insights. This kind of responsiveness enhances user satisfaction, strengthens the security posture, and improves operational efficiency.

HFEaaS's organized implementation approach usually consists of four stages, each supported by iterative improvement and the acquisition of empirical data. To identify performance bottlenecks and error-prone interactions, the evaluation phase employs methods such as process mapping, cognitive task analysis, and usability audits. Through adaptive interface design, security-by-design feature specification, and focused training modules, solutions are customized to fit the corporate context. To facilitate user acceptance, deployment incorporates these interventions into real-world settings, utilizing seamless security safeguards and ergonomic improvements. Successful methods are subsequently extended across units through scaling and iteration, which include continuous feedback loops and are driven by analytics to promote continuous improvements and the cultural acceptance of human-centered design concepts.

Applications of HFEaaS across various industries demonstrate its strategic value. By creating error-tolerant interfaces and cognitive support systems that prioritize important alerts, weed out unnecessary messages, and simplify job switching for analysts, HFEaaS improves incident response workflows in cybersecurity operations centers. By optimizing data entry workflows, reducing alert fatigue, and minimizing cognitive overload in high-stress clinical scenarios, service-based human factors interventions in electronic health record systems have significantly improved patient safety and clinician efficiency in the healthcare industry. Similar to this, ergonomic improvements and cockpit automation assessments aided by HFEaaS have enhanced pilot situational awareness, reduced workload during critical flight phases, and helped to lower the number of human error events in aviation quantifiably.

Additionally, HFEaaS provides a tactical advantage when navigating complex ethical and regulatory environments. Organizations can comply with strict usability criteria, as required by regulatory bodies for medical devices or for information systems under data protection frameworks, by incorporating systematic user feedback methods and iterative design processes. To avoid costly non-compliance penalties and strengthen company credibility, HFEaaS suppliers assist clients with recording human factors assessments, creating verifiable user testing records, and demonstrating conformance with industry standards.

Other advantages of implementing HFEaaS include improved customer satisfaction and competitive differentiation. By providing straightforward, dependable, and accessible experiences, human-centered design enhances stakeholder trust, reduces user frustration, and fosters user loyalty. Usability improvements have been linked in human factors research to increased market responsiveness, higher-quality results, and enhanced productivity across various industries. Businesses that adopt HFEaaS establish a culture of excellence in human-system integration and continuous development, establishing themselves as pioneers in operational resilience and user experience.

Use cases for HFEaaS in cybersecurity

This section presents a concise set of cybersecurity use cases that illustrate how HFEaaS reduces organizational risk by improving human–system interaction. Each use case briefly defines the operational problem, explains how HFEaaS is applied, and describes the resulting impact on security outcomes. The intent is to provide original, practical examples that demonstrate how HFEaaS strengthens human-centered cybersecurity and complements technical controls.

Use case 1: SOC operations and incident response

SOCs often struggle with alert overload, fragmented tooling, and high cognitive demand, which increases triage errors and slows response during time-sensitive events. HFEaaS applies task analysis, cognitive workload assessment, and interface redesign to prioritize actionable alerts, standardize escalation cues, and reduce tool switching by placing essential context and next steps at the point of decision. This improves analyst accuracy, shortens time-to-detect and time-to-respond, reduces alert fatigue, and lowers the likelihood that high-severity threats go unnoticed or uncontained.

Use case 2: usability of security tools and controls

Many identity, endpoint, and compliance tools deliver strong technical capability but impose non-intuitive workflows that users bypass when deadlines and friction collide. HFEaaS applies usability testing and heuristic evaluation across the lifecycle to simplify high-frequency interactions such as authentication, access requests, secure sharing, and policy acknowledgements, while introducing error-tolerant pathways such as clear confirmations, reversible actions, and context-rich prompts. The outcome is reduced workarounds, fewer user-driven misconfigurations, higher adoption of key controls, and improved adherence to required security procedures because secure actions become easier to execute correctly.

Use case 3: cybersecurity training and secure behavior

Many awareness programs increase exposure to content yet fail to produce durable behavior change because training is generic, detached from real tasks, and delivered without reinforcement in the flow of work. HFEaaS redesigns training around role-specific scenarios and decision points, using microlearning, realistic simulations, and just-in-time nudges to reinforce correct actions during routine workflows such as email handling, data sharing, and authentication. This approach increases retention and improves due to predictable decision errors.

Use case 4: secure software development lifecycle

Security vulnerabilities often persist because teams discover usability-driven failure points late, after deployment, when redesign becomes costly, and adoption becomes more difficult. HFEaaS embeds human-centered practices into secure design by participating in requirements definition, design sprints, prototyping, and iterative usability evaluation to identify where users will predictably misunderstand controls, misuse features, or create unsafe workarounds. This reduces post-deployment vulnerabilities, improves the usability of secure features, and strengthens resilience by ensuring that security requirements align with actual workflows and user cognition.

Use case 5: human performance, fatigue, and workforce sustainability

Cybersecurity teams frequently operate under sustained high workload, frequent interruptions, and high-consequence decision-making, which increases fatigue, burnout, and error risk, particularly during major incidents. HFEaaS applies workload assessment and operational ergonomics to support scheduling design, role clarity, handoff structure, and cognitive support tools such as checklists, decision aids, and standardized runbooks. These interventions reduce cognitive overload, improve reliability under stress, decrease turnover risk, and sustain operational readiness during prolonged threat activity.

Use case 6: human-centered root cause analysis

Incident investigations often focus on technical indicators and individual mistakes while missing the underlying human–system conditions that made failure likely, such as confusing interfaces, unclear policies, weak feedback, or ambiguous ownership. HFEaaS adds a structured human factors lens to post-incident review through error causality mapping, workflow reconstruction, and identification of latent conditions that shaped decisions before and during the event. This produces higher-quality corrective actions that address root causes, reduce recurrence, and improve organizational learning by converting "human error" into specific design and process changes.

Use case 7: human oversight in AI-enabled security automation

Security organizations increasingly deploy automation and AI for detection, triage, and response, but poorly designed human oversight can create overreliance, missed failures, and delayed intervention when automation behaves unexpectedly. HFEaaS applies human-in-the-loop design principles to define clear intervention points, transparency cues, and operator controls that support calibrated trust and rapid recovery when models misclassify or drift. The impact is improved decision quality, reduced automation surprise, stronger accountability, and more reliable outcomes from AI-enabled security operations.

Non-cyber domains and HFE

Healthcare, aviation, nuclear operations, and industrial safety have long used HFE to reduce error in high-risk environments by designing systems that anticipate human limitations and support reliable performance. Cybersecurity shares characteristics of complexity, time pressure, and consequences, which make these domains instructive. The core implication for this chapter is that cybersecurity can adopt comparable human-centered practices through HFEaaS to strengthen resilience and reduce organizational risk, while remaining focused on cybersecurity-specific workflows and controls.

Driving behavioral change through HFEaaS

Behavioral change is a foundational element in the pursuit of cybersecurity resilience because everyday human decisions and actions frequently create the conditions that adversaries exploit. HFEaaS offers a structured, human-centered approach to promoting secure behavior by embedding HFE principles into the design of security controls and the workflows in which those controls operate. Rather than relying primarily on training or policy reminders, HFEaaS reduces predictable error pathways by aligning systems with how people actually perceive risk, allocate attention, and make decisions under time pressure, interruption, and competing priorities.

In practice, HFEaaS shifts cybersecurity from a reactive, technology-centric posture to proactive risk reduction by designing secure behavior into routine work. For example, HFEaaS can redesign authentication and access approval experiences to reduce accidental approvals and rushed decisions through clearer prompts, context-rich verification, and error-tolerant steps that slow only the highest-risk actions. HFEaaS can also reduce cognitive overload in security operations by prioritizing actionable alerts, presenting essential context at the point of triage, and standardizing escalation cues so that analysts do not rely on memory or informal heuristics during fatigue. In end-user contexts, HFEaaS can strengthen resistance to phishing and social engineering by pairing scenario-based microlearning with just-in-time nudges, such as one-click reporting, specific risk explanations in email banners, and feedback loops that reinforce correct actions when they occur. These interventions convert "behavior change" from a training objective into an engineered outcome by making secure actions easier to execute correctly, harder to bypass, and more consistent across diverse roles and work environments.

A core aspect of fostering secure behavior involves addressing cognitive biases that impair users' ability to detect and respond to cyber threats. Biases such as inattentional blindness, decision fatigue, and overconfidence can diminish situational awareness and lead to risky choices. HFEaaS mitigates these risks through cognitive task analysis and user-centered interface design, simplifying interactions and reducing mental workload. Practical implementations include more intuitive authentication processes, visual threat indicators, and real-time guidance during security-critical tasks. These design improvements enhance user focus and decision-making, reducing the likelihood of errors and bolstering cybersecurity posture.

Training and education, though essential to behavioral change, often fall short when delivered through generic, static methods. HFEaaS introduces personalized, experiential learning environments that promote deeper engagement and knowledge retention. Interactive simulations, scenario-based exercises, and gamified modules are tailored to individual learning styles, helping users build practical skills in identifying phishing attempts, managing credentials, and responding to social engineering attacks. These immersive experiences reinforce secure behavior through active participation and positive reinforcement, leading to more durable and consistent cybersecurity habits across an organization.

Behavioral change in cybersecurity is gaining momentum, but what is surprising is that most organizations do not seek expertise in behavioral science to assist with such efforts. When it comes to changing behavior in cybersecurity, it should be intentional, scientific, customizable, and effective. One such model is the Capability, Opportunity, Motivation – Behavior (COM-B) model, which provides a simple perspective on behavior change, as shown in Figure 6.1.

The COM-B model provides a fundamental framework for comprehending behavior as a resultant attribute of three interrelated constructs: (a) capability, (b) opportunity, and (c) motivation (Willmott et al., 2021). This paradigm can be implemented through HFEaaS by human factors engineers to enhance behavioral interventions by systematically aligning system design with human limitations and traits, thereby optimizing engagement, adherence, performance, and outcomes. Refer to the following figure:

 

Figure 6.1: Basic COM-B model

 

Capability, characterized as the physical and psychological ability to execute an activity (Willmott et al., 2021), can be enhanced by HFEaaS by integrating cognitive support tools, task simplification techniques, and adaptable interfaces. HFEaaS solutions frequently address skill deficiencies, decision-making limitations, and attentional limitations through focused human factors design, thereby enhancing user preparedness and confidence in executing essential tasks.

Opportunity, which encompasses external physical and social facilitators of behavior (Willmott et al., 2021), is enhanced by HFE through the reconfiguration of the environment, both physical and digital, to reduce friction, eliminate contextual obstacles, and promote accessibility. HFEaaS establishes supportive environments that facilitate the attainment of desirable behaviors through ergonomic assessments, workflow analysis, and inclusive design strategies.

Motivation, encompassing reflective processes (such as goal-setting and planning) and automatic drivers (including emotional and habitual responses) (Willmott et al., 2021), can be shaped by HFEaaS via the implementation of behavioral nudges, persuasive design, and feedback mechanisms. By mitigating cognitive load, enhancing emotional engagement, and leveraging intrinsic reward processes, HFEaaS cultivates enduring motivation that aligns with company objectives or user intentions.

In this integrated approach, behavior is perceived not solely as an individual decision but as a systemic consequence influenced by the interaction of interventions informed by human factors. In practical fields such as cybersecurity, healthcare, and workplace safety, HFEaaS can implement the COM-B paradigm by integrating behavioral insights into tools, processes, and technologies, thereby enabling quantifiable enhancements in user behavior and system resilience.

The COM-B model can be integrated into HFE frameworks to assess how engineered improvements in capacity, opportunity, and incentive affect behavior, especially within intricate sociotechnical systems. HFE initiatives aim to enhance individual behavioral drivers and provide synergistic effects by intentionally manipulating motivational processes. Comprehending these dynamics is essential for creating adaptive, human-centered systems that correspond with strategic goals while reducing human error.

Social engineering remains one of the most challenging forms of cyberattacks to defend against, as it relies on human manipulation rather than system vulnerabilities. HFEaaS counters this threat by integrating principles from social psychology into training programs and system architecture. Techniques such as role-playing, phishing simulations, and contextual alerts empower users to recognize manipulation tactics and respond appropriately. By embedding social engineering defenses into daily workflows, HFEaaS enables a shift from reactive risk mitigation to proactive, user-informed cyber defense.

Sustained behavioral change requires an iterative design process supported by continuous feedback. Human factors engineers facilitate this by collecting behavioral data, identifying friction points in user interactions, and informing ongoing system enhancements. Security protocols, user interfaces, and feedback mechanisms are refined based on real-world usage patterns and user input. This dynamic process ensures that security systems evolve in tandem with user needs and emerging threats, creating a continuously improving alignment between human behavior and system performance.

Ultimately, the success of behavioral change initiatives depends on cultivating a culture of human-centered cybersecurity and placing humans as the central point and intentionally designing for them. This paradigm moves beyond technical compliance to embrace users as the essential participants, operators, and defenders in the cybersecurity ecosystem. HFEaaS operationalizes this shift by integrating behavioral science, system usability, and human-centered design into every layer of cybersecurity strategy. In doing so, it redefines resilience not only as a matter of technical capability but as the outcome of intentional, secure, and empowered human behavior.

HFEaaS in artificial intelligence in cybersecurity

HFEaaS is crucial for enhancing artificial intelligence (AI) in cybersecurity by integrating human-centered design into the development and operation of intelligent security systems. As AI becomes increasingly essential to cybersecurity tasks such as threat identification, behavioral analytics, and autonomous response, collaboration between human analysts and AI-driven solutions emerges as a crucial element in operational efficacy. HFEaaS provides organized, scalable access to human factors knowledge, ensuring that AI systems are designed with consideration for cognitive workload, situational awareness, and human interpretability. By customizing AI interfaces to align with the cognitive and behavioral characteristics of security professionals, HFEaaS enhances the quality of decision-making, mitigates alert fatigue, and accelerates incident response times.

Furthermore, HFEaaS enhances AI-driven cybersecurity by facilitating the development of explainable AI and reliable systems. Confidence in AI is crucial in high-stakes settings, such as SOCs, where human operators must swiftly evaluate and respond to AI-generated insights. HFEaaS employs heuristic evaluations, task analysis, and user feedback loops to guarantee that AI models deliver transparent, contextually rich outputs that correspond with human reasoning processes. This is especially crucial in addressing insider threats or intricate, multifaceted attacks because excessive dependence on obscure AI choices may result in overlooking or misinterpreting them. According to Galaits et al. (2022), the effective incorporation of AI into cybersecurity relies not only on the efficacy of the model but also on users' comprehension and trust in AI-generated recommendations.

Ultimately, HFEaaS promotes ongoing human-AI collaboration in cybersecurity settings by enabling adaptive system architecture and operational robustness. As cyber threats progress, the tools and methods utilized by defenders must also adapt. HFEaaS facilitates the iterative evaluation and reconfiguration of AI-cybersecurity interfaces, guaranteeing conformity with the evolving requirements of analysts and the changing strategies of adversaries. This dynamic flexibility improves the long-term sustainability of AI implementations by integrating feedback from practical applications into subsequent versions. Moreover, by mitigating human limits such as fatigue, attention span, and perceptual bias, HFEaaS facilitates the development of technologies that enhance human strengths rather than exacerbate vulnerabilities. The incorporation of human factors into AI-driven cybersecurity operations is not merely a technical requirement but a strategic asset for safeguarding digital infrastructure.

The distant future of HFEaaS

In an ideal scenario, the cybersecurity sector would acknowledge the significance of HFE and integrate it as a fundamental component of its defense strategy. However, in reality, HFE continues to progress slowly as a corporate cybersecurity priority. This is attributable not to irrelevance but to a deficiency in awareness. Cybersecurity has consistently faced a singular, enduring challenge: human behavior. At the convergence of humans and technology, HFEaaS emerges as a potent and timely solution.

The primary objective of HFEaaS is to avoid fostering long-term dependency. Instead, it serves as a strategic initiative to integrate essential human factors expertise into cybersecurity operations until the sector develops a sustainable and adequately qualified workforce of human factors specialists. Until the pipeline is built, the necessity for HFEaaS is both pressing and persuasive. It must address the persistent and frequently overwhelming challenges faced daily by end users, security analysts, and leadership teams. This friction results in inaccuracies, exhaustion, and heightened security vulnerabilities.

By integrating HFE principles and methodologies into cybersecurity frameworks, HFEaaS facilitates the development of systems that prioritize human needs rather than undermine them. It enables enterprises to develop behavioral frameworks that promote optimal user engagement, reduce cognitive load, and facilitate secure decision-making. HFEaaS facilitates the optimization of incident response systems, improves alert prioritizing, and creates more user-friendly security solutions, thereby fostering behaviors that correspond with security objectives and human capabilities.

The complete potential of HFEaaS in cybersecurity remains uncertain due to its recent emergence; however, its significance can be deduced by analyzing its effects in other high-risk sectors. HFE has consistently demonstrated its effectiveness in various domains, including healthcare, aviation, nuclear power, and industrial safety. These disciplines have long recognized the imperative to design in accordance with human capabilities and limits. Their efforts in HFE have led to enhanced operational safety, increased system resilience, and a substantial decrease in mistake rates.

Cybersecurity has the potential to achieve analogous advantages. HFEaaS is not merely a service; it is a conduit to a future in which security solutions are both technologically sophisticated and human-centric. It advocates for a paradigm shift in perception, recognizing individuals not as liabilities to be controlled, but as resources to be cultivated. By implementing HFEaaS, enterprises make a significant advancement in developing cybersecurity solutions that are both efficient and sustainable.

Summary

As the cybersecurity landscape becomes increasingly intricate and adversaries become more adaptable, the human element remains both the foremost vulnerability and the most underutilized advantage. HFEaaS is a revolutionary approach to realigning cybersecurity with human skills, behaviors, and requirements. It is neither a transient solution nor a superficial improvement. HFEaaS signifies a fundamental transformation in how businesses can implement human-centered design at scale, particularly in contexts where talent deficits, cognitive strain, and usability issues jeopardize both resilience and performance.

HFEaaS addresses a significant deficiency in cybersecurity strategy by providing an accessible, scalable, and systematic method for incorporating human factors knowledge in essential areas. It recognizes the pressing necessity to tackle the human-technology interface and presents a service-oriented approach that integrates empirical insights into the daily operations of security teams, developers, and decision-makers. HFEaaS establishes the foundation for more intuitive, safe, and efficient systems through focused interventions, continuous feedback loops, and rigorous user-centered analysis.

In conclusion, HFEaaS is not merely a service; it is a strategic necessity for any organization dedicated to developing cybersecurity technologies that complement rather than hinder user interaction. Investing in HFEaaS today enables firms to transition from reactive problem-solving to proactive performance design, changing the human element from a risk factor into a fundamental component of cyber resilience.

The next chapter, on addressing stress, fatigue, and burnout in cybersecurity, is essential because sustained cognitive overload and high-consequence decision-making degrade attention, judgment, and response reliability, thereby increasing organizational risk. Human-centered interventions, such as workload-aware staffing and scheduling, alert rationalization, clearer handoffs, and ergonomic decision supports, sustain performance during prolonged operations while improving retention and long-term resilience.
