9
Benefits of Human Factors Analysis and Classification System in Cybersecurity

Human-factors breakdowns are a significant contributor to adverse cybersecurity outcomes across sectors, frequently precipitating incidents, attacks, breaches, and errors. Human factors breakdowns are failures in the interaction among people, technology, tasks, and organizational processes that increase the risk of cybersecurity errors, incidents, and breaches. This concept emphasizes that many cybersecurity failures are not solely technical problems or individual mistakes but result from poorly aligned system conditions that shape human performance. One example is a phishing attack in which an employee clicks a malicious link while under time pressure and cognitive overload. Another example is cloud misconfiguration in which an administrator accidentally exposes data because security settings are confusing or difficult to navigate. In both cases, the breakdown reflects human-system design and organizational conditions, not only personal error.

To address this challenge, the Human Factors Analysis and Classification System (HFACS), grounded in Reason's barrier-based "Swiss-cheese" model of organizational accidents, offers a rigorous multi-level taxonomy for converting narrative incident accounts into analyzable causal structures. Reason's Swiss-cheese model of organizational accidents is shown in Figure 9.1:

Figure 9.1: Swiss-cheese model (Source: https://hfacs-healthcare.com/the-hfacs-framework/)

This model promotes a systems perspective, viewing human error not as the root cause but as a symptom of deeper organizational conditions. Within this framework, organizations implement multiple defensive barriers across technology, procedures, supervision, and governance. These barriers are structured in a sequential manner, with higher-level influences shaping lower-level behaviors. Failures at any level create vulnerabilities, either active, occurring close to the event, or latent, stemming from distant conditions that can align across layers to produce adverse outcomes. The Swiss Cheese model is applicable to cybersecurity by providing a methodology to understand how latent and active failures lead to catastrophic events in cybersecurity. A latent failure is a hidden system weakness, such as poor design, weak policy, or inadequate training, that creates conditions for error. An active failure is the immediate human error or unsafe action, such as clicking a malicious link or misconfiguring a system, that directly contributes to an incident. The Swiss Cheese model will be explained in more depth in the chapter.

This chapter provides a comprehensive review for applying HFACS to cybersecurity operations, including its definition, scope, origin, and theoretical foundations. It details the system's four-level structure and introduces an expanded, domain-specific treatment of layers and associated nanocodes tailored to digital environments such as security operations centers (SOCs), identity and access management (IAM), software delivery pipelines, cloud and operational technology (OT) systems, and higher-education enterprises. The application of HFACS enables consistent post-incident analysis, cross-case trend discovery, and targeted, system-level interventions, emphasizing prevention and a just culture over blame.

By the end of this chapter, readers will be able to explain how HFACS strengthens cybersecurity by providing a structured, evidence-based framework for identifying, classifying, and mitigating human, supervisory, and organizational contributors to cyber incidents. In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

What is HFACS?
The need for HFACS in cybersecurity
HFACS theoretical foundations
Error science
Enabling a blame-free culture
HFACS and risk management
Structure of HFACS: four cascading levels
Need for a modified HFACS-Cyber framework
What is HFACS?

HFACS is a human factors framework used to analyze how multiple layers of failure contribute to accidents by exploring the adverse outcomes. HFACS is a hierarchical, evidence-oriented taxonomy that classifies contributory factors to adverse events across four cascading levels:

Unsafe Acts (Example: An employee clicks a phishing link)
Preconditions for Unsafe Acts (Example: The employee is fatigued and managing too many tasks)
Supervisory Factors (Example: The team did not receive updated phishing training)
Organizational Influences (Example: Leadership underfunded security awareness and staffing)

The HFACS preserves causal coherence by requiring analysts to connect what a person directly did at the time of the incident to the immediate conditions that influenced that action, and then to the relevant supervisory and organizational drivers, rather than skipping levels and making unsupported causal claims. This structured analytic discipline converts complex incident narratives into consistent, comparable data, which helps identify latent conditions, recurring control weaknesses, and patterns of systemic drift over time (Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020 Perneger, 2005 Reason, 1990 Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022). For example, in a phishing-related credential compromise, the analyst would not stop at the employee clicking the malicious link. Using HFACS, the analysis would trace that action to immediate conditions such as cognitive overload and email ambiguity, then to supervisory factors such as outdated awareness training, and finally to organizational influences such as understaffing and weak investment in secure communication tools. This produces a more accurate explanation of the breach and a stronger basis for prevention.

Contemporary applications in cybersecurity extend the same logic to digital operations with high cognitive load, complex interfaces, and distributed authority structures (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022). Codified subcategories at the "sharp end," including skill-based errors, decision errors, perceptual errors, and procedural or exceptional violations, are analytically linked to preconditions such as fatigue and interface affordances, to supervisory practices such as planning assumptions and oversight bandwidth, and to organizational determinants such as resource allocation, climate, and process design. This principle protects explanatory integrity, supports inter-rater reliability, and reduces hindsight bias by binding each inference to contemporaneous artifacts such as change records, staffing rosters, and interface captures (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022).

The need for HFACS in cybersecurity

HFACS is needed in cybersecurity because incidents are rarely caused by a single user mistake or technical failure. It provides a structured way to examine what a person did during an incident, the immediate conditions that shaped that action, and the supervisory and organizational factors that made the event more likely. This prevents oversimplified explanations, reduces blame-focused analysis, and helps organizations identify deeper systemic weaknesses that repeatedly contribute to breaches. By organizing incident data in a consistent way, HFACS also makes it easier to compare cases, detect patterns, and design stronger prevention strategies across people, processes, and technology. Additionally, HFACS can be used to prevent further incidents by assessing the data and mitigating weaknesses in the defensive layers.

HFACS theoretical foundations

Safety-science syntheses further differentiate active failures at the operational "sharp end" from latent conditions embedded within supervision and organizational structures at the "blunt end" (Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020 Perneger, 2005), a distinction that contemporary expositions of the HFACS reinforce in favor of organizational learning over individual fault (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022). Defensive layers include technology configurations, procedural controls, training and staffing arrangements, supervisory oversight, and organizational governance.

Building on this architecture, HFACS translates the conceptual account into a practicable, evidence-driven taxonomy that preserves causal adjacency across four levels: (a) Unsafe Acts, (b) Preconditions for Unsafe Acts, (c) Supervisory Factors, and (d) Organizational Influences (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022). Adjacency requires disciplined tracing from observed actions to immediately adjacent preconditions, and then to supervisory and organizational contributors, without speculative tier-jumping directly from frontline behavior to board-level causation (Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020 Perneger, 2005).

Critiques of the Swiss-Model caution that frameworks such as HFACS should not be used as if they can identify one clear and final cause of an incident (Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020 Perneger, 2005). Instead, HFACS is better understood as an inquiry scaffold, or a structured way to guide analysis across different parts of a system. Think of it as an investigative process to uncover fundamental causes of a significant data breach or cyber attack at different levels in the organization. This is important in cybersecurity because incidents rarely result from a single error. More often, they emerge from a combination of technical, human, supervisory, and organizational factors that interact over time. When used in this way, HFACS helps analysts ask better questions, look beyond blame, and identify patterns of weakness across the system. This leads to stronger root-cause analysis and more effective corrective actions. In cybersecurity, the Swiss Cheese Model is especially useful because it shows how breaches can occur when multiple layers of defense fail at the same time. Rather than blaming one user mistake or one failed control, the model encourages analysts to examine how factors such as workload pressure, misaligned workflows, weak supervision, policy drift, and under-resourced security controls combine to create a path to harm. This systems-based view is highly relevant in cybersecurity settings, where alert saturation, configuration debt, unclear shared-responsibility models, and release schedules that move faster than risk checks can increase the chance that several defenses will fail together rather than separately (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022 Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020 Perneger, 2005). A socio-technical interpretation extends the barrier model by recognizing joint causation across people, processes, technologies, and organizational contexts. Under this lens, Unsafe Acts frequently reflect reasonable local adaptations to workload and tooling constraints; Preconditions often encode cognitive load, handoff frictions, and interface affordances. Supervisory Factors capture planning assumptions, exception management, and oversight bandwidth. Organizational Influences capture resourcing, climate, and governance patterns (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022). The HFACS linkage of each stratum to concrete evidence converts narrative incident accounts into analyzable structures that expose patterns of latent conditions and inform system-level interventions (Perneger, 2005).

Applying HFACS in cybersecurity requires a practical approach that focuses on what should be measured, how evidence should be analyzed, and how lessons learned should be used to strengthen the system (Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020 Perneger, 2005 Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022). First, analysis should extend beyond technical controls alone and also examine human and organizational factors, such as workload, usability, training, communication handoffs, and governance. Second, investigators should trace incidents step by step across system levels rather than jumping too quickly to high-level conclusions, making sure to show how unsafe actions connect to preconditions, supervisory issues, and organizational influences. Third, HFACS should support continuous learning by turning recurring patterns into corrective action, such as redesigning controls, updating runbooks, adjusting staffing, strengthening incentives, and improving vendor oversight. In this way, HFACS becomes not just a classification tool, but a structured method for understanding and reducing cybersecurity risk.

In summary, Reason's barrier theory supplies the causal architecture for understanding organizational accidents (Reason, 1990), and the Human Factors Analysis and Classification System supplies the operational discipline for evidentiary analysis and prevention (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022). Together, these perspectives elevate analysis from person-centric error narratives to socio-technical causation (Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020 Perneger, 2005), preserve rigor through causal adjacency, and enable prevention by revealing modifiable latent conditions across supervision and organizational governance. In cybersecurity, this error-science foundation is especially important because incidents that appear to result from isolated user actions often emerge from deeper interactions among workload, tool design, supervisory practices, and organizational controls.

Error science

Error science is the systematic, interdisciplinary study of human, organizational, and system errors, focusing on their causes, patterns, and prevention mechanisms (Reason, 1990; Dekker, 2014 Hollnagel, 2017). It seeks to explain why errors occur, how they propagate through complex sociotechnical systems, and how their consequences can be mitigated or eliminated through design, training, and policy interventions (Reason, 1990; Hollnagel, 2017). By integrating multiple disciplines, error science provides a structured framework to shift attention from individual blame to systemic understanding, thereby supporting proactive strategies to enhance safety and performance.

Error science examines human error as a predictable and analyzable phenomenon rather than a random or purely individual failure (Reason, 1990 Dekker, 2014). The field integrates human factors engineering, cognitive psychology, systems engineering, safety science, and organizational behavior to develop evidence-based models for identifying and managing error pathways (Reason, 1990; Hollnagel, 2017). Some examples of evidence-based models mitigating error pathways include the HFACS, which classifies contributing factors across human, supervisory, and organizational levels; the Swiss Cheese Model, which explains how harm occurs when multiple defenses fail in alignment; and the Systems-theoretic accident model and processes / systems-theoretic process analysis (STAMP/STPA), which examines failures in control and feedback within complex systems. Other examples include the functional resonance analysis method (FRAM), which analyzes how normal work variability can combine into failure, and Bayesian network models, which estimate the probability of error pathways and incident outcomes. In cybersecurity, these models help identify and manage pathways such as cloud misconfiguration, delayed escalation, and access-control failure. Central to this approach is the recognition of systemic causation and the importance of latent conditions that shape the environment in which active errors occur. This perspective underscores error management strategies rather than punitive measures, thereby encouraging organizational learning and resilience (Dekker, 2014 Hollnagel, 2017).

Human error is defined as unintended actions or decisions that deviate from expected performance or procedures, and is commonly categorized into slips, lapses, mistakes, and violations (Dekker, 2014 Reason, 1990). Latent conditions refer to system-level weaknesses, such as poor procedures, inadequate supervision, or flawed interfaces, that create fertile conditions for active errors (Reason, 1990). Error management emphasizes detection, containment, and organizational learning, recognizing that not all errors can be prevented. This aligns with resilience-oriented approaches (Dekker, 2014 Hollnagel, 2017). Systemic causation captures the emergence of errors from the interaction of technology, processes, environments, and human cognition, consistent with systems and resilience perspectives (Hollnagel, 2017 Reason, 1990).

A foundational influence on error science is the Swiss Cheese Model, which conceptualizes accidents or incidents as the alignment of multiple latent and active failures across defensive layers (Reason, 1990). This model shifts the analytic focus from individual culpability to systemic vulnerability, emphasizing that errors are symptoms of deeper system flaws rather than isolated acts. This conceptual shift has informed modern safety and reliability engineering practices across numerous high-risk domains. A cybersecurity example is a ransomware incident through remote access. An attacker gains access by reusing a password on a remote desktop service. The breach is not caused by a single failure. It occurs because multiple defenses fail in alignment, such as weak password practices, no multi-factor authentication, delayed patching, limited network segmentation, and inadequate monitoring. In Swiss Cheese terms, the ransomware attack succeeds because the gaps in several layers line up at the same time.

Applications across domains

Applications of error science span multiple critical industries and contexts. In aviation and aerospace, error science informs accident investigation and pilot training programs (Shappell & Wiegmann, 2017). In healthcare, it supports patient safety initiatives and medication error reduction strategies (Reason, 1990 Hollnagel, 2017). Within the nuclear power sector and other high-reliability operations, it provides tools for preventing cascading system failures (Hollnagel, 2017). Cybersecurity incident analysis increasingly draws upon error science to understand how human errors intersect with technical vulnerabilities (Dekker, 2014 Hollnagel, 2017), while manufacturing and transportation systems apply error science to enhance reliability and operational safety (Reason, 1990).

Within cybersecurity, error science provides a structured analytical lens for understanding how human behaviors, such as misconfigurations, phishing susceptibility, and policy violations, intersect with technical vulnerabilities (Dekker, 2014 Hollnagel, 2017 Shappell & Wiegmann, 2017). Applying error taxonomies allows analysts to identify error-prone scenarios and design proactive mitigations that enhance organizational resilience. By framing cybersecurity incidents as systemic failures rather than individual lapses, error science supports more effective defense strategies, risk management, and the integration of human factors into technical and procedural security architectures.

Methodological tools

A variety of methodological tools help translate error science principles into practical ways of studying, classifying, and reducing failure in complex systems. One of the most widely used is the HFACS, which provides a structured taxonomy for examining how unsafe actions, preconditions, supervisory issues, and organizational influences contribute to incidents (Shappell & Wiegmann, 2017). In practice, HFACS helps investigators move beyond blaming a single individual by showing how errors often emerge from interacting conditions across multiple levels of the system. In cybersecurity, for example, HFACS can help analysts examine not only a mistaken click or misconfiguration, but also the surrounding factors such as alert fatigue, inadequate training, weak supervision, or policy gaps that made the error more likely.

Other tools complement this systems-based approach. Failure mode and effects analysis (FMEA) is used to identify where a process, technology, or workflow might fail and to prioritize those risks before harm occurs (Reason, 1990). This makes it especially useful for prospective risk management, such as evaluating vulnerabilities in authentication workflows, incident response procedures, or cloud access controls. Root cause analysis is more retrospective and is used after an event to trace how an incident occurred and to uncover deeper latent conditions rather than stopping at the most visible error (Reason, 1990). Event and resilience analyses extend this perspective by examining how systems respond under stress, variability, and disruption, with attention not only to failure but also to adaptation and recovery (Hollnagel, 2017). Collectively, these tools support both backward-looking learning from incidents and forward-looking efforts to strengthen system design, decision-making, and organizational resilience. It is important to highlight that while this chapter focuses on HFACS, there are other tools, such as root cause analysis and FMEA, that are just as helpful. However, HFACS provides a means of thoroughly examining latent and active failures across the organization to prevent future incidents and attacks and to strengthen the organization's cybersecurity defense posture.

The strategic goal of error science is not to eliminate error entirely, a fundamentally infeasible objective, but to understand, predict, and manage it effectively (Reason, 1990 Dekker, 2014 Hollnagel, 2017). This involves designing resilient systems that anticipate and absorb human variability, building feedback loops for learning from incidents and near misses, and enhancing human performance through training, automation support, and cognitive engineering. Such a framework creates robust socio-technical systems capable of sustaining performance under dynamic and uncertain conditions.

Enabling a blame-free culture

The concept of blame in cybersecurity constitutes a critical and often contentious domain with substantial implications for an organization's overall security posture. Although assigning accountability remains necessary for governance and compliance, a punitive blame culture is widely regarded as detrimental to effective security management. Emphasizing culpability over causation narrows organizational attention to isolated missteps and obscures the complex socio-technical conditions that enable incidents.

A blame-focused internal culture actively harms security outcomes in several ways. Fear of punishment discourages prompt reporting of mistakes, such as clicking a phishing link, thereby delaying detection and containment, and increasing the severity and cost of breaches. Fixation on the individual who erred diverts inquiry from systemic contributors, including inadequate training, ambiguous or conflicting policies, technical vulnerabilities, and flawed security architectures. When prevention yields to punishment, organizational learning stalls. Diminished psychological safety further suppresses the surfacing of weak signals and constructive suggestions, as personnel seek to avoid reputational harm or perceived incompetence rather than engage in candid disclosure.

A blame-free or Just Culture offers a constructive alternative, drawing on practices from high-reliability sectors such as aviation and healthcare. The central aim following an incident is rigorous learning: understanding why the failure occurred and implementing design, process, and policy changes that reduce the likelihood and impact of recurrence. A Just Culture distinguishes human error, an inevitable byproduct of complex work, from malicious, reckless, or intentionally non-compliant behavior that warrants disciplinary response. Within this framework, employees are not penalized for honest mistakes, while organizational leadership retains accountability for providing usable systems, adequate training, clear procedures, and resources that enable secure performance. A concise cybersecurity example of Just Culture is a cloud administrator who accidentally exposes a storage bucket during a rushed deployment. Instead of only blaming the administrator, the organization examines the full context, including workload, interface complexity, weak peer review, and change-control gaps. The response addresses accountability appropriately while also fixing system issues. The positive outcomes are earlier incident reporting, stronger root-cause analysis, better control redesign, and fewer repeat errors. This strengthens both security and organizational trust.

HFACS enables and supports a blame-free culture by providing a structured, evidence-based taxonomy that distinguishes unsafe acts from their preconditions, unsafe supervision, and organizational influences. By mapping errors to latent conditions and supervisory or organizational drivers, HFACS redirects attention from individual culpability to systemic causation, thereby reducing scapegoating and promoting learning. HFACS strengthens post-incident analysis through consistent coding, increases inter-rater reliability in investigations, and translates findings into actionable organizational interventions, such as procedure redesign, interface improvements, staffing adjustments, and targeted training. Alignment between HFACS and Just Culture principles fosters transparent reporting, disciplined root-cause analysis, and leadership accountability for system design, collectively enhancing resilience and continuous improvement (Reason, 1990 Shappell & Wiegmann, 2017).

Beyond internal governance, blame intersects with strategy in the external domain of the nation-state and transnational cyber conflict. Attribution remains technically and analytically challenging given the limitations of digital forensics and the ease with which adversaries can obfuscate origins or plant deceptive indicators. Public assignment of blame thus functions as a geopolitical act as much as a technical conclusion, employed to deter adversaries, justify responses, or meet broader political objectives even when evidentiary details cannot be fully disclosed. Concurrently, accountability expectations within interconnected supply chains have intensified, with organizations and governments pressing vendors and contractors to meet verifiable cybersecurity standards to maintain baseline resilience across shared ecosystems.

Collectively, these considerations indicate that effective cybersecurity governance requires movement from punitive reflexes toward systemic learning, discriminating accountability, and transparent responsibility across internal operations, strategic communications, and supply chain relationships. Such an approach enhances resilience by encouraging timely reporting, enabling root-cause remediation, and aligning leadership accountability with the socio-technical realities that shape secure performance.

HFACS and risk management

The HFACS provides a comprehensive framework for identifying, categorizing, and analyzing human and organizational contributors to adverse events across high-risk domains. By imposing a disciplined taxonomy that spans organizational influences, unsafe supervision, preconditions for unsafe acts, and the unsafe acts themselves, HFACS aligns incident investigation with system-level learning rather than post hoc attribution. Cross-domain utility appears in aviation, healthcare, mining, construction, maritime operations, warehousing, and related sectors, where consistent causal coding yields comparable datasets and translational insights that inform control redesign and policy refinement (Aji & Susilowati, 2024 Batalden & Sydnes, 2013 Wiegmann & Shappell, 2000 Zhang et al., 2020). This same value is increasingly relevant in cybersecurity, where incidents are often analyzed through fragmented technical logs or case-specific narratives that limit cross-case learning. Applying consistent causal coding in cybersecurity can help organizations systematically compare phishing events, misconfigurations, insider actions, access control failures, and response breakdowns across units and sectors, thereby revealing recurring human, supervisory, and organizational contributors that can inform stronger control design, governance, and resilience policy.

A principal contribution of HFACS lies in systematically identifying contributing factors beyond proximate operator actions. For example, if a CISO approves an accelerated system rollout without sufficient security testing and the organization later experiences a breach, HFACS does not stop at the CISO's decision. It extends the analysis to preconditions such as incomplete risk information and time pressure, supervisory factors such as weak challenge processes or limited cross-team review, and organizational influences such as incentive structures that prioritize speed to deployment over security assurance. This approach produces a more accurate explanation of the incident and supports stronger corrective action at the system level. A layered architecture brings latent organizational conditions and supervisory practices into view, showing how they shape frontline performance and enabling interventions that address deeper sources of risk. Evidence from mining indicates that preconditions for unsafe acts and unsafe supervision frequently dominate accident pathways, directing attention to targeted mitigations at those levels rather than solely to worker behavior (Helvida et al., 2022 Moosavi & Kamyab, 2022). In long-term care and healthcare, adapted HFACS variants highlight system design weaknesses and planning gaps, informing risk management plans that strengthen safety controls and reduce the recurrence of adverse events (McGrane et al., 2023).

HFACS also supports prioritization and quantification when integrated with analytical methods such as Bayesian networks, multi-criteria decision analysis, and simulation. HFACS becomes more useful for prioritization when combined with quantitative methods. Bayesian networks estimate how factors across HFACS levels influence incident risk. Multi-criteria decision analysis (MCDA) helps compare corrective actions using criteria such as risk reduction, cost, and feasibility. Simulation tests how changes in staffing, workload, policies, or controls may affect outcomes before implementation. Together, these methods help organizations move from causal classification to evidence-based decision-making. In cybersecurity, HFACS integrated with decision-analysis tools can identify high-leverage contributors to incidents such as cloud misconfigurations, privileged access misuse, or delayed incident escalation, thereby guiding targeted process redesign, supervision enhancements, and control investments. When HFACS-coded incident data are combined with Bayesian models, organizations can estimate which causal pathways, such as configuration complexity, time pressure, weak change oversight, or inadequate staffing, most strongly predict breach likelihood or severity, which supports focused prevention strategies and more effective allocation of security resources.

HFACS also enhances cybersecurity risk management through cross-context adaptability. In cloud and hybrid environments, systematic HFACS coding improves root-cause clarity by linking exposed storage, identity misconfigurations, or segmentation failures to human, supervisory, and organizational conditions rather than treating them as isolated technical mistakes. In security operations centers and infrastructure teams, HFACS helps analysts trace how alert overload, unclear escalation protocols, and policy drift propagate to frontline response errors, thereby strengthening the evidentiary basis for risk modeling and control redesign. In this way, HFACS supports more proactive and system-level prevention strategies across diverse cybersecurity settings.

Beyond retrospective analysis, HFACS contributes to proactive prevention and learning cultures by structuring near-miss reporting, thematic synthesis, and feedback loops that inform design and supervision. Occupational health and safety studies using HFACS link richer narratives and consistent coding to improvements in safety climate, supervisory oversight, and the evidentiary basis for risk controls across hospitals, healthcare, and mines (Afshari et al., 2019 Hizriansyah & Maretalinia, 2025 Verma & Chaudhari, 2017). These practices align with Just Culture principles, reinforcing psychological safety and encouraging timely disclosure of weak signals that precede harm.

Economic decision-making and risk financing also benefit from HFACS-enabled insight. In cybersecurity, HFACS can be used to link human, supervisory, and organizational contributors to measurable loss patterns, such as cloud misconfiguration exposure, delayed incident escalation, privileged access misuse, and change-management failures. This allows organizations and insurers to develop more precise cyber risk models, align premiums and coverage conditions with demonstrated control maturity, and connect investments in training, staffing, workflow redesign, and governance to measurable reductions in breach likelihood and severity. In this way, HFACS outputs become organizational levers that influence both cybersecurity performance and financial outcomes.

Recent methodological advances further extend HFACS utility in cybersecurity through analytics and visualization of risk pathways. For example, association-rule mining can reveal recurring combinations of contributors, such as alert overload plus unclear escalation thresholds plus understaffed shifts, that precede delayed response events. Network analysis can map how organizational influences, supervisory gaps, and frontline conditions interact across incident types, helping analysts identify both direct and indirect contributors to compromise. Multi-criteria decision models can then prioritize interventions by weighing expected risk reduction, implementation cost, operational feasibility, and time to impact. Together, these approaches strengthen the evidentiary basis for cyber risk management by moving from isolated incident narratives to analyzable patterns of socio-technical failure.

These approaches support holistic intervention portfolios that address systemic couplings rather than isolated nodes of failure.

In conclusion, HFACS advances risk management by furnishing a layered causal architecture, enabling prioritization through quantitative integration, demonstrating adaptability across sectors, fostering learning cultures that act on near-miss intelligence, informing economic optimization, and revealing risk networks that guide systemic interventions. When embedded in governance and resilience programs, HFACS facilitates sustained improvement from the individual task level to deep organizational processes, enhancing the reliability and safety of complex operations (Aji & Susilowati, 2024 Batalden & Sydnes, 2013 Khanal et al., 2024 Souza & Russomano, 2017 Wiegmann & Shappell, 2000 Zhang et al., 2020).

Capitalizing on prevention

The aviation, healthcare, nuclear power, and space exploration domains use HFACS to prevent deadly and costly accidents. HFACS is not meant to be punitive or to identify end-users for punishment; instead, it is designed to prevent major cybersecurity incidents. The cybersecurity sector should take heed and use HFACS to prevent costly and operationally crippling catastrophes. For example, an organization can analyze its data and reports to identify troubled areas, areas lacking sufficient security controls, or high-risk operations, and engineer solutions to prevent active and latent failures from aligning. This is depicted in the Swiss-cheese model in the next section.

A preventive approach allows organizations to develop nanocodes under the modified HFACS based on native operations, cybersecurity strategy, and risk appetite. It is important to note that organizations should modify the HFACS taxonomy to meet their specific needs. Trying to apply the taxonomy as is might not yield the desired results. Tailoring HFACS is not easy and will require considerable effort; however, once it is modified and applied, the organization will have a mechanism in place to thoroughly investigate and prevent incidents from occurring. This process is continuous and requires constant application as processes, people, technologies, and organizational and system factors are continually evolving.

One salient objective is to create nanocodes and align them with the different levels and categories of the HFACS model. Each nanocode should reflect the organization's environment. Failure to assess the nanocodes and tailor them specifically could prevent organizations from preemptively breaking the chain of events that result in catastrophes, which we will now discuss.

Structure of HFACS: four cascading levels

The HFACS was originally developed by behavioral scientists within the United States Navy to address growing concerns about human performance shortfalls in aviation operations (Shappell & Wiegmann, 2003). To systematically evaluate how performance decrements contribute to accidents, its creators drew upon scientifically grounded investigation theory, most notably Reason's Swiss-cheese model in Figure 9.1. This model promotes a systems perspective, viewing human error not as the root cause but as a symptom of deeper organizational conditions. Within this framework, organizations implement multiple defensive barriers across technology, procedures, supervision, and governance. These barriers are structured in a sequential manner, with higher-level influences shaping lower-level behaviors. Failures at any level create vulnerabilities, either active, occurring close to the event, or latent, stemming from distant conditions that can align across layers to produce adverse outcomes.

The following cybersecurity scenario enhances understanding of the Swiss-cheese model by illustrating a phishing-based ransomware attack in a healthcare organization. A staff member receives an email that appears to be a legitimate invoice from a known vendor. The employee opens the attachment, malware executes, credentials are captured, and the attacker later moves through the network and deploys ransomware. The breach is severe, but it is not caused by one action alone. It occurs because multiple defensive layers each had a weakness, and those weaknesses aligned. The HFACS framework helps analysts examine an incident across multiple levels of the system rather than attributing failure to a single mistake. In the phishing example below, each layer shows how frontline actions, immediate conditions, supervisory practices, and organizational decisions combined to create a path to compromise. The layers below are the HFACS model's actual layers.

Unsafe Acts (frontline actions): This is the action closest to the incident. In this case, the employee clicks the phishing email attachment and enables content in the file. That action is visible and immediate but is only one part of the failure path.
Preconditions for Unsafe Acts: These are the conditions that made the unsafe act more likely. The employee may have been fatigued, under time pressure, and processing a high volume of messages. The phishing email may also have looked highly credible, making it difficult to detect. These human and task conditions created the opportunity for error.
Unsafe Supervision: Supervisory and management practices that failed to protect frontline staff. For example, the team may not have received recent phishing simulations or updated security awareness training. Supervisors may also have normalized excessive workload, reducing employees' ability to carefully review suspicious emails.
Organizational Influences: Leadership may have underinvested in email filtering, endpoint protection, staffing, and cyber training. The organization may also have lacked strong policies for patching, segmentation, and incident response readiness. These strategic decisions created latent weaknesses that remained hidden until the attack occurred.

In Swiss Cheese terms, the ransomware incident happened because the "holes" in each layer aligned. The model shows why cybersecurity events should be analyzed as system failures across multiple barriers, rather than as a single user mistake.

HFACS operationalizes this theory by defining four analytically adjacent levels, each with standardized definitions, exemplars, and coding rules that support inter-rater reliability and cross-case trend analysis. This structured approach shifts the focus from individual blame to identifying modifiable barrier degradations, thereby enhancing organizational learning and prevention (Shappell & Wiegmann, 2003 Wiegmann & Shappell, 2022).

HFACS offers organizations a powerful framework for understanding and mitigating the systemic breakdowns that contribute to accidents and safety incidents. Rather than focusing solely on individual errors, HFACS enables a comprehensive analysis of the entire system, identifying latent conditions, organizational influences, and recurring patterns in human performance that lead to unsafe outcomes. By examining historical data, organizations can proactively uncover trends and vulnerabilities, allowing for targeted, data-driven interventions that enhance safety and reduce incident rates. Figure 9.2 breaks down the key components of this framework:

Figure 9.2. The Human Factors Analysis and Classification System (Source: https://hfacs-healthcare.com/the-hfacs-framework/)

HFACS breaks down complex events into structured categories, helping analysts trace unsafe acts back to their psychological origins or organizational deficiencies. This structured approach is equally valuable in cybersecurity because it improves the quality of incident investigations and supports the development of more robust, searchable security event databases. When organizations classify incidents consistently across unsafe acts, preconditions, supervisory factors, and organizational influences, they can compare cases over time and identify recurring risk patterns that would otherwise remain hidden in isolated technical reports. This makes it easier to prioritize corrective actions based on evidence rather than anecdote.

For example, a large healthcare system could apply HFACS to phishing incidents, cloud misconfigurations, and delayed-escalation events across its hospitals. After coding incidents over a year, the organization might find that a high proportion of serious events involve routine violations, such as repeated bypassing of change-control steps, the use of shared privileged accounts, or informal workarounds during high-workload periods. By identifying this trend, leadership could target interventions such as workflow redesign, stronger supervisory review, safer system defaults, and clearer accountability practices. Over time, this HFACS-informed approach would help reduce repeat incidents, strengthen organizational learning, and improve overall cyber resilience.

Ultimately, HFACS empowers organizations to shift from reactive responses to proactive safety management, fostering a culture of continuous improvement and resilience in high-risk environments. Organizations can leverage HFACS to prevent cybersecurity incidents, attacks, and breaches by analyzing its data and developing mitigative strategies to break the chain of events that lead to catastrophic cyber events.

Let's now look at this system in detail.

Level 1: Unsafe acts

Level 1, Unsafe Acts, encompasses frontline behaviors observed at the operational point of performance.

Decision errors involve rule-based or knowledge-based misjudgments during configuration approvals, access exceptions, or incident triage. Typical examples include approval of an insecure security group based on an incorrect rule interpretation and authorization of an emergency entitlement without adequate risk appraisal.
Skill-based errors involve slips and lapses during routine tasks such as firewall edits, identity entitlement changes, or command-line operations conducted under distraction.
Perceptual errors arise from misperceiving alert salience or misunderstanding console affordances under high cognitive load, often during alert surges or live incident response.
Violations represent departures from established procedures, either routine or exceptional, such as bypassing multi-factor authentication during an outage or implementing a change outside approved windows. Classification at this level requires concrete artifacts, for example, timestamped change records, terminal histories, and console captures, to distinguish errors from violations and to separate rule-based decisions from skill-based slips.

It is important to highlight the difference between routine and exceptional violations through a cybersecurity lens. In cybersecurity, routine violations are repeated workarounds that become normalized, such as administrators regularly sharing privileged accounts or skipping formal change approvals to move faster. Exceptional violations are rare, crisis-driven departures from policy, such as temporarily bypassing multi-factor authentication during a major outage to restore critical access. The distinction matters because routine violations signal control drift and cultural weakness, while exceptional violations reflect high-pressure decision-making during abnormal events.

Level 2: Preconditions for unsafe acts

Level 2, Preconditions for Unsafe Acts, captures local conditions that shape performance at the point of work

Individual factors: Adverse mental states include fatigue, cognitive tunneling, and stress during surge windows created by patch cycles, seasonal onboarding, or incident backlogs. Adverse physiological states include extended shifts, circadian disruption, and illness, all of which degrade attention and memory.
Situational factors: The technological and physical environment includes console complexity, confusing defaults, fragmented telemetry, poor display ergonomics, and workspace constraints that induce error through excessive context switching or low visual salience. Personnel factors include mismatches between skill and tool demands, tenure and training gaps for newly assigned staff, and workload misallocation that concentrates high-risk tasks on a small set of engineers.
Team factors: Team dynamics and communication factors include handoff ambiguity, breakdowns in shared mental models, and coordination lags across security, operations, and development groups. Evidence for preconditions arises from schedules, on-call logs, training matrices, tool inventories, handoff transcripts, and usability findings, thereby enabling targeted interventions such as staffing buffers, revised runbooks, and user-interface redesign.
Level 3: Supervisory factors

Level 3, Supervisory Factors, addresses managerial practices that configure frontline conditions.

Inadequate supervision appears in insufficient code-review standards, limited runbook rehearsal for high-risk tasks, and weak oversight of privileged access
Planned inappropriate operations include release cadences that preclude risk gates, maintenance windows that overlap with peak business activity, and staffing ratios that do not support safe execution
Failure to correct known problems includes the accumulation of security exceptions, deferred certificate rotation, and unresolved audit findings that remain open across multiple quarters
Supervisory violations involve explicit or implicit pressure to circumvent change control to meet deadlines or service-level targets

Documentary evidence includes standard operating procedures, change-advisory minutes, exception registers, audit trackers, and staffing plans, allowing linkage from supervisory patterns to corrective actions such as revised approval workflows, rehearsal requirements, and service-level incentives aligned with safety

Level 4: Organizational influences

Level 4, Organizational culture, covers portfolio-level determinants that shape supervisory practice and local work

Resource management concerns under-investment in training, usability, and operability; inadequate budgets for security automation and human-centered design; and insufficient time allocation for reliability work
Organizational climate reflects just-culture norms, clarity of risk appetite, and the presence of symbolic compliance that substitutes paperwork for meaningful control effectiveness
Organizational process includes siloed ownership across security, IT, and compliance; fragmented vendor governance that obscures third-party risk; and immature learning loops that fail to translate incident insights into durable design changes.

Evidence includes budget records, organizational policies, vendor contracts, governance charters, and metrics from corrective-action programs. Interventions at this level involve strategic investment decisions, explicit risk appetite statements, integrated ownership models, and governance architectures that connect incident learning to enterprise planning and vendor management.

Purpose of nanocodes and their use in HFACS

Nanocodes in the HFACS are highly granular sub-categories nested under the standard HFACS taxonomy (e.g., Unsafe Acts, Preconditions for Unsafe Acts, Supervisory Factors, Organizational Influences). Their purpose is to define each category with precise, observable descriptors so that investigators can code incident data consistently at a fine level of detail.

The organization and supervision nanocodes sections help analysts move beyond frontline errors by identifying the management and institutional conditions that shape cybersecurity performance. The nanocodes below are examples of how they are applied to sections. Organization nanocodes capture higher-level influences such as resource allocation, operational tempo, training, policy, and culture, while supervision nanocodes focus on leadership oversight, planning, accountability, and supervisory compliance. In cybersecurity, these categories are useful for explaining why incidents recur, for example, when understaffing, weak governance, unclear priorities, or poor supervisory enforcement create conditions that increase misconfigurations, delayed escalations, and policy violations.

Figure 9.3: Examples of NASA nanocodes at the organizational and supervision levels (Source: NASA, 2023)

A high-resolution version of this image is available in the Graphic Bundle. You can access the graphics here: https://packt.link/gbp/9781806118335.

The precondition nanocodes section helps analysts identify the immediate human, environmental, and task conditions that make cybersecurity errors more likely. These nanocodes capture factors such as cognitive overload, fatigue, complacency, communication breakdowns, interface complexity, and information quality, which often shape performance before an incident occurs. In cybersecurity, precondition nanocodes are useful for explaining why actions such as missed alerts, misconfigurations, or delayed responses happen because they reveal the operational conditions that influence decision-making and execution.

Figure 9.4: Examples of NASA nanocodes at the precondition level (Source: NASA, 2023)

A high-resolution version of this image is available in the Graphic Bundle. You can access the graphics here: https://packt.link/gbp/9781806118335.

The ACT nanocodes section helps analysts classify the specific actions taken at the point of the cybersecurity incident, such as incorrect action, omitted action, misperception, or violation of procedure. These nanocodes provide a consistent way to document what happened at the frontline level without stopping the analysis there. In cybersecurity, ACT nanocodes are useful for coding events such as clicking a malicious link, applying the wrong configuration, failing to escalate an alert, or bypassing a required control, which then supports deeper analysis across preconditions, supervision, and organizational influences.

 

Figure 9.5: Examples of NASA nanocodes at the unsafe acts level (Source: NASA, 2023)

A high-resolution version of this image is available in the Graphic Bundle. You can access the graphics here: https://packt.link/gbp/9781806118335.

Nanocodes serve as a critical mechanism for operationalizing HFACS by translating broad categorical frameworks into precise, behaviorally anchored labels. Functionally, nanocodes refine higher-level HFACS categories into specific, observable actions and errors; for example, within the "Skill-Based Errors" category, nanocodes might indicate "omitted checklist step," "mis-sequenced action," or "inadequate instrument cross-check." Structurally, this system is organized hierarchically, beginning with the HFACS level, followed by category, sometimes subcategory, and ultimately the nanocode, which provides the most granular point of analysis.

In application, these nanocodes are used during event investigations to tag causal and contributory factors with a high degree of specificity, enhancing inter-rater reliability, enabling richer trend analyses, and guiding targeted interventions. This methodological precision yields multiple benefits, including improved data quality for understanding the epidemiology of human error, strengthened benchmarking across organizational units or temporal spans, and direct support for interventions such as training, procedural redesign, supervisory adjustments, and policy refinements.

The following are a few examples:

Unsafe Acts → Errors → Skill-Based: "attention fixation," "checklist step omitted," "habit intrusion"
Preconditions → Environmental Factors → Technological: "interface affordance mismatch," "alert salience insufficient"
Supervision → Inadequate Supervision: "training currency not maintained," "tasking beyond qualification"
Organization → Resource Management: "staffing below minimum," "competing production pressure"

In short, nanocodes are the fine-grained vocabulary that makes HFACS analytically actionable, turning broad human-factors concepts into specific, codable phenomena that support robust learning and prevention. Without a doubt, the cybersecurity domain would need to modify the existing HFACS model to fit and apply to cybersecurity operations. Figure 9.6 presents an example of agency nanocodes organized as a frequency chart. The colors distinguish broad HFACS-related categories, and the numbers at the ends of the bars show how often each nanocode appeared in the coded cases. In other words, the number is the frequency count for that specific nanocode. A higher number means that issue appeared more often across the incidents or events being analyzed, which suggests it may be a recurring weakness or pattern within the organization.

The color scheme appears to group the nanocodes by higher-level analytic domains. For example, the darker purple codes near the top reflect organizational-level influences, such as operational information, equipment, funding, training, program oversight, and operations tempo. The bright pink and magenta sections represent supervisory and precondition-related factors, including rule enforcement, supervisory pressure, proficiency, training issues, personnel management, fatigue, distraction, complacency, and communication or planning problems. The lighter pink and related tones continue this focus on human, environmental, and performance-shaping conditions, such as memory limits, motivation, workspace incompatibility, visibility restrictions, and environmental effects. The blue codes at the bottom represent unsafe acts or performance-level errors, including misperception, breakdowns in visual scan, overcontrol or undercontrol, inadvertent operation, inadequate real-time assessment, incorrect action execution, and actions taken too soon. Thus, the colors help readers quickly distinguish whether a code belongs primarily to organizational influences, supervisory issues, preconditions, or unsafe acts.

The alphanumeric structure of each nanocode also serves an important purpose. The letters indicate the general category or domain, while the numbers identify the specific coded issue within that category. For instance, codes such as OR, OP, OC, and SV point to broader organizational or supervisory domains, while PP, PC, PT, and PE appear to identify preconditions or performance-shaping factors, and AD, AS, AP, and AV refer to action-level errors or unsafe acts. This structure allows analysts to code incidents consistently and also makes it easier to aggregate findings across many cases. For example, if AD005 Inadequate Real-Time Assessment appears 39 times, that immediately signals a recurring action-level problem that may require targeted intervention.

The frequencies shown in the figure help the organization identify which issues are most common. For example, AD005 Inadequate Real-Time Assessment has a frequency of 39, PT010 Fuels or Materials has 33, PC004 Miscommunication has 32, and OP006 Organizational Training has 30. These higher-frequency nanocodes suggest areas where the organization may need to focus attention because they recur more often than others. By contrast, nanocodes with frequencies of 1 or 2 may still matter, but they appear less often and may represent more isolated or contextual issues.

Most importantly, organizations should develop nanocodes that fit their own environment rather than simply adopting another agency's list without modification. Nanocodes are most useful when they reflect the actual language, workflows, hazards, technologies, and recurring failure patterns of the organization using them. For example, a cybersecurity organization would likely need nanocodes that differ from those used by NASA or another operational agency. Instead of codes such as fuels or materials, the organization might create nanocodes for alert fatigue, phishing susceptibility, MFA failure handling, cloud misconfiguration, privilege escalation gaps, incomplete log visibility, weak incident escalation, policy drift, vendor dependency, or patching delay. These codes should still align to the broader HFACS structure, but the labels and definitions should be tailored to the organization's own mission and risk profile.

A practical way for organizations to develop their own nanocodes is to begin with the major HFACS levels and then create narrower subcodes based on actual incident reports, after-action reviews, audit findings, and expert input from personnel across technical, supervisory, and leadership roles. After initial development, the codes should be tested on real cases to see whether analysts can apply them consistently. Over time, the organization can refine the codebook by merging vague codes, clarifying definitions, adding missing categories, and retiring codes that do not provide analytic value. In this way, nanocodes become a living organizational taxonomy that supports pattern recognition, root-cause analysis, and more targeted corrective action.

 

Figure 9.6: Example of NASA nanocodes(Source: NASA, 2023)

Need for a modified HFACS-Cyber framework

Organizations need a structured human-factors methodology, such as a modified HFACS-Cyber framework, to systematically examine how unsafe acts, preconditions, supervisory factors, and organizational influences combine to produce cyber incidents. This is necessary because many cybersecurity investigations still focus too narrowly on technical artifacts or frontline mistakes, which can obscure the human, supervisory, and organizational conditions that repeatedly produce risk. A modified HFACS-Cyber program provides a consistent method for coding incidents using cyber-relevant categories such as alert overload, configuration complexity, policy drift, access-control workarounds, and governance gaps. In cybersecurity, this approach is essential because it improves analytic consistency, strengthens cross-incident comparison, supports more reliable risk databases, and helps organizations prioritize corrective actions that address systemic causes rather than isolated symptoms. This section explores the applications of a modified HFACS-Cyber framework to provide organizations with a scientifically based approach to identify cyber incidents and attacks, foundational causes, and methods for preventing catastrophic incidents and attacks in the future.

Continuous application to near misses and assessments

Beyond sharpening post-incident learning, a continuously applied modified HFACS supports a proactive risk posture for near-misses (for example, change-related exceptions and pipeline rollbacks) and routine assessments (for example, red-team findings and phishing exercises). Although HFACS originated as an accident-investigation framework, evidence from cloud-centric adaptations demonstrates that iterative coding of day-to-day signals surfaces high-friction pathways before those pathways culminate in a breach. Repeated, like-for-like coding turns scattered lessons into leading indicators that guide system and policy redesign, converting episodic insights into a durable prevention cadence.

Across multiple high-risk domains, including aviation, rail, maritime, healthcare, mining, oil and gas, and unmanned aerial systems, researchers and practitioners have modified HFACS to fit the specific operational, technical, and organizational realities of their environments. Cybersecurity is now following the same path by adapting HFACS to analyze issues such as cloud misconfigurations, human error, supervisory gaps, and policy-driven risk in digital systems.

Alignment with behavioral cybersecurity strategy

Continuous HFACS coding aligns with a behavioral cybersecurity strategy that couples measurement with a closed-loop improvement process. Behavioral science cautions that awareness campaigns alone rarely change behavior at scale. A superior sequence prioritizes adaptation of systems and processes first (sensible defaults, friction-reducing nudges, automation, and guardrails), followed by role-specific training and targeted behavior-change campaigns, all wrapped in feedback mechanisms that measure effectiveness and unintended effects such as "shadow security." Modified HFACS taxonomies map directly onto this sequence: codes identify where to simplify workflows, where to embed just-in-time cues, and where knowledge or motivation deficits persist. Interventions are thus ordered from design to education to communication, rather than defaulting reflexively to training.

Explicit treatment of human performance degraders

A further strength of HFACS for cyber leadership is explicit treatment of human performance degraders, such as fatigue, overload, and burnout, as preconditions that propagate to unsafe acts. Security fatigue and chronic workload correlate with policy workarounds and configuration errors. Coding those factors in HFACS ensures representation on the risk register with accountable ownership and mitigation plans, rather than dismissal as individual failings. Behavioral guidance also warns that stacking initiatives without guardrails can induce additional fatigue; therefore, strain metrics and recovery practices must be integrated into the improvement loop to avoid risk migration.

Governance integration and investment discipline

HFACS provides a common language that integrates with governance artifacts such as risk registers, control libraries, and audit evidence. Code heatmaps distinguish problems that demand process re-engineering (for example, OP1 Process Uncodified, OP2 Process Impractical) from problems that require supervisory redesign or usability enhancements to tooling. This decomposition supports rational capital allocation, defensible prioritization, and evidence of continuous enhancement during audits. Establishing a formal human-factors program to run, interpret, and act on HFACS analyses closes a persistent blind spot in many enterprises, where technology spending overwhelms attention to human performance engineering and ad-hoc practices persist.

Minimal viable implementation playbook

A modified HFACS-Cyber framework can be most effective when it is implemented as a disciplined operational practice rather than a one-time analytic exercise. A minimal viable implementation playbook provides organizations with a practical starting point for building a consistent evidence base, translating findings into targeted interventions, and embedding human-factors insights into governance and continuous risk management. This approach helps ensure that incident learning is systematic, measurable, and directly tied to prevention. Below are some straightforward points for establishing a minimal viable playbook:

Define a minimal set of domain-specific nanocodes tailored to the environment (for example, CI/CD approvals, infrastructure-as-code guardrails, identity policy variants, and exception management)
Apply the codes consistently across incidents, near-misses, and routine assessments to build a comparable evidence base.
Quarterly, translate the top organizational and supervisory findings into an engineered behavioral strategy: redesign affordances and defaults, add nudges and just-in-time prompts, deploy role-specific training, run a concise communications campaign, and instrument feedback for both effectiveness and unintended effects.
Track strain and fatigue alongside traditional performance indicators to prevent risk reintroduction through initiative overload.
Feed results into governance artifacts to sustain accountability and demonstrate risk reduction over time.
Outcomes that matter for leadership

Without a doubt, HFACS can assist with transforming a cybersecurity program from a reactive, person-centric view of error to a system-centric model in which leaders remove structural drivers of noncompliance, monitor fatigue explicitly, and demonstrate measurable reductions in misconfigurations and other human-enabled events. Cumulative experience from cloud-focused HFACS applications and behavioral cybersecurity practice indicates three durable benefits:

Reduced subjectivity in causal analysis
Elevation of prevention over post hoc remediation
Enhanced durability of security enhancements through closed-loop learning

In short, a modified HFACS provides leaders with metrics that matter, a prioritized backlog of systemic fixes, and a repeatable cadence for demonstrating risk reduction over time.

Weaknesses in HFACS

HFACS is a widely utilized framework for retrospective analysis of human errors following incidents. However, recent scholarly discourse points out inherent weaknesses that limit its evidential robustness and practical applicability. One primary limitation is the lack of built-in mechanisms for quantitative risk assessment and prioritization within HFACS. This deficiency has prompted researchers to integrate HFACS with methodologies such as FMEA to introduce scoring and ranking capabilities to support decision-making. Without this amalgamation, HFACS predominantly serves a descriptive function and fails to gauge the relative severity, probability, or detectability of different failure modes.

Moreover, various studies have noted a tendency within HFACS to disproportionately focus on immediate actions occurring at or near the incident site, while neglecting broader organizational and external influences. This bias has been particularly highlighted in research within the rail and offshore transport domains, prompting calls for explicit expansions of HFACS to encompass regulatory, political, and societal factors as external layers of causality.

The credibility of HFACS is further challenged by concerns regarding the reliability of inter-rater agreement, particularly when analysts encode information from incomplete narratives or summaries. This issue poses a threat to the reproducibility of findings across contexts unless rigorous coder training, decision protocols, and adjudication mechanisms are implemented.

HFACS, as a taxonomy-based system, provides limited support for modeling the dynamic, multilevel transmission of latent organizational deficiencies to frontline behaviors. To bridge this representational gap, scholars advocate combining HFACS with structural or cognitive modeling techniques, such as structural equation modeling, path analysis, and network methodologies, to make intermediary pathways and interaction effects explicit. Additionally, the direct transfer of HFACS across sectors has revealed discrepancies in constructs and terminology, necessitating sector-specific adaptations to maintain construct validity and diagnostic accuracy.

Another critical constraint of HFACS is its retrospective nature, which hampers proactive risk management unless its outcomes are explicitly linked to leading indicators and prospective monitoring tools. Evidence from high-reliability sectors suggests integrating HFACS findings with safety climate assessments, workload data, staffing metrics, and control health indicators to enhance the predictive and preventive value of the analysis. In practical applications, there is a tendency to overweight human and immediate supervisory error categories while underweighting upstream managerial and organizational contributions, thereby diverting attention from systemic redesign efforts.

The inferential fidelity of HFACS is heavily dependent on data quality and analytical expertise. Inadequate, inconsistent, or heterogeneous incident records can diminish the granularity and accuracy of categorization assignments, potentially biasing aggregated learning or trend analyses. Even with comprehensive documentation, expert judgment plays a central role in category selection and boundary delineation within HFACS, introducing variability across investigators in the absence of calibration and adjudication procedures. Moreover, HFACS has limited capacity to represent technical design flaws or environmental conditions, underscoring the need for complementary utilization of system-theoretic or engineering-focused models to maintain a comprehensive system-level perspective.

Challenges of implementing HFACS in cybersecurity

Integrating the HFACS into cybersecurity requires targeted adaptation to domain-specific phenomena. Initially developed for aviation and subsequently applied in other safety-critical domains dominated by physical processes, HFACS must be reconstructed to account for intangible cyber threat vectors and socio-cognitive dynamics, including insider threats, social engineering, and policy nonadherence. Cyber contexts demand categories that capture behaviors such as employees' unfamiliarity with organizational policies, ambiguous procedural guidance, and interface-induced slips and lapses, which broaden the scope of "unsafe acts" beyond physical operator errors (Zeng et al., 2022). Accordingly, HFACS layers and subcategories require cyber-specific definitions and exemplars that connect human actions to organizational influences and technical failure modes in digital ecosystems.

The intrinsic complexity of cybersecurity as a sociotechnical enterprise presents further challenges. Cyber failures frequently emerge from nonlinear interactions among human operators, automation pipelines, software-defined infrastructures, and institutional processes rather than from isolated human missteps. This interactional complexity complicates the reliable assignment of causal codes and demands HFACS extensions capable of representing dependencies across organizational policies, security operations workflows, and adaptive adversary behaviors (Jaboski & Jaboski, 2021 Zeng et al., 2022). Without such extensions, analyses risk overemphasizing proximal human actions while underrepresenting latent systemic contributors distributed across technology stacks and governance structures.

A robust HFACS application depends on high-quality incident data that delineate human contributions and contextual conditions with sufficient granularity. In cybersecurity, incident reporting that captures human factors is often sparse, inconsistent, or intentionally limited due to confidentiality, privacy constraints, and legal exposure. The result is an incomplete evidentiary basis for fine-grained coding across HFACS layers. Moreover, adversary-induced behaviors, such as social engineering, hinge on subtle cognitive and socio-behavioral cues that are rarely captured in operational logs or post-incident summaries, further degrading data quality for human-centric analysis (Alzubaidi et al., 2023 Salahdine & Kaabouch, 2019). Addressing these deficits requires standardized, privacy-aware human-factors fields in incident reporting, along with methods to augment sparse data using validated analytic and learning techniques.

Alignment between HFACS and automated cybersecurity tooling introduces additional integration hurdles. Contemporary defenses rely extensively on intrusion detection systems, threat intelligence pipelines, and machine learning classifiers that may serve as opaque decision elements, limiting traceability from machine outputs to human-interpretable causes. To ensure that HFACS insights inform alert triage, playbook design, and control selection, organizations must advance explainable, interpretable analytics that enable human analysts to connect algorithmic detections to HFACS-relevant precursors and organizational drivers. Achieving this alignment requires governance for model transparency, human-AI teaming practices, and feedback loops that translate HFACS findings into feature engineering, rule refinement, and interface adjustments (Salahdine & Kaabouch, 2019 Zhang et al., 2021).

Organizational culture and policy enforcement materially shape the distribution of unsafe acts and preconditions in cyber operations. Unsafe behaviors often reflect insufficient policy clarity, irregular training cadence, or cultural norms that discourage timely reporting and peer escalation. Embedding HFACS thus necessitates assessments that make organizational influences explicit, including leadership priorities, workload and staffing, and the usability of security tooling. Such visibility can face resistance rooted in privacy concerns, measurement fatigue, and perceived surveillance, which must be addressed through Just Culture principles, transparent metrics governance, and participatory design of monitoring practices (Jaboski & Jaboski, 2021 Zeng et al., 2022).

The pace and adaptability of cyber threats make taxonomy maintenance a moving target. Static HFACS codebooks can lag novel attack patterns, emergent socio-technical workflows, and evolving user-adversary interactions. Sustained relevance requires periodic codebook updates, versioned nanocodes, and mechanisms for rapidly incorporating new human-error modes associated with sophisticated phishing, identity abuse, and software supply-chain compromises. Continuous learning pipelines that link incident response retrospectives to taxonomy curation are therefore essential (Nguyen & Reddi, 2021 Shaukat et al., 2019).

Finally, effective use of HFACS in cybersecurity depends on integration with broader risk and resilience frameworks. While HFACS excels at structured causal analysis of human and organizational contributors, resilience goals demand coupling those insights with enterprise risk registers, business impact analyses, and recovery planning. Embedding HFACS outputs into scenario-based exercises, control rationales, and metrics for adaptive capacity can align micro-level human-error findings with macro-level continuity and safety objectives in complex industrial and critical-infrastructure settings (Rahim et al., 2024). Such coupling supports prioritization of systemic interventions over isolated corrective actions and advances organization-wide learning.

In summary, the integration of HFACS into cybersecurity faces challenges related to domain-specific adaptation, representation of sociotechnical complexity, incident-data scarcity and variability, coordination with opaque automated defenses, cultural and policy frictions, rapid threat evolution, and alignment with enterprise resilience and risk management. Addressing these challenges requires cyber-tailored taxonomies, improved data practices, explainable analytics, culture-aware implementation, agile codebook governance, and explicit linkage to resilience frameworks to realize HFACS's full value in cyber contexts.

Summary

HFACS-Cyber offers a holistic approach to cybersecurity by helping organizations examine how human behavior, technology design, supervisory practices, and organizational conditions interact to produce risk. Rather than isolating incidents as technical failures or individual mistakes, the framework supports a system-level understanding of cyber events across unsafe acts, preconditions, supervision, and organizational influences. This enables cybersecurity programs to benefit more comprehensively from stronger investigations, clearer cross-incident comparisons, more reliable risk data, and better-targeted interventions in training, workflow design, governance, and control engineering. In this way, HFACS-Cyber strengthens cybersecurity not only at the incident-response level, but across the broader enterprise risk, resilience, and prevention ecosystem.

With this human-factors foundation established, the next chapter examines identification and technology strategies that organizations can use to detect, manage, and reduce human-centered cybersecurity risks in operational environments.
